US Officially a Measles Endemic Country
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-18
03:30:00 UTC
12:30:00 KST

US Officially a Measles Endemic Country 2
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from The Hill: The Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) confirmed Tuesday that it was withdrawing the U.S.'s measles elimination status, ending more than two decades of what is considered to be one of the greatest public health achievements in modern history. The U.S. met with PAHO's Measles and Rubella Elimination Regional Monitoring and Re-Verification Commission for a three-day, closed-door meeting last week. "The commission concluded that the endemic measles transmission has now been reestablished in Bolivia, Mexico and the United States, and recommended that their measles elimination status be withdrawn, a recommendation which I have accepted," Jarbas Barbosa, PAHO's director, said during the briefing Tuesday. The U.S. achieved measles elimination status in 2000 and has held onto that for more than 25 years. But the past two years have seen close to 8,000 measles infections and roughly a dozen related deaths. This year has been the worst in terms of transmission in more than 30 years.
Erica Schwartz, director of the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) has previously said losing elimination status won't lead to changes in the federal response.
"We know where transmission is occurring and we've invested $100 million in state and local health departments to reach those communities," Schwartz said on Tuesday. "Losing a designation doesn't mean we abandon an evidence-based approach. It means we remain focused on stopping outbreaks."

Nvidia’s startup stakes ballooned to $65B, but Huang’s exit puts future  deals in doubt
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-18
01:44:21 UTC
10:44:21 KST

Nvidia’s equity in private companies continues to grow: it revealed its stakes are now worth $65.5 billion on Tuesday, up from the $22 billion reported in January. The increase includes both new investments and significant markups of existing portfolio companies.
The company invested $16 billion into equities in the past quarter alone, it said, and $58 billion in the past nine months of its fiscal year. This amount includes buying shares in public and private companies, yet it shows how the chipmaker made massive bets over the course of the year, especially compared to last year; in November 2025, the company reported spending $5 billion buying equity in the first nine months of that fiscal year.
But it’s unclear if this growth will continue under the interim CEO and Jensen Huang’s successor.
Nvidia stated in late 2023 that the goal of its corporate investing is to expand the AI ecosystem by backing startups it considers to be “game changers and market makers.” Over the nearly three years that followed, the chipmaker has invested in nearly 350 startups, according to PitchBook. The companies Nvidia backed include Anthropic, OpenAI; neolabs like Thinking Machines and Reflection; neoclouds, like Lambda, Nscale, and Crusoe; as well as other companies developing AI infrastructure, robotics startups, and applications.
It’s also become a big financier of the data center build out, reporting $118 billion in guarantees with $105 billion of that the backstop it supplied for OpenAI’s $500 billion data center in Ohio.
The company made those commitments by allocating a meaningful portion of its excess cash flows. In Tuesday’s earning call, Kress said Nvidia would continue providing financing assistance to strong customers and that “as the market matures and additional sources of capital become available,” the company would be “rigorously evaluating future investments, commitments, and guarantees.”
But the question for the VC ecosystem is whether the company will continue to back startups at anywhere near the same level without Huang at the helm.
Huang’s rampant dealmaking has reportedly been a source of concern for Kress. Investors have told TechCrunch that they anticipate that she will take a more conservative approach to startup investing, becoming more selective with new investments and writing smaller-sized checks into its existing portfolio companies.
“We intend to return all excess free cash flow net of strategic uses to shareholders during this last quarter and going forward,” Kress said on the earnings call on Tuesday. Just how strategic startups will be under her leadership, and under the future permanent CEO, remains to be seen. But for now, VCs are bracing for a meaningful pullback in capital.

Nvidia Rejects Jensen Huang's Offer to Return as CEO
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-18
01:30:00 UTC
10:30:00 KST

Nvidia Rejects Jensen Huang's Offer to Return as CEO (apnews.com) 30
			
		 	
				Nvidia's board has unanimously rejected former CEO Jensen Huang's offer to return, despite public support from President Trump and prominent Silicon Valley figures. The decision came as Nvidia reported blockbuster third-quarter revenue of $116 billion, double the year-earlier figure. The Associated Press reports:  The embattled company's board of directors "unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO," Nvidia Chairman Harvey Jones said at the start of Nvidia's quarterly earnings call before yielding to Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress, who is running the company as a permanent replacement is sought. [...] Earlier on Tuesday, Huang said in a social media post that if the board decided that his return was in the best interest of shareholders, employees, the company and the country, he would be "honored to answer these calls." He acknowledged making "serious mistakes" in his personal life.
The turmoil notwithstanding, Nvidia blew past Wall Street expectations for the third quarter, pulling in $116 billion in revenue, double the level from a year ago. The company also posted net income of $2.70 per share, beating estimates of $2.48 per share. Nvidia stock shot up 5.2% in after-hours trading. Referring to Huang's replacement, Jones said, "We want to install the permanent CEO as quickly as possible, but we won't expedite the CEO before finding exactly the right person."   Nvidia also announced a new board committee overseeing national security and trade compliance amid investigations into alleged AI chip smuggling to China.
Further reading: Nvidia Stopped Disclosing Singapore Sales As Revenues Hit $104 Billion

Cerebras Disaggregated Inference
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-18
00:56:23 UTC
09:56:23 KST

We’ve deployed Cerebras Wafer Scale Engines with GPUs to deliver lightning-fast tokens at scale
The world is hungry for ultrafast tokens. In 2026, Cerebras saw an insatiable demand for fast inference, both on our inference cloud and via our partners. To meet demand, we began developing disaggregated inference: GPUs handle prompt processing (prefill) and Cerebras systems handle output generation (decode).
This solution is now in production and delivers five times more tokens per megawatt at the same ultrafast Cerebras speeds.
We can now serve more ultrafast tokens via Cerebras Inference Cloud to meet market demand. Infrastructure partners can also leverage the Cerebras disaggregated solution to achieve more ultrafast tokens in their own datacenters.
Alongside our recently announced CS-4, our dramatically scaled manufacturing, and our planned 700MW of data center capacity, disaggregated inference will enable Cerebras and partners to serve many more ultrafast tokens in 2027 and beyond.
In this blog we cover:
- The need for more ultrafast inference capacity
- Why Cerebras is advantaged for decode
- Cerebras disaggregated inference production results
- Cerebras disaggregated inference for infrastructure partners
The need for more fast inference capacity
Agentic applications like Muse, Copilot, and Codex are scaling rapidly and require highly interactive inference to keep users engaged. To meet the growing demand of these applications in a power-constrained environment, our industry must deliver significantly more inference capacity per megawatt, at response speeds that make these applications useful.
Sources: Goldman Sachs Research, “AI Agents Forecast to Boost Tech Cash Flow as Usage Soars,” May 20, 2026; Goldman Sachs data center capacity estimates, as reported by Investing.com, November 6, 2026.
That is why Cerebras invested in disaggregation. Pairing a prefill accelerator for prompt processing with the Cerebras System for response generation increases token throughput per megawatt while preserving Cerebras’ ultrafast generation speeds. For inference providers, that means more capacity to serve customers and greater revenue potential from the same power budget.
Why Cerebras is advantaged for decode
An inference request has two main stages. Prompt processing, known as prefill, processes the input and produces intermediate model state. Response generation, known as decode, uses that state to generate output tokens. There are many ways to split the inference workload, including draft–target and attention–FFN among others, but separating prefill from decode is uniquely advantageous when the goal is highly interactive inference.
Decode is autoregressive. That means the model generates one token at a time, using each generated token as an input to generate the next. Each step means reading model data from memory again, so memory bandwidth matters a lot. In the ultrafast inference domain, SRAM-centric chips have an advantage because they can keep that data on-chip and read it much more quickly. Cerebras takes this to wafer scale, with tens of gigabytes of SRAM and tens of petabytes per second of memory bandwidth on a single chip—350× the SRAM capacity and 5,400× the memory bandwidth of a typical GB300 GPU. This allows the Cerebras System to fit more concurrent requests and model layers directly on the chip, enabling the CS-3, CS-4, and CS-5 to achieve ultrafast speeds at an attractive throughput.
Cerebras disaggregated inference production results
Pairing Cerebras systems, which are optimized for ultrafast decode, with accelerators well-suited to the compute needs of prefill, such as AMD Instinct GPUs, allows the delivery of an immense amount of ultrafast inference at scale. In a production deployment on the Cerebras Inference Cloud, AMD Instinct GPUs process prompts, and Cerebras CS-3 systems generate responses. Moving prompt processing to GPUs allows Cerebras systems to devote their serving capacity to response generation. In our measured production deployment, this combination delivered 5X the throughput per megawatt of a CS-3-only configuration, with no loss in token generation speed.
Source: November 14th shared GPT-OSS-120B endpoint via the Cerebras Inference Cloud throughput per megawatt before and after enabling disaggregated inference, normalized to the aggregated inference baseline (1×).
We plan to deploy disaggregated inference broadly across hundreds of megawatts in the Cerebras Inference Cloud and in partner deployments by the end of 2027. Our aim is to increase the ultrafast inference capacity available for our customers.
Cerebras disaggregated inference for infrastructure partners
The Cerebras disaggregated inference solution was also built for our partners to adopt. Infrastructure partners can deploy disaggregated inference clusters based on Cerebras-provided reference architecture, co-locating their chosen prefill accelerators and Cerebras systems within a unified network. For software integration, partners can either integrate with the Cerebras end-to-end disaggregated software stack or a decode-only SDK.
The end-to-end disaggregated software stack is best suited for partners optimizing time-to-market. It consists of:
- A disaggregated inference framework to route requests between prefill and decode pools and manage replica capacity and health.
- A prefill engine to process prompts using open-source inference engines like vLLM or SGLang.
- A Cerebras decode engine for response generation on Cerebras systems.
- KV Portal software, an interface to transfer intermediate model state, including key-value caches, between prefill and decode engines over RDMA.
The decode-only SDK is best suited for partners optimizing for control. With the decode-only SDK, partners can bring their own inference framework and prefill engine to the Cerebras decode engine and KV Portal software. In addition, the decode-only SDK provides development aids such as a mock Cerebras decode replica and a reference integration to an open-source disaggregated inference framework and prefill engine.
Early-access partners, including Amazon Web Services, Fireworks AI, Gimlet Labs, and General Compute, already have access to the Cerebras disaggregation solution for their deployments. We are now expanding access to additional partners looking to bring ultrafast inference to their customers at scale.
To discuss the results, solutions, or deployment opportunities in more detail, contact us here.

ID.me terms required for IRS sign-in
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-18
00:51:45 UTC
09:51:45 KST

ID.me terms required for IRS sign-in
Taxpayers who sign in to Internal Revenue Service (IRS) online services with ID.me, a private-sector sign-in provider,[1][2] use it under ID.me's own Terms of Service, which send disputes outside small claims court to a neutral arbitrator instead of a judge or jury unless the user opts out within 30 days of accepting them.[3] ID.me's Privacy Policy says it "may use information for marketing purposes" & may transfer information in a sale, merger or other corporate transaction,[4] though it says information from state or federal government identity verification is not used for marketing for ID.me Shop, its shopping-discount site, unless the user consents or meets another listed exception.[4][5]
Login.gov, which the U.S. government maintains, also works for IRS sign-in,[2] & the IRS's Direct Pay service takes bank-account tax payments with "No sign-in required".[6] ID.me replaced the traditional login for Treasury's savings-bond site TreasuryDirect on October 28, 2026,[7] & on October 29, 2026 senators Ron Wyden & Elizabeth Warren asked Treasury & the IRS to explain why they continue to use ID.me "instead of the government-run Login.gov platform".[8]
Background
[edit | edit source]
The IRS describes ID.me as "a private sector sign-in provider for secure access to government and non-government websites".[2] ID.me's Terms of Service say the company provides "a level of assurance to businesses and government agencies" that a user's identity and attributes are verified "based upon widely-recognized standards",[3] & that "ID.me does not charge members any fees for registering and/or using the Service or Website."[3]
The IRS sign-in page says taxpayers "need an ID.me or Login.gov account" to access tax information online.[1] The IRS says it uses these sign-in providers "to protect your privacy and data through identity verification".[2] One account covers the IRS online services: "Individual Online Account, Business Tax Account, Tax Pro Account, e‑Services and more".[1] ID.me's Privacy Policy says some partners, "such as the IRS", may require identity verification "to a higher level of certainty" under National Institute of Standards and Technology guidelines.[4]
Treasury's savings-bond site TreasuryDirect says it uses ID.me because it "aligns with Treasury security requirements and those used by the Internal Revenue Service".[7] The IRS said on December 7, 2021 that it was "using ID.me to provide verification services".[9] It announced on February 7, 2022 that it would "transition away from using a third-party service for facial recognition",[10] & said on February 21, 2022 that taxpayers could still choose biometric verification "through ID.me’s self-assistance tool".[11]
Acceptance screen and continued-use rule
[edit | edit source]
A user accepts ID.me's Terms of Service, with the Credential Policy made available at that time, by selecting "I agree" or "taking another affirmative action clearly identified as acceptance when these Terms are presented to you".[3] The Terms say ID.me's Credential Policy "gives further details of how we accomplish our identity proofing".[3] The Terms say the company keeps a wallet for each user "that contains your identity and group affiliation information".[3] ID.me's personal-information obligations are governed "at all times" by the Credential Policy & Privacy Policy.[3]
A taxpayer emailed consumer advocate Louis Rossmann a screenshot of the "UPDATED TERMS & CONDITIONS" screen the taxpayer said they saw while signing in to IRS.gov through ID.me, showing ID.me & IRS logos and one button, "Accept Terms & Continue".
ID.me's Privacy Policy dated November 2, 2026 says by using the platform "you agree that ID.me. determines how personal information may be used" for the user's wallet.[4] The policy's May 7, 2026 text lacked it.[12]
The current Terms, Version 10.1.0 dated November 13, 2026, count continued use after revised Terms take effect as "acceptance of those changes", "Unless we expressly state otherwise"; a user who disagrees may stop using the Service & close the wallet first.[3] Version 10.0.3, dated March 3, 2023, said continuing use after notice "will indicate your agreement to all changes", "Unless otherwise noted by us".[13] The Credential Policy, Version 10.1, published July 1, 2024, requires users to reaffirm acceptance of "the prevailing Terms of Service, Privacy and other Policies and any other required agreements" "at least once every 5 years" or face credential suspension.[14]
Public radio program Marketplace reported that Nancy Kim, a professor emerita at Illinois Tech's Chicago-Kent College of Law, said she found nothing reassuring in ID.me's terms, pointing out that although they state ID.me won't sell data, ID.me can change them.[15]
Privacy policy and terms clauses
[edit | edit source]
Marketing use
[edit | edit source]
ID.me's Privacy Policy says:
We may use information for marketing purposes. We may use your information to send promotional messages and newsletters via email, or otherwise alert you to products or Services we think might be of interest to you, including for ID.me Shop.[4]
ID.me Shop, a page of deals & discounts, says it "is part of the Amazon Associates program and other affiliate programs" & "may earn money when you buy products through links on Shop".[5]
Corporate transactions
[edit | edit source]
The policy also says:
We may share your information in connection with a corporate transaction. We may disclose or transfer your information as part of, or during negotiations for, any purchase, sale, lease, merger, or any other type of acquisition, disposal, or financing involving our brands.[4]
Arbitration and class-action waiver
[edit | edit source]
A capitalized notice atop ID.me's Terms of Service says they contain "A MUTUAL ARBITRATION PROVISION, JURY TRIAL WAIVER, AND CLASS-ACTION WAIVER" & that users may opt out "WITHIN THIRTY (30) DAYS OF ACCEPTING THESE TERMS".[3] Section 20 covers disputes "arising out of or relating to" the Terms, the Privacy Policy, the website or the service.[3] A dispute not resolved within 45 calendar days after the notice of dispute is received goes to arbitration, where "a neutral arbitrator will resolve all Disputes"; both sides give up suing before a judge or jury, individually or in a class action, except in small claims court.[3] Subject to an age exception, a valid opt-out & "nonwaivable statutory rights", disputes proceed "solely on an individual basis".[3]
A user may opt out by emailing a notice titled "Opt-Out Notice" to [email protected] within 30 calendar days "after you first accept these Terms" or within 30 calendar days "after a material change to the Opt-Out Provisions takes effect".[3] Section 10 caps ID.me's aggregate liability at $1,000.00 "To the extent allowed by law".[3]
Version 10.0.3, dated March 3, 2023, had the same cap, & its notice listed "A BINDING ARBITRATION CLAUSE AND A CLASS ACTION WAIVER" with no opt-out.[13] The opt-out appears in Version 10.1.0, in a copy last updated September 18, 2025 whose opt-out text matches the November 13, 2026 version.[16][3]
Data kept after closing a wallet
[edit | edit source]
Closing a wallet directs ID.me to deactivate the credential & "purge the associated data from active use", but ID.me "does retain" account & verification history "for up to three (3) years".[4] Use of the credential with state or federal government agencies "will be stopped" if the account is deleted.[4] Information given "in connection with a public sector agency" for verification "may be retained for up to three (3) years after wallet closure", "unless applicable regulations require a shorter retention period".[4]
ID.me reserves the right to keep data from "certain high-risk transactions, particularly in government and healthcare settings", "exclusively for fraud prevention and government audit purposes".[4] The policy says biometric deletion requests "may be subject to an internal investigation prior to completion", "where determined as necessary to protect the platform against fraud", followed by "ID.me reserves the right to decline such requests".[4]
Marketplace reported that Kim said ID.me can keep a user's data for up to three years even after a deletion request.[15]
2023 and 2026 versions
[edit | edit source]
Government-verification carve-out
[edit | edit source]
ID.me's Privacy Policy adds after its marketing clause:
Please note, if you are using ID.me Services in connection with legal identity verification for a state or federal government agency, or in association with Electronic Prescriptions for Controlled Substance Services, ID.me does not use any Personal Information provided as part of your verification for any type of marketing or promotional purposes related to ID.me Shop without your consent, or unless you otherwise use your ID.me credential for verification with any ID.me customer who is not a state or federal government agency customer, use your ID.me wallet in connection with ID.me Shop, ID.me Jobs, or ID.me Rx, or otherwise opt-in to receiving marketing communications from ID.me.[4]
ID.me's policy says the company "may share" a user's name, date of birth & contact details for reporting with the government agency the user verifies for, & that verifying "constitutes consent" to it.[4] Terms of Service 10.1.0, dated September 18, 2025 & November 13, 2026, "do not provide consent to marketing calls or text messages".[16][3]
The August 18, 2026 IRS help page said ID.me "will not share, sell, rent or trade" personally identifiable information "with other parties, except to verify your identity and eligibility";[18] the October 29, 2026 version points readers to "their privacy policies at Login.gov and IDMe".[2]
Login.gov and paying without signing in
[edit | edit source]
The IRS help page last reviewed or updated on October 29, 2026 says that "The U.S. government maintains the Login.gov system", & that taxpayers who cannot verify their identity with one provider should "try the other provider".[2] The version last reviewed or updated on August 18, 2026 named only ID.me as the sign-in account.[18] The IRS sign-in page says to "choose ID.me" for anyone who does not have "an SSN, U.S. address, and smartphone, or tablet with a camera", SSN being a Social Security number.[1]
Login.gov is provided by the federal General Services Administration (GSA).[19] Login.gov's privacy page says a user who creates an account & verifies their identity consents to Login.gov "collecting, using, keeping, and sharing your personal information" as its policies describe, "including to verify your identity and to help detect and prevent fraud".[20]
Login.gov's Rules of Use say customer-support feedback may be used "for other purposes as we see fit, such as for marketing", though Login.gov "will not reveal your personal information outside of the Login.gov service" except "as required by applicable law or as stated elsewhere in these terms".[19] They also say disputes "shall be governed by federal law" & that no fees are charged for registering or using the service.[19] In a 2026 blog post, GSA wrote that Login.gov's privacy protections let people access government services "without their information being used for commercial purposes".[21]
Direct Pay, the IRS service for paying taxes from a bank account, lists "No sign-in required"; people who have never filed, or who last filed over six years ago, are told they "can pay another way".[6] Taxpayers who cannot register online can have a tax return or tax account transcript mailed; the IRS lists "transcripts of past tax returns" & "tax account information" among their tax records.[22]
TreasuryDirect move and Senate letter
[edit | edit source]
ID.me became available for TreasuryDirect logins on September 13, 2026 & replaced the traditional TreasuryDirect login on October 28, 2026.[7] TreasuryDirect says Login.gov "cannot be used to log in to your TreasuryDirect account", & a user who declines to let ID.me share their verified identity information cannot log in.[7] Its FAQ states neither TreasuryDirect nor ID.me "sells, rents, or shares your personal information with third parties for marketing or advertising purposes".[7] An ID.me spokesman wrote to Marketplace, "We do not sell, rent, or trade users’ personal information".[15]
On October 29, 2026, Senate Finance Committee Ranking Member Ron Wyden, D-Ore., & Senate Banking, Housing, and Urban Affairs Committee Ranking Member Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., demanded that Treasury halt "a rushed transition" to ID.me for Americans trying to access their savings bonds online.[8] The senators wrote:
Americans should not have to provide sensitive personal information to ID.me as the price of accessing essential government services when a secure government-operated alternative is available[8]
They asked the agencies to delay the ID.me requirement until after the 2027 tax filing deadline.[8]
Their letter, dated October 27, 2026, said federal law requires agencies to implement "a single sign-on trusted identity platform" developed by the General Services Administration, citing 6 U.S.C. § 1523(b)(1)(D).[23] It said ID.me was awarded a five-year agreement with a ceiling of more than $1 billion for identity verification, authentication, and fraud prevention services.[23] The letter requested a response by November 9, 2026.[23]
Trade publication Biometric Update reported that two Treasury orders for ID.me subscriptions were placed under "a five-year Treasury blanket purchase agreement for ID.me identity verification and authentication services" with "a ceiling of approximately $1.03 billion", and that "Federal procurement records show about $93 million obligated against the vehicle so far".[24]
An August 31, 2026 memorandum from the Office of Management and Budget, M-26-18, says agencies offering other identity solutions must promote Login.gov as the default option for new account creation "for any user population that Login.gov is able to serve".[25] The IRS said in an emailed statement, quoted by news site FedScoop, that "Taxpayers can use Login.gov or ID.me" ahead of the 2027 filing season.[26]

ID.me terms required for IRS sign-in

09:51:45 KST

ID.me terms required for IRS sign-in
Taxpayers who sign in to Internal Revenue Service (IRS) online services with ID.me, a private-sector sign-in provider,[1][2] use it under ID.me's own Terms of Service, which send disputes outside small claims court to a neutral arbitrator instead of a judge or jury unless the user opts out within 30 days of accepting them.[3] ID.me's Privacy Policy says it "may use information for marketing purposes" & may transfer information in a sale, merger or other corporate transaction,[4] though it says information from state or federal government identity verification is not used for marketing for ID.me Shop, its shopping-discount site, unless the user consents or meets another listed exception.[4][5]
Login.gov, which the U.S. government maintains, also works for IRS sign-in,[2] & the IRS's Direct Pay service takes bank-account tax payments with "No sign-in required".[6] ID.me replaced the traditional login for Treasury's savings-bond site TreasuryDirect on October 28, 2026,[7] & on October 29, 2026 senators Ron Wyden & Elizabeth Warren asked Treasury & the IRS to explain why they continue to use ID.me "instead of the government-run Login.gov platform".[8]
Background
[edit | edit source]
The IRS describes ID.me as "a private sector sign-in provider for secure access to government and non-government websites".[2] ID.me's Terms of Service say the company provides "a level of assurance to businesses and government agencies" that a user's identity and attributes are verified "based upon widely-recognized standards",[3] & that "ID.me does not charge members any fees for registering and/or using the Service or Website."[3]
The IRS sign-in page says taxpayers "need an ID.me or Login.gov account" to access tax information online.[1] The IRS says it uses these sign-in providers "to protect your privacy and data through identity verification".[2] One account covers the IRS online services: "Individual Online Account, Business Tax Account, Tax Pro Account, e‑Services and more".[1] ID.me's Privacy Policy says some partners, "such as the IRS", may require identity verification "to a higher level of certainty" under National Institute of Standards and Technology guidelines.[4]
Treasury's savings-bond site TreasuryDirect says it uses ID.me because it "aligns with Treasury security requirements and those used by the Internal Revenue Service".[7] The IRS said on December 7, 2021 that it was "using ID.me to provide verification services".[9] It announced on February 7, 2022 that it would "transition away from using a third-party service for facial recognition",[10] & said on February 21, 2022 that taxpayers could still choose biometric verification "through ID.me’s self-assistance tool".[11]
Acceptance screen and continued-use rule
[edit | edit source]
A user accepts ID.me's Terms of Service, with the Credential Policy made available at that time, by selecting "I agree" or "taking another affirmative action clearly identified as acceptance when these Terms are presented to you".[3] The Terms say ID.me's Credential Policy "gives further details of how we accomplish our identity proofing".[3] The Terms say the company keeps a wallet for each user "that contains your identity and group affiliation information".[3] ID.me's personal-information obligations are governed "at all times" by the Credential Policy & Privacy Policy.[3]
A taxpayer emailed consumer advocate Louis Rossmann a screenshot of the "UPDATED TERMS & CONDITIONS" screen the taxpayer said they saw while signing in to IRS.gov through ID.me, showing ID.me & IRS logos and one button, "Accept Terms & Continue".
ID.me's Privacy Policy dated November 2, 2026 says by using the platform "you agree that ID.me. determines how personal information may be used" for the user's wallet.[4] The policy's May 7, 2026 text lacked it.[12]
The current Terms, Version 10.1.0 dated November 13, 2026, count continued use after revised Terms take effect as "acceptance of those changes", "Unless we expressly state otherwise"; a user who disagrees may stop using the Service & close the wallet first.[3] Version 10.0.3, dated March 3, 2023, said continuing use after notice "will indicate your agreement to all changes", "Unless otherwise noted by us".[13] The Credential Policy, Version 10.1, published July 1, 2024, requires users to reaffirm acceptance of "the prevailing Terms of Service, Privacy and other Policies and any other required agreements" "at least once every 5 years" or face credential suspension.[14]
Public radio program Marketplace reported that Nancy Kim, a professor emerita at Illinois Tech's Chicago-Kent College of Law, said she found nothing reassuring in ID.me's terms, pointing out that although they state ID.me won't sell data, ID.me can change them.[15]
Privacy policy and terms clauses
[edit | edit source]
Marketing use
[edit | edit source]
ID.me's Privacy Policy says:
We may use information for marketing purposes. We may use your information to send promotional messages and newsletters via email, or otherwise alert you to products or Services we think might be of interest to you, including for ID.me Shop.[4]
ID.me Shop, a page of deals & discounts, says it "is part of the Amazon Associates program and other affiliate programs" & "may earn money when you buy products through links on Shop".[5]
Corporate transactions
[edit | edit source]
The policy also says:
We may share your information in connection with a corporate transaction. We may disclose or transfer your information as part of, or during negotiations for, any purchase, sale, lease, merger, or any other type of acquisition, disposal, or financing involving our brands.[4]
Arbitration and class-action waiver
[edit | edit source]
A capitalized notice atop ID.me's Terms of Service says they contain "A MUTUAL ARBITRATION PROVISION, JURY TRIAL WAIVER, AND CLASS-ACTION WAIVER" & that users may opt out "WITHIN THIRTY (30) DAYS OF ACCEPTING THESE TERMS".[3] Section 20 covers disputes "arising out of or relating to" the Terms, the Privacy Policy, the website or the service.[3] A dispute not resolved within 45 calendar days after the notice of dispute is received goes to arbitration, where "a neutral arbitrator will resolve all Disputes"; both sides give up suing before a judge or jury, individually or in a class action, except in small claims court.[3] Subject to an age exception, a valid opt-out & "nonwaivable statutory rights", disputes proceed "solely on an individual basis".[3]
A user may opt out by emailing a notice titled "Opt-Out Notice" to [email protected] within 30 calendar days "after you first accept these Terms" or within 30 calendar days "after a material change to the Opt-Out Provisions takes effect".[3] Section 10 caps ID.me's aggregate liability at $1,000.00 "To the extent allowed by law".[3]
Version 10.0.3, dated March 3, 2023, had the same cap, & its notice listed "A BINDING ARBITRATION CLAUSE AND A CLASS ACTION WAIVER" with no opt-out.[13] The opt-out appears in Version 10.1.0, in a copy last updated September 18, 2025 whose opt-out text matches the November 13, 2026 version.[16][3]
Data kept after closing a wallet
[edit | edit source]
Closing a wallet directs ID.me to deactivate the credential & "purge the associated data from active use", but ID.me "does retain" account & verification history "for up to three (3) years".[4] Use of the credential with state or federal government agencies "will be stopped" if the account is deleted.[4] Information given "in connection with a public sector agency" for verification "may be retained for up to three (3) years after wallet closure", "unless applicable regulations require a shorter retention period".[4]
ID.me reserves the right to keep data from "certain high-risk transactions, particularly in government and healthcare settings", "exclusively for fraud prevention and government audit purposes".[4] The policy says biometric deletion requests "may be subject to an internal investigation prior to completion", "where determined as necessary to protect the platform against fraud", followed by "ID.me reserves the right to decline such requests".[4]
Marketplace reported that Kim said ID.me can keep a user's data for up to three years even after a deletion request.[15]
2023 and 2026 versions
[edit | edit source]
Government-verification carve-out
[edit | edit source]
ID.me's Privacy Policy adds after its marketing clause:
Please note, if you are using ID.me Services in connection with legal identity verification for a state or federal government agency, or in association with Electronic Prescriptions for Controlled Substance Services, ID.me does not use any Personal Information provided as part of your verification for any type of marketing or promotional purposes related to ID.me Shop without your consent, or unless you otherwise use your ID.me credential for verification with any ID.me customer who is not a state or federal government agency customer, use your ID.me wallet in connection with ID.me Shop, ID.me Jobs, or ID.me Rx, or otherwise opt-in to receiving marketing communications from ID.me.[4]
ID.me's policy says the company "may share" a user's name, date of birth & contact details for reporting with the government agency the user verifies for, & that verifying "constitutes consent" to it.[4] Terms of Service 10.1.0, dated September 18, 2025 & November 13, 2026, "do not provide consent to marketing calls or text messages".[16][3]
The August 18, 2026 IRS help page said ID.me "will not share, sell, rent or trade" personally identifiable information "with other parties, except to verify your identity and eligibility";[18] the October 29, 2026 version points readers to "their privacy policies at Login.gov and IDMe".[2]
Login.gov and paying without signing in
[edit | edit source]
The IRS help page last reviewed or updated on October 29, 2026 says that "The U.S. government maintains the Login.gov system", & that taxpayers who cannot verify their identity with one provider should "try the other provider".[2] The version last reviewed or updated on August 18, 2026 named only ID.me as the sign-in account.[18] The IRS sign-in page says to "choose ID.me" for anyone who does not have "an SSN, U.S. address, and smartphone, or tablet with a camera", SSN being a Social Security number.[1]
Login.gov is provided by the federal General Services Administration (GSA).[19] Login.gov's privacy page says a user who creates an account & verifies their identity consents to Login.gov "collecting, using, keeping, and sharing your personal information" as its policies describe, "including to verify your identity and to help detect and prevent fraud".[20]
Login.gov's Rules of Use say customer-support feedback may be used "for other purposes as we see fit, such as for marketing", though Login.gov "will not reveal your personal information outside of the Login.gov service" except "as required by applicable law or as stated elsewhere in these terms".[19] They also say disputes "shall be governed by federal law" & that no fees are charged for registering or using the service.[19] In a 2026 blog post, GSA wrote that Login.gov's privacy protections let people access government services "without their information being used for commercial purposes".[21]
Direct Pay, the IRS service for paying taxes from a bank account, lists "No sign-in required"; people who have never filed, or who last filed over six years ago, are told they "can pay another way".[6] Taxpayers who cannot register online can have a tax return or tax account transcript mailed; the IRS lists "transcripts of past tax returns" & "tax account information" among their tax records.[22]
TreasuryDirect move and Senate letter
[edit | edit source]
ID.me became available for TreasuryDirect logins on September 13, 2026 & replaced the traditional TreasuryDirect login on October 28, 2026.[7] TreasuryDirect says Login.gov "cannot be used to log in to your TreasuryDirect account", & a user who declines to let ID.me share their verified identity information cannot log in.[7] Its FAQ states neither TreasuryDirect nor ID.me "sells, rents, or shares your personal information with third parties for marketing or advertising purposes".[7] An ID.me spokesman wrote to Marketplace, "We do not sell, rent, or trade users’ personal information".[15]
On October 29, 2026, Senate Finance Committee Ranking Member Ron Wyden, D-Ore., & Senate Banking, Housing, and Urban Affairs Committee Ranking Member Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., demanded that Treasury halt "a rushed transition" to ID.me for Americans trying to access their savings bonds online.[8] The senators wrote:
Americans should not have to provide sensitive personal information to ID.me as the price of accessing essential government services when a secure government-operated alternative is available[8]
They asked the agencies to delay the ID.me requirement until after the 2027 tax filing deadline.[8]
Their letter, dated October 27, 2026, said federal law requires agencies to implement "a single sign-on trusted identity platform" developed by the General Services Administration, citing 6 U.S.C. § 1523(b)(1)(D).[23] It said ID.me was awarded a five-year agreement with a ceiling of more than $1 billion for identity verification, authentication, and fraud prevention services.[23] The letter requested a response by November 9, 2026.[23]
Trade publication Biometric Update reported that two Treasury orders for ID.me subscriptions were placed under "a five-year Treasury blanket purchase agreement for ID.me identity verification and authentication services" with "a ceiling of approximately $1.03 billion", and that "Federal procurement records show about $93 million obligated against the vehicle so far".[24]
An August 31, 2026 memorandum from the Office of Management and Budget, M-26-18, says agencies offering other identity solutions must promote Login.gov as the default option for new account creation "for any user population that Login.gov is able to serve".[25] The IRS said in an emailed statement, quoted by news site FedScoop, that "Taxpayers can use Login.gov or ID.me" ahead of the 2027 filing season.[26]

Google's AI answers reducing search traffic by 40%
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-18
00:17:58 UTC
09:17:58 KST

Google's AI Overviews cut clicks to websites by 40%
Google's AI answers are taking clicks from websites in the search results. In a randomized experiment, researchers hid AI Overviews from some US desktop users. Comparing them with users who still saw Overviews shows that an Overview cuts clicks to outside websites by about 40%. Those Overviews now appear on more than half the US keywords DataForSEO tracks, up from 6% two years ago. The industry's answer, GEO (generative engine optimization), has yet to show a way to compensate for the loss. While AI citations correlate with the same kind of link profile that goes with higher Google rankings, in March AI assistants sent websites just 0.3% of their traffic.
Search engine optimization (SEO) still decides whether you can be found, but where an AI answer appears, a ranking brings 40% fewer visits than it used to. Generative engine optimization (GEO), the practice of getting your content cited in AI-written answers, looks mostly like SEO plus two additions: being present on the third-party sites AI answers draw from, and writing pages that are easy to quote. We found little evidence for any other tactic.
Google answers first
An AI Overview is a generated summary that Google places above the ordinary results, with links to the pages it drew on. In DataForSEO's monthly panel of 20 million keywords, the share of results showing one climbed from 5.93% in September 2024 to 61.43% in August 2026. The chart at the top of this post shows that climb. Over the same period the featured snippet, the quoted passage Google used to show at the top of a page, fell from 8.96% to 0.44%, and the discussions and forums block fell from 21.13% to 7.42%. Video results rose from 9.67% to 19.98%, though that is down from a peak of 31.05% in October 2025, and local map listings rose from 19.98% to 25.99%. DataForSEO's dataset covers 190 million keywords, 165 million AI answers, and 1.1 billion backlinks, mostly for US English searches. Pew Research Center's data gives an independent check. Pew logged the searches of 900 US adults in March 2025, re-ran them in April, and found summaries on 18% of them. DataForSEO's figures for those months are 14.67% and 17.63%, close to Pew's.
You can't reliably avoid Overviews by choosing different keywords. Between the fourth quarter of 2025 and the third quarter of 2026, the rate rose for every type of search intent:
Low-volume keywords aren't safer either. In the third quarter of 2026, keywords with 201 to 300 monthly searches showed Overviews 58.59% of the time, against 48.62% for those above 600. A separate dataset, from June to September 2026, covers difficulty and price. The easiest keywords showed Overviews 60.64% of the time and the hardest 53.76%. Keywords costing advertisers more than $5 a click were at 49.55% and those under 25 cents at 50.54%, with a low of 42.63% between $0.51 and $1.00. More than half the result pages have no price data and show Overviews 60.06% of the time. The rate does vary by industry. Health is at 69.68% and finance at 68.80%, while apparel is at 37.48%, dining at 29.37%, and real estate at 22.27%.
Queries are also getting longer, especially on desktop. Over a year, queries of five or more words grew from 29.3% to 34.8% of unique desktop queries and from 29.6% to 31.9% on mobile. Three and four word searches are still the most common.
What happens to the clicks
The strongest evidence is an experiment. Saharsh Agarwal and Ananya Sen gave US desktop Chrome users an extension that put each of them at random into a group. One group saw Google as usual, and another had AI Overviews hidden. The researchers then watched two weeks of real searches in January and February 2026. On searches that would show an Overview, the group that could see them averaged 0.37 clicks to outside websites per search, against 0.62 for the group that couldn't, which the authors report as a 39.8% reduction. Searches ending with no outbound click went from 54% to 73%. Clicks on ads didn't change, which suggests Google takes care not to cannibalize its own revenue. Across all searches, with and without an Overview, the authors put the loss at roughly 18.5% of the organic clicks a site gets from Google. The authors expect the loss to grow as Overviews appear on more searches. This is a randomized controlled trial, the most reliable kind of study for separating cause from correlation or coincidence. The effect was statistically significant for informational searches. The navigational and transactional samples were too small to settle anything. This is a working paper, not yet peer reviewed, and it covers desktop only. A separate preregistered experiment by Wang and colleagues, with 1,100 people, also found that removing AI Overviews and AI Mode raised clicks to publishers.
Observational data points the same way. Pew found people clicked an ordinary result on 8% of visits to a results page with a summary and on 15% without one, and ended their browsing session on 26% against 16%. Pew's comparison isn't a controlled experiment, because the searches that draw a summary tend to be longer and phrased as questions.
Click-through rate (CTR) is the share of people shown a result who click it. Across more than 2,000 websites in DataForSEO's panel, the desktop CTR of the first organic result fell from 17.83% in September 2025 to 7.43% in August 2026, a drop of 58%. Mobile fell from 25.17% to 20.80%, a drop of 17%.
Desktop plunged from 15.18% to 7.94% between February and March 2026. AI Overviews can't easily explain that step since the report has them almost flat that month, at 42.79% and then 43.38%. A change in the format or quality of the Overviews might explain part of it, though that is speculation on our part. Mobile, meanwhile, sank to 12.96% and then rebounded to 22.49% by May. The report itself says its data "does not establish" whether AI answers, layout changes, or changes in behavior are the cause. Its sample may also be shifting underneath it, since the unique desktop queries it counts fell by a third over the year, from 1.97 million to 1.32 million. Whatever the cause, a top ranking on desktop now earns less than half the clicks it did a year earlier.
DataForSEO also estimates where clicks go on pages with and without an Overview. By its model, the first organic result holds 9 to 16 points less click share on pages with one, though these are different searches on a single day's snapshot:
The click share for the Overview's cited links looks promising, but it comes from a model. Pew, which logged real browsing, saw people click a link inside a summary on just 1% of visits, against 8% for ordinary results. Eighteen months and a large change in the product separate the two measurements, so it is difficult to say which is closer to the truth.
Google disputes that search traffic to websites is declining. In August 2025 its head of Search, Liz Reid, wrote that total organic clicks were "relatively stable year-over-year" and that Google was sending "slightly more quality clicks", meaning clicks where people don't quickly come back. She gave no figures to back that up.
Your site can't see whether someone goes back to Google, so you can't check Google's quality-clicks claim yourself, but you can check what your Google clicks are worth. RevScope, our product, shows your revenue per visitor from Google, AI Overviews included, and which landing pages earn the most.
Who the answers cite
If people read the summary and stop there, it matters which sites the summary cites. The report compares Google's AI Overviews with ChatGPT. Their most-cited lists overlap, but a different kind of site leads each one.
The report warns that the two platforms' counts aren't comparable, so compare where a site ranks within each list. Reddit and Wikipedia rank near the top of both, and by our count 16 of the 30 domains in each top 30 appear on both lists. The differences are still large. YouTube is first in AI Overviews and nineteenth in ChatGPT. Facebook, Instagram, and TikTok make Google's top ten and miss ChatGPT's top 30, while Reuters, The Guardian, AP News, and The Washington Post make ChatGPT's top 30 and miss Google's. ChatGPT also often cites nobody: 27.3% of its answers had no source, against 0.7% for AI Overviews. So, as the report puts it, strength on one platform "doesn't automatically guarantee" the same on another.
Reddit also appears in Google's ordinary top ten for 32.15% of the report's tracked results, nearly eight times Quora's 4.11%. Two limits apply to all of this. The first is that the answers come from prompts DataForSEO collected, and the report doesn't explain how they were chosen or whether they match what people really ask. The second is that big platforms do about as well in ordinary results: Pew found Wikipedia, YouTube, and Reddit made up 15% of the sources in AI summaries and 17% of those in standard results. Still, the common belief that AI favors Reddit holds up in this data.
GEO looks mostly like SEO
Google's position is that there are "no additional requirements to appear in AI Overviews or AI Mode, nor other special optimizations necessary", beyond a page being indexed and eligible to show with a snippet. Since that is a company describing its own product, we looked at outside data on two questions. The first is which sites get considered, and the second is which page on a site gets picked.
Which sites: link profiles correlate with citations
A referring domain is another website that links to yours. DataForSEO measured how closely referring domains track AI citations using Spearman's correlation, a score where 0 means no relationship between two rankings and 1 means they match exactly. The score was 0.406 for AI Overviews and 0.377 for ChatGPT. Links marked as coming from user-generated content, such as forum posts, scored lower, at 0.342 and 0.314.
These are moderate relationships, and they don't show that links cause citations, since large, well-known sites tend to have more of everything. The samples also contain only domains cited at least once, so they can't tell you what it takes to get cited for the first time. A similar pattern shows up in ordinary rankings: domains ranking first had a median of 111 referring domains against 85 for positions 4 to 10, with heavy overlap between the groups. For AI Overviews, the median cited domain had 282 referring domains on the easiest keywords and 1,571 on the hardest, after the report excluded giant platforms like YouTube and Amazon and any domain with fewer than 50 referring domains. Small sites weren't measured at all. The ranges are also wide, at 518 to 5,765 for the middle half of domains on the hardest keywords, so sites with very different link profiles get cited.
Which page: cited pages often don't rank for the query
Ranking for a query and being cited for it are drifting apart. Ahrefs found 76% of pages cited in AI Overviews ranked in the top 10 for that query in July 2025, and about 38% in March 2026. The two studies differ in sample, parsing, and the Gemini model behind the Overviews, so the size of that fall isn't a clean measurement. One possible mechanism is what Google calls query fan-out: its documentation says these features may issue "multiple related searches across subtopics and data sources". A page could then be cited because it ranks for a related question the reader never typed. Ahrefs offers this only as a possibility, and its earlier study tested the idea and found that the data didn't support it.
The most careful test of page-level tactics we found is a study by the SEO software company SISTRIX, published in October 2026. It paired 48,527 pages cited by ChatGPT, AI Mode, or AI Overviews with uncited pages from the same site, which removes the site's overall strength from the comparison. In the table, a factor of 2.7 means pages with the feature were cited 2.7 times as often as pages without it.
An experiment in the paper that gave GEO its name points the same way as SISTRIX's correlations. Aggarwal and colleagues rewrote web pages and measured how much of a generated answer drew on them. On their 10,000-query benchmark, adding quotations raised a page's visibility by about 41%, adding statistics by about 31%, and citing sources by about 27%, while keyword stuffing lowered it by about 8%, all on their main measure, the position-weighted share of the answer's words. Lower-ranked pages gained the most: citing sources lifted a page ranked fifth by 115% and cut the top-ranked page by 30%. They also tried Perplexity, an AI search product, on 200 samples, uploading the source text as files rather than letting it search. The gains on that measure were smaller, at about 21% for quotations and 9% for statistics. The main test used GPT-3.5 reading the top five search results, which is well behind today's products, so the percentages are dated even if the direction still holds.
The most debated shortcut has nothing behind it so far. llms.txt is a proposed file that tells AI systems what a site contains. SE Ranking analyzed nearly 300,000 domains and found no correlation with citations. SISTRIX also concluded there is "no measurable effect", though it cautions that domains with the file differ from others in many ways, so this part of its data doesn't have the same-site pairing. Google says "you don't need to create new machine readable files, AI text files, or markup" for its AI features. The data agrees about the files, though SISTRIX's FAQ structured data result suggests some markup may help.
AI sends little traffic, but it is growing and may convert well
AI assistants sent 0.28% of total traffic across 74,752 websites in March 2026, according to Ahrefs. That was about 3.5 million visitors, against 345 million from Google, or roughly a hundred to one. Ahrefs describes it as small but growing fast. The true figure is somewhat higher because some visits, especially from mobile apps, get counted as direct traffic. The same Ahrefs article reports that on its own site, AI search visitors were 0.5% of visitors and 12.1% of signups. Ahrefs sells SEO software, so its audience may be unusually likely to use AI tools, and one site proves little. Still, on that site an AI visitor was about 24 times as likely to sign up as the average visitor. Some of the value never shows up as traffic. People read an AI answer, form an opinion, and never click.
To count what AI visits are worth on your own site, RevScope reports when ChatGPT, Claude, Perplexity, and other assistants send visitors and whether they buy. It also tracks when AI and search engine bots crawl your pages.
Google's own reporting has a gap too. It counts traffic from its AI features inside the overall "Web" numbers in Search Console. Since 2026 Search Console also has generative AI reports, which Google says show how often your URLs appeared in those features, along with pages, countries, and devices. The announcement describes impressions and doesn't mention clicks, so you learn how often you appear, not how many visits or sales that brings. Rank tracking can't fill the gap, since a page can hold position 1 and earn fewer clicks than it used to.
What to do about it
- Keep the fundamentals. Pages must be indexable and eligible for a snippet. Referring domains correlated with citations more strongly than user-generated links did, and higher-ranking domains had moderately more of them, so links from other sites remain a reasonable investment, even though neither relationship proves cause.
- Find out who gets cited on your topics, per platform. Check your important queries in AI Overviews and ChatGPT and note the sources. Being described accurately there may matter as much as your own page. Take part in the communities those answers cite, such as Reddit, so that you are mentioned there.
- Write pages worth quoting. Answer related questions directly, add original data and named sources, and use clear headings. If you run an editorial site, show when the page was last updated.
- Skip the shortcuts. An llms.txt does no harm but has shown no benefit, AI-specific markup isn't needed, and keyword stuffing lowered visibility in the GEO paper's test.
- Judge the work by revenue. With fewer clicks to go around, it matters more which ones pay. Tie each channel to the sales it produces rather than to its rank or its visit count.
The numbers above are mainly US English, and the products change quickly, so the 2025 figures may already be out of date. Nearly all the large datasets come from companies that sell SEO data or software, including DataForSEO, Ahrefs, SISTRIX, and SE Ranking, and we sell analytics. Almost everything about what earns a citation is correlation. Three findings hold up. Controlled experiments show fewer clicks to outside websites when Google's AI features are present, domains with more referring domains tend to be cited more often among those already cited, and we found no proven shortcut to being cited.
Stop guessing what makes money
RevScope connects your web traffic to your payments, so you can see which channels, pages, and keywords bring in revenue. Start on the free plan and put your time and budget where it pays.
Rather run it yourself? RevScope's source is public. Self-host it for free.

====================================================================================================

Google's AI answers reducing search traffic by 40%
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-18
00:17:58 UTC
09:17:58 KST

Google's AI Overviews cut clicks to websites by 40%
Google's AI answers are taking clicks from websites in the search results. In a randomized experiment, researchers hid AI Overviews from some US desktop users. Comparing them with users who still saw Overviews shows that an Overview cuts clicks to outside websites by about 40%. Those Overviews now appear on more than half the US keywords DataForSEO tracks, up from 6% two years ago. The industry's answer, GEO (generative engine optimization), has yet to show a way to compensate for the loss. While AI citations correlate with the same kind of link profile that goes with higher Google rankings, in March AI assistants sent websites just 0.3% of their traffic.
Search engine optimization (SEO) still decides whether you can be found, but where an AI answer appears, a ranking brings 40% fewer visits than it used to. Generative engine optimization (GEO), the practice of getting your content cited in AI-written answers, looks mostly like SEO plus two additions: being present on the third-party sites AI answers draw from, and writing pages that are easy to quote. We found little evidence for any other tactic.
Google answers first
An AI Overview is a generated summary that Google places above the ordinary results, with links to the pages it drew on. In DataForSEO's monthly panel of 20 million keywords, the share of results showing one climbed from 5.93% in September 2024 to 61.43% in August 2026. The chart at the top of this post shows that climb. Over the same period the featured snippet, the quoted passage Google used to show at the top of a page, fell from 8.96% to 0.44%, and the discussions and forums block fell from 21.13% to 7.42%. Video results rose from 9.67% to 19.98%, though that is down from a peak of 31.05% in October 2025, and local map listings rose from 19.98% to 25.99%. DataForSEO's dataset covers 190 million keywords, 165 million AI answers, and 1.1 billion backlinks, mostly for US English searches. Pew Research Center's data gives an independent check. Pew logged the searches of 900 US adults in March 2025, re-ran them in April, and found summaries on 18% of them. DataForSEO's figures for those months are 14.67% and 17.63%, close to Pew's.
You can't reliably avoid Overviews by choosing different keywords. Between the fourth quarter of 2025 and the third quarter of 2026, the rate rose for every type of search intent:
Low-volume keywords aren't safer either. In the third quarter of 2026, keywords with 201 to 300 monthly searches showed Overviews 58.59% of the time, against 48.62% for those above 600. A separate dataset, from June to September 2026, covers difficulty and price. The easiest keywords showed Overviews 60.64% of the time and the hardest 53.76%. Keywords costing advertisers more than $5 a click were at 49.55% and those under 25 cents at 50.54%, with a low of 42.63% between $0.51 and $1.00. More than half the result pages have no price data and show Overviews 60.06% of the time. The rate does vary by industry. Health is at 69.68% and finance at 68.80%, while apparel is at 37.48%, dining at 29.37%, and real estate at 22.27%.
Queries are also getting longer, especially on desktop. Over a year, queries of five or more words grew from 29.3% to 34.8% of unique desktop queries and from 29.6% to 31.9% on mobile. Three and four word searches are still the most common.
What happens to the clicks
The strongest evidence is an experiment. Saharsh Agarwal and Ananya Sen gave US desktop Chrome users an extension that put each of them at random into a group. One group saw Google as usual, and another had AI Overviews hidden. The researchers then watched two weeks of real searches in January and February 2026. On searches that would show an Overview, the group that could see them averaged 0.37 clicks to outside websites per search, against 0.62 for the group that couldn't, which the authors report as a 39.8% reduction. Searches ending with no outbound click went from 54% to 73%. Clicks on ads didn't change, which suggests Google takes care not to cannibalize its own revenue. Across all searches, with and without an Overview, the authors put the loss at roughly 18.5% of the organic clicks a site gets from Google. The authors expect the loss to grow as Overviews appear on more searches. This is a randomized controlled trial, the most reliable kind of study for separating cause from correlation or coincidence. The effect was statistically significant for informational searches. The navigational and transactional samples were too small to settle anything. This is a working paper, not yet peer reviewed, and it covers desktop only. A separate preregistered experiment by Wang and colleagues, with 1,100 people, also found that removing AI Overviews and AI Mode raised clicks to publishers.
Observational data points the same way. Pew found people clicked an ordinary result on 8% of visits to a results page with a summary and on 15% without one, and ended their browsing session on 26% against 16%. Pew's comparison isn't a controlled experiment, because the searches that draw a summary tend to be longer and phrased as questions.
Click-through rate (CTR) is the share of people shown a result who click it. Across more than 2,000 websites in DataForSEO's panel, the desktop CTR of the first organic result fell from 17.83% in September 2025 to 7.43% in August 2026, a drop of 58%. Mobile fell from 25.17% to 20.80%, a drop of 17%.
Desktop plunged from 15.18% to 7.94% between February and March 2026. AI Overviews can't easily explain that step since the report has them almost flat that month, at 42.79% and then 43.38%. A change in the format or quality of the Overviews might explain part of it, though that is speculation on our part. Mobile, meanwhile, sank to 12.96% and then rebounded to 22.49% by May. The report itself says its data "does not establish" whether AI answers, layout changes, or changes in behavior are the cause. Its sample may also be shifting underneath it, since the unique desktop queries it counts fell by a third over the year, from 1.97 million to 1.32 million. Whatever the cause, a top ranking on desktop now earns less than half the clicks it did a year earlier.
DataForSEO also estimates where clicks go on pages with and without an Overview. By its model, the first organic result holds 9 to 16 points less click share on pages with one, though these are different searches on a single day's snapshot:
The click share for the Overview's cited links looks promising, but it comes from a model. Pew, which logged real browsing, saw people click a link inside a summary on just 1% of visits, against 8% for ordinary results. Eighteen months and a large change in the product separate the two measurements, so it is difficult to say which is closer to the truth.
Google disputes that search traffic to websites is declining. In August 2025 its head of Search, Liz Reid, wrote that total organic clicks were "relatively stable year-over-year" and that Google was sending "slightly more quality clicks", meaning clicks where people don't quickly come back. She gave no figures to back that up.
Your site can't see whether someone goes back to Google, so you can't check Google's quality-clicks claim yourself, but you can check what your Google clicks are worth. RevScope, our product, shows your revenue per visitor from Google, AI Overviews included, and which landing pages earn the most.
Who the answers cite
If people read the summary and stop there, it matters which sites the summary cites. The report compares Google's AI Overviews with ChatGPT. Their most-cited lists overlap, but a different kind of site leads each one.
The report warns that the two platforms' counts aren't comparable, so compare where a site ranks within each list. Reddit and Wikipedia rank near the top of both, and by our count 16 of the 30 domains in each top 30 appear on both lists. The differences are still large. YouTube is first in AI Overviews and nineteenth in ChatGPT. Facebook, Instagram, and TikTok make Google's top ten and miss ChatGPT's top 30, while Reuters, The Guardian, AP News, and The Washington Post make ChatGPT's top 30 and miss Google's. ChatGPT also often cites nobody: 27.3% of its answers had no source, against 0.7% for AI Overviews. So, as the report puts it, strength on one platform "doesn't automatically guarantee" the same on another.
Reddit also appears in Google's ordinary top ten for 32.15% of the report's tracked results, nearly eight times Quora's 4.11%. Two limits apply to all of this. The first is that the answers come from prompts DataForSEO collected, and the report doesn't explain how they were chosen or whether they match what people really ask. The second is that big platforms do about as well in ordinary results: Pew found Wikipedia, YouTube, and Reddit made up 15% of the sources in AI summaries and 17% of those in standard results. Still, the common belief that AI favors Reddit holds up in this data.
GEO looks mostly like SEO
Google's position is that there are "no additional requirements to appear in AI Overviews or AI Mode, nor other special optimizations necessary", beyond a page being indexed and eligible to show with a snippet. Since that is a company describing its own product, we looked at outside data on two questions. The first is which sites get considered, and the second is which page on a site gets picked.
Which sites: link profiles correlate with citations
A referring domain is another website that links to yours. DataForSEO measured how closely referring domains track AI citations using Spearman's correlation, a score where 0 means no relationship between two rankings and 1 means they match exactly. The score was 0.406 for AI Overviews and 0.377 for ChatGPT. Links marked as coming from user-generated content, such as forum posts, scored lower, at 0.342 and 0.314.
These are moderate relationships, and they don't show that links cause citations, since large, well-known sites tend to have more of everything. The samples also contain only domains cited at least once, so they can't tell you what it takes to get cited for the first time. A similar pattern shows up in ordinary rankings: domains ranking first had a median of 111 referring domains against 85 for positions 4 to 10, with heavy overlap between the groups. For AI Overviews, the median cited domain had 282 referring domains on the easiest keywords and 1,571 on the hardest, after the report excluded giant platforms like YouTube and Amazon and any domain with fewer than 50 referring domains. Small sites weren't measured at all. The ranges are also wide, at 518 to 5,765 for the middle half of domains on the hardest keywords, so sites with very different link profiles get cited.
Which page: cited pages often don't rank for the query
Ranking for a query and being cited for it are drifting apart. Ahrefs found 76% of pages cited in AI Overviews ranked in the top 10 for that query in July 2025, and about 38% in March 2026. The two studies differ in sample, parsing, and the Gemini model behind the Overviews, so the size of that fall isn't a clean measurement. One possible mechanism is what Google calls query fan-out: its documentation says these features may issue "multiple related searches across subtopics and data sources". A page could then be cited because it ranks for a related question the reader never typed. Ahrefs offers this only as a possibility, and its earlier study tested the idea and found that the data didn't support it.
The most careful test of page-level tactics we found is a study by the SEO software company SISTRIX, published in October 2026. It paired 48,527 pages cited by ChatGPT, AI Mode, or AI Overviews with uncited pages from the same site, which removes the site's overall strength from the comparison. In the table, a factor of 2.7 means pages with the feature were cited 2.7 times as often as pages without it.
An experiment in the paper that gave GEO its name points the same way as SISTRIX's correlations. Aggarwal and colleagues rewrote web pages and measured how much of a generated answer drew on them. On their 10,000-query benchmark, adding quotations raised a page's visibility by about 41%, adding statistics by about 31%, and citing sources by about 27%, while keyword stuffing lowered it by about 8%, all on their main measure, the position-weighted share of the answer's words. Lower-ranked pages gained the most: citing sources lifted a page ranked fifth by 115% and cut the top-ranked page by 30%. They also tried Perplexity, an AI search product, on 200 samples, uploading the source text as files rather than letting it search. The gains on that measure were smaller, at about 21% for quotations and 9% for statistics. The main test used GPT-3.5 reading the top five search results, which is well behind today's products, so the percentages are dated even if the direction still holds.
The most debated shortcut has nothing behind it so far. llms.txt is a proposed file that tells AI systems what a site contains. SE Ranking analyzed nearly 300,000 domains and found no correlation with citations. SISTRIX also concluded there is "no measurable effect", though it cautions that domains with the file differ from others in many ways, so this part of its data doesn't have the same-site pairing. Google says "you don't need to create new machine readable files, AI text files, or markup" for its AI features. The data agrees about the files, though SISTRIX's FAQ structured data result suggests some markup may help.
AI sends little traffic, but it is growing and may convert well
AI assistants sent 0.28% of total traffic across 74,752 websites in March 2026, according to Ahrefs. That was about 3.5 million visitors, against 345 million from Google, or roughly a hundred to one. Ahrefs describes it as small but growing fast. The true figure is somewhat higher because some visits, especially from mobile apps, get counted as direct traffic. The same Ahrefs article reports that on its own site, AI search visitors were 0.5% of visitors and 12.1% of signups. Ahrefs sells SEO software, so its audience may be unusually likely to use AI tools, and one site proves little. Still, on that site an AI visitor was about 24 times as likely to sign up as the average visitor. Some of the value never shows up as traffic. People read an AI answer, form an opinion, and never click.
To count what AI visits are worth on your own site, RevScope reports when ChatGPT, Claude, Perplexity, and other assistants send visitors and whether they buy. It also tracks when AI and search engine bots crawl your pages.
Google's own reporting has a gap too. It counts traffic from its AI features inside the overall "Web" numbers in Search Console. Since 2026 Search Console also has generative AI reports, which Google says show how often your URLs appeared in those features, along with pages, countries, and devices. The announcement describes impressions and doesn't mention clicks, so you learn how often you appear, not how many visits or sales that brings. Rank tracking can't fill the gap, since a page can hold position 1 and earn fewer clicks than it used to.
What to do about it
- Keep the fundamentals. Pages must be indexable and eligible for a snippet. Referring domains correlated with citations more strongly than user-generated links did, and higher-ranking domains had moderately more of them, so links from other sites remain a reasonable investment, even though neither relationship proves cause.
- Find out who gets cited on your topics, per platform. Check your important queries in AI Overviews and ChatGPT and note the sources. Being described accurately there may matter as much as your own page. Take part in the communities those answers cite, such as Reddit, so that you are mentioned there.
- Write pages worth quoting. Answer related questions directly, add original data and named sources, and use clear headings. If you run an editorial site, show when the page was last updated.
- Skip the shortcuts. An llms.txt does no harm but has shown no benefit, AI-specific markup isn't needed, and keyword stuffing lowered visibility in the GEO paper's test.
- Judge the work by revenue. With fewer clicks to go around, it matters more which ones pay. Tie each channel to the sales it produces rather than to its rank or its visit count.
The numbers above are mainly US English, and the products change quickly, so the 2025 figures may already be out of date. Nearly all the large datasets come from companies that sell SEO data or software, including DataForSEO, Ahrefs, SISTRIX, and SE Ranking, and we sell analytics. Almost everything about what earns a citation is correlation. Three findings hold up. Controlled experiments show fewer clicks to outside websites when Google's AI features are present, domains with more referring domains tend to be cited more often among those already cited, and we found no proven shortcut to being cited.
