Nvidia promises that revenue will keep soaring — for now
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-18
00:16:50 UTC
09:16:50 KST

Despite the turmoil at Nvidia following the departure of its disgraced founder and CEO Jensen Huang, the company — as expected — delivered a solid quarter on Tuesday. More importantly, Nvidia reassured investors that its streak of record-breaking revenue won’t stop.
On Tuesday, CFO-turned-interim CEO Colette Kress reported revenue of $115.8 billion, up 103% from the year-ago quarter and a beat above the company’s guidance. Nvidia had told Wall Street to expect $108 billion in revenue for its third quarter of fiscal 2027. Kress also projected an even bigger fourth quarter, with revenue expected to reach $130 billion, plus or minus 3%.
She also said full-year revenue is on track to exceed expectations.
“We are raising our fiscal year 2028 outlook. We expect revenue to grow approximately 75% year over year, up from 70% and off what is now a higher fiscal 2027 base,” Kress said on the quarterly conference call on Tuesday. “Our outlook considers data center readiness, the strength and durability of end customer demand, and our customers’ access to capital.”
The company also reminded investors that it was using some of its billions in cash to buy back shares. In September 2026, Nvidia increased its share buyback program by $150 billion. It spent $28.1 billion on its own shares during the quarter and still had another $227.3 billion remaining in the kitty for additional repurchases.
Ironically, Nvidia could also benefit from the plunge in its share price following Huang’s resignation last week, should shares remain depressed over the longer term. The stock is down about 10% since his departure. Investors, however, celebrated Tuesday’s quarterly results, sending shares up about 4% in after-hours trading.
Kress stressed that the company was in good shape now and for the foreseeable future, even without Huang at the helm.
“We have the best technologists on the planet with a world-class multi-year roadmap,” Kress stressed. “From a business perspective, it’s business as usual. We’ve engaged with our customers, we’ve engaged with our suppliers, our partners. They’re all 100% behind us.”
She added that the company has secured memory and advanced packaging capacity and is working with its chip manufacturer, TSMC, to “narrow the supply and demand gaps.”

====================================================================================================

Stripe backer Atomico calls for Europe to own its success stories
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-18
00:01:00 UTC
09:01:00 KST

Europe is home to many of the world’s fastest growing companies of all time, veteran VC firm Atomico highlighted in its latest annual State of European Tech report.
The report, for instance, notes that the likes of Black Forest Labs and Microagi have reached $100 million in revenue in months, not years. “I think we can put to bed the idea that there’s a fundamental reason or constraint on the ability to scale a breakout company from here in Europe,” Atomico partner and head of intelligence Tom Wehmeier (pictured) told TechCrunch.
Yet, there’s still more that the VC firm founded by Skype’s Niklas Zennström would like to see — and its call this year is for Europe to “build it” and “scale it,” but also “own it.” That’s a way of wishing these companies will remain European longer. But there’s also a sense that this success hasn’t been acknowledged enough, perhaps obscured by louder calls for improvements.
Wehmeier, the report’s lead author, was quick to opine that “policy, procurement and capital are not moving at the same speed as our founders.” But he said the tech sector should advocate for itself in an inspiring way.
“When it comes to policy, I think the language needs to be, ‘Hey, this is not us complaining. This is us saying here is an opportunity to make the magnitude of impact even greater,’” Wehmeier said. “When we frame it as problems, that becomes harder.”
This reframing may also help European tech improve its image abroad, where complaints around overregulation may have overshadowed Europe’s potential to leverage its deep tech talent and growing pool of repeat entrepreneurs to have even more of success stories.
Plenty could be still done to fully unlock this potential, and Wehmeier said that “the next few weeks are going to be a really important test, with EU Inc.” Also known as the 28th regime, this initiative would create a unified European corporate regime akin to the Delaware C Corp.
If the last stretch of EU Inc’s grassroots campaign achieves it goal, it will become much easier to create and operate businesses across the EU. It would also show that the tech system got itself heard, which could open the door to the kind of endorsement Atomico is hoping to see more of.
Politicians have strong incentives to give the tech sector what it wants. If it is Europe’s second largest sector, biggest contributor to growth, and biggest job creator, “Do we not want more of that?” Wehmeier asked rhetorically. But it is unclear whether it will win them any votes.
To make the answer more straightforward, the tech sector will have to work on having public opinion on its side. For Wehmeier, one key is to “demonstrate that this is translating into value for citizens.”
The report is supporting this endeavor with a mix of high-level data and examples from its portfolio; for instance, it notes that ICEYE’s satellites monitor severe weather, flooding, and other hazards; and Daniel Ek’s Neko Health has now done more than 100,000 body scans, some of which helped identify severe or life-threatening health conditions.
One limit to this argument is that Neko Health’s scans will seem pricey to most Europeans, who largely rely on collective, public health insurance. But Atomico seems to hope that its report will give readers FOMO not just as potential customers, but also as potential shareholders.
There’s more to be done for this to happen. Beyond joining the sector’s push for an EU capital markets union, he hopes more of Europe’s institutional investors will invest in the region’s own tech companies. “We must get to the place where Europe and European beneficiaries are the biggest shareholders in the success that we create,” Wehmeier said.
Before that can happen, the industry must overcome anti-industry beliefs like fears of AI-induced job destruction. But Wehmeier is a believer in the power of proof. “I think that trust is going to be earned through showing results to the public — be that lower energy bills, better healthcare, more homes built for cheaper and faster, and having infrastructure that we can rely on.”

Satya Nadella says he’s AGI-pilled now
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
23:43:37 UTC
08:43:37 KST

Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella says he’s AGI-pilled, at least when it comes to how AI might make startling changes to the structure of the economy and how politics might change because of it. “I am AGI-pilled in the sense that at an economy level, what is the political economy’s organization around AGI?” he asked at a roundtable discussion following his Microsoft Ignite keynote at the Chase Center in San Francisco.
Satya Nadella says he’s AGI-pilled now
But he doesn’t think Claude is conscious.
But he doesn’t think Claude is conscious.
But Nadella doesn’t think frontier models like Claude are conscious. “I don’t think so,” he said, adding that he follows Microsoft AI CEO Mustafa Suleyman’s view that companies should not give rights to models and treat them like they might have a moral status, which companies like Anthropic do as a matter of principle. Suleyman has told me he thinks Anthropic’s team has “wireheaded” themselves, and that this approach is dangerous.
Nadella wasn’t quite as firm about it. “It’s a good debate,” he said. “I’m not sort of dogmatic about it. What I’m saying is the most important social technical issue we have to deal with is how do you ensure that an economy can diffuse this technology broadly?”
I asked Nadella how he was thinking about AI after hearing his response to another reporter’s questions about Anthropic releasing more products in its office suite. Nadella talked at length about the potential collapse of the economy and the end of democracy if one AI model were allowed to achieve monopoly status.
“If the Marxist sort of view is that there’s only one firm, then it’s over, right? For all of us.”
“Most people think about this as a competition between tech firms, which of course it will be,” he said. “But remember, the model has no limits. It’ll be a healthcare company. It can be a financial services company. It can be anything if it wants to be. It can take over the government. Thinking of this as some narrow competition between tech firms, of who is going to get to the next trillion dollars of market cap, is one of the strangest, narrowest ways to think about it.”
Nadella went on to say that the very nature of companies stood to be disrupted in the future. “If intelligence has network effects, what is the future of a firm?” he asked. “If the model sees anything and learns it, and essentially all of human capital collapses into token capital, what is the role of a firm and the human capital in a firm? That’s the debate.”
“I think the political economy is going to get more and more interesting in that context. No one — at least in a democracy — can even win an election if this just goes that way, if there’s only capital and there’s no labor. I mean, I don’t know, that’s not an economy.”
“If the Marxist sort of view is that there’s only one firm, then it’s over, right? For all of us. Not just for me. You are also going to somehow be some affiliate of Anthropic.”
Needless to say, this is not how tech CEOs usually talk about the launch of competing spreadsheet software.
Of course, Nadella was also promoting Microsoft’s various AI tools at Ignite, which promise to let enterprise clients pick the most cost-effective models for various tasks, run their own models, and control their own data in different ways. The idea here is that the market will demand many different kinds of models and sovereign data in order to keep control from centralizing, a theme Nadella kept coming back to as we spoke.
“I think that if you believe that the world, the economy needs more than one firm or two firms, then by definition there need to be as many models as firms of some sort,” he said.

Nvidia is abandoning China after Jensen Huang’s ouster
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
23:37:48 UTC
08:37:48 KST

Nvidia will no longer sell its H200 chips in China, acting CEO Colette Kress said Tuesday, ending the company’s participation in a controversial loophole in the U.S. ban on the sale of advanced chips to the country.
China’s access to leading AI accelerators was an important topic during the company’s first earnings call since CEO and co-founder Jensen Huang stepped down last week. The disclosure comes the same day Nvidia announced it was creating a board-level committee to oversee national security policies, export control compliance, and global trade risks.
Nvidia’s deal to sell H200 chips in China was brokered by Huang and President Donald Trump. The arrangement required the chips to be shipped through the U.S., allowing the government to collect a 25% tariff.
The pathway angered China hawks who wanted to deny Chinese labs computing power and maintain the United States’ lead in AI, but Huang long argued that it was more important for the U.S. to compete in China. In April, he told podcaster Dwarkesh Patel that “to concede that market for the United States technology industry is a disservice to our country.”
Huang’s ouster — in part over an affair with the CEO of a company under investigation for smuggling chips into China — has coincided with new rules, issued Monday, barring advanced U.S. chips from foreign data centers that serve Chinese customers remotely.
Investment bank Jefferies forecast that those rules might trim between $44 billion and $62 billion from Nvidia’s data center revenue next year. Referencing the new export controls, Kress said that the backlog of demand for advanced chips like Vera Rubin means the rules would have no impact on the company’s top-line revenue.
In the long term, the company may face the loss of potential customers in Singapore, Malaysia, Thailand, and Indonesia. Critics of Nvidia’s dependence on China claim that as much as 20% of its revenue comes from demand linked to the country — annual revenue at its Singapore subsidiary hit $104 billion in 2025 before Nvidia stopped disclosing those figures — but that’s clearly going to change.
Even before Nvidia decided to stop shipping H200s, China had largely refused to accept their delivery. The government would prefer to generate demand for the country’s domestic chip industry, even if major AI compute users want the still-superior Nvidia equipment, according to Jacob Feldgoise, a researcher at Georgetown’s Center for Security and Emerging Technology.
Meanwhile, Chinese frontier labs are finding ways to access Nvidia chips elsewhere. All of China’s leading AI models show evidence of having been trained on Nvidia Grace Blackwell chips that are prohibited from being exported to China.
That training may have taken place at data centers outside of China, but sales to those facilities will be barred under the new rules. If large data centers suited for training AI models want to buy Nvidia chips, they will have to certify they aren’t serving any Chinese customers, export control attorney Dan Fisher-Owens told TechCrunch.
If they can’t provide that certification, the new rules may force them toward other chip options and further eat into Nvidia’s market share.

Apple Discussions, also known as the Apple Support Community and Apple Tech Exchange, is Apple’s online forum where customers ask questions, share advice, and troubleshoot Apple products and services.
Shutdown
The shutdown was announced on 2026-11-10 where the service will be shut down on 2026-11-20.[1]
The Apple Support Community will permanently close on November 20, 2026. Starting today, the site will no longer accept new posts and is available in a read-only state.
You can continue to find trusted guidance and support on the official Apple Support site. 
We want to thank the community members who have contributed their time and expertise to help others with their Apple products.

Nvidia says its open source AI strategy remains intact after Jensen Huang’s exit
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
23:04:35 UTC
08:04:35 KST

Nvidia interim CEO Colette Kress reaffirmed the company’s commitment to open source AI during Tuesday’s earnings call with analysts — the first since CEO Jensen Huang’s resignation.
“At Nvidia, we are committed to fostering a vibrant open model ecosystem, so every country, company, and industry can build its own intelligence,” Kress said in her opening remarks. She also noted that open models were accelerating AI adoption, citing a 35-fold year-over-year surge in weekly token volume on OpenRouter, including a 3.5-fold increase over the past three months.
Kress also spoke about Nvidia’s plans for Hugging Face, a platform for open source AI models that the company agreed to acquire for $12.9 billion in September, in what would be the second-largest acquisition in Nvidia’s history.
The platform, which serves more than 18 million developers and 200,000 companies, is still on track to join Nvidia, despite Huang’s ouster. Huang had heavily advocated for open source AI technology. His departure had raised questions about the company’s future approach to open source AI models, with Hugging Face’s CEO calling Huang’s departure “a sad day,” and promising to “keep fighting for open-source AI, harder than ever.”
So far, Nvidia’s plans for Hugging Face appear unchanged. The acquisition was not directly addressed during the Q&A portion of the earnings call.
“Together, we will scale its platform and make intelligence more open, more capable, and more accessible,” said Kress of Nvidia’s plans for Hugging Face.
Last week, Nvidia founder Huang resigned from his position as CEO following news about his extramarital affairs, including one with Alice Huang, an executive at the Singapore-based company Megaspeed who is suspected of being a Chinese spy. He’s also accused of having extramarital affairs with two direct reports and is now set to testify before Congress by Thanksgiving. Meanwhile, the SEC is investigating Nvidia over whether the company’s disclosures misled investors, The WSJ reported.
Within Nvidia, Huang pushed for the development of the Nemotron family of open-weight AI models built for long-running agentic AI systems and reasoning workloads. On today’s call, Kress noted that Nemotron is now the “most widely used U.S. open model on OpenRouter,” and it’s “where developers start to build their own intelligence.”
“That foundation is empowering the ecosystem,” she said.
Correction: Due to a typo, an earlier version of this article incorrectly stated the number of developers served by Hugging Face; it is 18 million, not 118 million.

Google To Keep Temporary Gemini Chats For Two Months (techcrunch.com) 6
			
		 	
				timob58 shares a report from TechCrunch: Google is extending how long it keeps temporary Gemini chats and conversations made with chat history turned off to around two months, saying the change aims to improve safety and security as AI systems become more capable. The change, which will roll out globally in the Gemini app over the coming weeks, marks a significant increase from the previous 72-hour retention period mentioned in Google's Gemini Apps Privacy Hub, according to an archived version from November 12. OpenAI, by comparison, says it may keep temporary chats in ChatGPT for up to 30 days, whereas Anthropic retains incognito chats in Claude for 30 days by default.
Google did not answer questions about what prompted the change and whether it would affect how conversations are accessed or reviewed, instead pointing to a blog post that says chats are kept longer to respond to users, process feedback, and protect its services and users. Its updated Gemini Apps Privacy Hub also cites potential system failures as a reason for retaining chats and says temporary conversations are not used to train AI models.  "A recent explainer on AI and business IP notes that providers keep data for abuse monitoring even when it is not used for training," adds timob58.

Nvidia says it doesn’t plan to reappoint Jensen Huang as CEO
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
22:42:27 UTC
07:42:27 KST

Despite Nvidia founder and former CEO Jensen Huang’s recent offer to return to the chip company he helped build from the ground up, the company is not looking to take him back.
During the company’s earnings call on Tuesday, Nvidia chairman Harvey C. Jones told listeners that Huang’s return was not something that company leadership was considering.
“On November 12, our board of directors announced that after disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company’s code of conduct, standards, and cultural norms,” Jones said, “Jensen Huang voluntarily resigned his positions as president, CEO, and board member. The Nvidia board unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO.”
On Tuesday, Huang had posted on X that since he left the company, he has “seen an outpouring of calls from Nvidia’s employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO,” and added that “if the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.”
Huang resigned last week in dramatic fashion following revelations of a number of extramarital affairs, including one with a woman who has been accused of being a Chinese intelligence agent. That woman, former Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, has maintained close ties to the former Nvidia CEO for years. Critics worry that the relationship may have influenced Nvidia’s dealings with China, and U.S. intelligence officials have expressed suspicions that she works for China’s Ministry of State Security.
The development has sent shockwaves throughout the tech industry at a moment when the future of the AI business, in which Nvidia plays a pivotal role, hangs in the balance. It has also spurred investigations, including an independent investigation into Huang’s activities by Nvidia’s board and an SEC probe into Nvidia. Huang has been replaced by interim CEO Colette Kress, who is also the chipmaker’s chief financial officer and executive vice president, while the board searches for a permanent replacement. Jones added Tuesday that the company was looking at both internal and external candidates.

Jeff Bezos says he was ‘heavily involved’ in picking the new James Bond

Robert Pattinson’s casting as James Bond made enough sense given his recent work with director Denis Villeneuve, who’s making the franchise’s next film over at Amazon. But it turns out that Amazon founder Jeff Bezos played a significant role in making the final decision, too.
Jeff Bezos says he was ‘heavily involved’ in picking the new James Bond
Bezos said that he was immediately drawn to Robert Pattinson’s performance during screen tests.
During Semafor’s Silicon Valley and the World conference today, Bezos said that he was “heavily involved” in the process of casting the newest 007:
We ran a really tight process and we made the final decision on Friday. There was a big group initially, and we down-selected to five finalists. Then we did real old-fashioned screen tests of those five finalists, and it was really interesting — full costume, real sets, other actors. We did three different scenes with each of the five finalists, and then we got together on Friday to spend a couple of hours looking at all those screening tests, and reviewing all of that. And all of the actors were extraordinarily talented.
By the time we got to this list of five, they were all amazing, but there was something special about Rob Pattinson and his performances. It was kind of immediately undebated. Denis — the director — we all sort of gravitated to Rob. By the way, initially, I would say we were slightly biased a little against him, just because he’s already so famous. We sort of thought, ‘maybe we should have somebody a little more unknown.’ But in the screen test, it was so obvious. He is James Bond.
It’s not exactly surprising that a studio leader would want to have a say in casting the new face for one of their most lucrative franchises. But with Bezos being so hands-on, one can only image what this new era of Bond films will look and feel like once Amazon starts releasing them in theaters.

Read everything that Nvidia’s board chair had to say about Jensen Huang
Harvey C. Jones said the company would not be addressing or taking questions ‘regarding the circumstances of Jensen’s resignation.’
Ahead of Tuesday’s earnings, Huang said he would be willing to return as CEO. However, during the company’s earnings call, Jones stated that “the Nvidia board unanimously accepted [Huang’s] resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO.” Jones also said that during the call, the company would not be addressing or taking questions “regarding the circumstances of Jensen’s resignation and the ongoing investigation.” He added that, “the Board is proactively dealing with the immediate consequences facing the company, as well as enhancing our oversight capacity going forward.”

As Chairman of NVIDIA’s Board of Directors, I want to say a few words on the Board’s behalf. NVIDIA is an exceptionally strong company. Its strength comes from its extraordinary people, groundbreaking technology, and exemplary track record of execution. Our financial performance speaks for itself.
On November 12th, our Board of Directors announced that after disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company’s code of conduct, standards, and cultural norms, Jensen Huang voluntarily resigned his positions as President, CEO, and board member. The NVIDIA Board unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO. The Board appointed Colette Kress, NVIDIA’s longtime and well-respected CFO, as interim CEO. In addition, the Board announced it was initiating a search for a permanent chief executive, and we have since retained Heidrick and Struggles to lead the search.

We will not be addressing or taking questions on today’s call regarding the circumstances of Jensen’s resignation and the ongoing investigation. Our Board will communicate further when appropriate. On behalf of our shareholders, the Board is proactively dealing with the immediate consequences facing the company, as well as enhancing our oversight capacity going forward.
We have an incredibly talented leadership team, and we have every confidence in Colette’s leadership, integrity, and ability to deliver as interim CEO. Colette has been at the center of this company’s extraordinary growth for the last— growth for the last 13 years, instrumental in executing strategy, and with a deep understanding of our business, customers, operations, and financial position. The Board and the entire leadership team remain laser-focused on driving NVIDIA forward. Our commitment to generating value for shareholders, customers, and partners remains unchanged.
Kress responded to a question about the company’s leadership transition, saying “the most important thing that we think through right now is our employees.” She added, “From a business perspective, it’s business as usual.”
Jones also gave an update on Nvidia’s search for a new permanent CEO:
From the board’s point of view, we take the search very seriously. We’re going to look into internal candidates as well as external candidates. The Board appreciates that we’re living in a world where the advanced silicon technologies and national security dynamics are making major changes in our marketplace. Our new CEO will have to navigate those waters very carefully. So, the search will be thorough and we want to install the CEO, the permanent CEO, as quickly as possible, but we won’t expedite the CEO before finding exactly the right person.

I’ll Never Cook Thanksgiving Without This $5 Costco Grocery (It Saves Me So Much Time)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
22:30:00 UTC
07:30:00 KST

I’ll Never Cook Thanksgiving Without This $5 Costco Grocery (It Saves Me So Much Time)
Thanksgiving is the one time of year that I get organized in the kitchen. I start my planning spreadsheet weeks in advance, with the hope that the holiday’s marathon of shopping, decorating, cooking, and hosting will go as smoothly as possible. And yet, the chaos still manages to find me — particularly just before we’re supposed to sit down to eat, when I should have my feet up with a glass of wine in hand.
In these final moments, I’m usually in a tizzy wrapping up loose ends — especially the turkey gravy. It can’t be made without a stock, a component that I always wait until the day-of to make so I can include the turkey giblets and backbone. As much as I wanted to prep it ahead of time, I couldn’t imagine omitting those parts. They’re so essential to building the rich and complex flavor. 
That was until last year, when I had an epiphany on my annual Thanksgiving shopping trip to Costco. There, I spied a rotisserie chicken and realized that maybe I was torturing myself for nothing. Maybe I could use the bones, fat, and skin from this $5 fan-favorite to prep my stock in advance — and maybe, it’d be just the missing ingredient to achieving the stress-free Thanksgiving I’d always dreamt of. 
Turns out, it solved that and so much more. 
Costco Rotisserie Chicken Is a Thanksgiving Lifesaver
Costco’s rotisserie chicken has achieved near-mythical status, for good reason: There simply isn’t a more affordable or copious protein source on the market. One of these birds provides nearly enough meat for a week’s worth of meals, but what makes it a boon for Thanksgiving are actually the parts you don’t eat, including the carcass within and the gelatinous fatty chunks that develop on it after a turn in the fridge.
I’ve used leftover rotisserie chicken bones before to make a homemade stock, so I knew this would work for Thanksgiving broth-making purposes before going in. I had a bit of concern about how it was spiced, and whether that would affect the dishes where the stock would be used, like the gravy and stuffing. But a quick peek at the ingredient label told me that none of the ingredients these chickens are cooked with — which include only neutral seasonings like salt and sugar, although they vary from one Costco to the next — would have much of an impact.
The success of this trick can be attributed to the collagen and fat in the bones, which lend a velvety, unctuous texture and exactly the kind of deep poultry essence you expect from a good stock. But the best part? You can also use the meat for quick and easy meals when you might not otherwise have the time or wherewithal — as is often the case in the days leading up to Thanksgiving. My go-to recipe is a big bowl of chicken salad, which takes just minutes to throw together and can be eaten in the speediest of formats (like on toast or in a wrap).
What’s the Best Way to Enjoy Costco Rotisserie Chicken?
To make a Thanksgiving stock with a Costco rotisserie chicken, I start by removing all the meat until there’s nothing left but the bones and fat. If the skin has lost its crispy texture (which it does after a night in the fridge) I’ll throw it in my pot, too.
From there, I’ll add all the usual suspects, including onions, garlic, carrots, black peppercorns, bay leaves, and herbs like rosemary, thyme, and sage to perfume the broth with classic Thanksgiving flavors. Then, I fill my pot with water until all the ingredients are fully immersed by at least a few inches, place it over a medium-low heat, and let the whole thing gently simmer until my house smells like autumn and fat bubbles pool at the top — about three to four hours. Finally, I strain the liquid through a fine-mesh sieve to remove all of the very small bones and veggie sediment, then let it cool to room temperature before transferring it to the fridge.
If I make it very far in advance, I like to freeze it because it only has a shelf life of about four days. But I prefer to get it ready the Monday before Thanksgiving, so I can get cracking on a make-ahead gravy as early as Tuesday or Wednesday. Now, by the time Thursday finally rolls around, all those finishing touches that normally put me in a last-second frenzy are already done and dusted before the turkey is even out of the oven.
What’s your tip for Thanksgiving prep as easy as pie? Tell us about it in the comments.

What’s “the Best” Store-Bought Cookie Dough of All Time? 3 Pastry Chefs Named the Same Brand
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
22:30:00 UTC

What’s “the Best” Store-Bought Cookie Dough of All Time? 3 Pastry Chefs Named the Same Brand
Sure, homemade chocolate chip cookies are tough to beat, but let’s be honest: There are some pretty incredible refrigerated cookie doughs lining grocery store shelves these days. And there’s no shame in keeping a pack in your fridge, either. They save a ton of time.
As it turns out, even chefs and bakers agree. I asked a group of professional bakers and pastry chefs to share their favorite store-bought chocolate chip cookie dough, and one name kept coming up again and again: Nestlé Toll House Cookie Dough, specifically the yellow tub. Here’s why.
The Best Chocolate Chip Cookie Dough, According to Chefs
Katy Languidey is pastry chef at Vincente, a bakery and bistro in Charlotte, N.C. She’s very pro store-bought chocolate chip cookie dough (and not just for its time-saving abilities). Her go-to is the big tub of Nestlé Toll House Cookie Dough.
“I love the way that they bake,” says Languidey. “They have the best taste straight out of the oven (when they’re still a bit soft but crunchy on the outside) and they hold their shape together well.” The bucket is just the added convenience if you want one cookie or need a whole bunch.
Christine Yang, the founder of iloafyou, a sourdough microbakery in New York City, didn’t hesitate with her answer either: “It’s gotta be Nestlé Toll House.” She gave the “nostalgic” factor a 10/10.
For Catherine Jessee, a former line cook and current recipe developer, it’s also extra sentimental. She “only ever uses Nestlé Toll House,” in part because she used to make it with her grandmother. The “buttery, chocolatey flavor” and sweet memories stay with her to this day. 
Buy: Nestlé Toll House Chocolate Chip Cookie Dough, $8.48 for 36 ounces at Walmart 
What’s your favorite store-bought chocolate chip cookie dough? Tell us about it in the comments below.

Amazon and Google will together own nearly 30% of Anthropic after its IPO
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
22:29:05 UTC
07:29:05 KST

Anthropic on Tuesday disclosed more details about the biggest investment agreements with major cloud providers Amazon and Google.
While we already knew that Amazon stood to make a killing on its Anthropic investments from the IPO, Anthropic revealed that Amazon’s total stake will come in at just shy of 15% (14.7% to be exact).
Even more interesting is that Amazon could have, if it wished, owned an even bigger share of the company. When Amazon invested billions in Anthropic in 2023 and 2024 (long before Claude Code took the enterprise world by storm, back in the days when Anthropic looked like it would forever be a speck in OpenAI’s shadow), the cloud giant obtained promissory notes that could convert into equity.
These notes carry conversion prices of about $2.09 or $5.05 per share, depending on the year of investment. Amazon has opted to convert a chunk of them as part of the IPO into a special class of non-voting shares that will give it about 15% ownership. (The shares can receive dividends and convert to standard voting Class A shares when sold, the filing says.)
But it has also opted to hold on to billions of dollars’ worth of the notes for now. If it converted them all right now, it would own just under 24% of Anthropic (23.8% to be precise).
Google, meanwhile, will own just under 14% (13.7%), also in non-voting shares, which come from the conversion of notes, as well as the “private placement” sale of shares as part of this IPO. A group of major tech companies (AMD, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia, and TSMC Partners) have agreed to buy $26 billion worth of this non-voting stock. Google and Nvidia each agreed to buy $10 billion worth.
So while a lot of investors stand to make billions of dollars in Anthropic’s IPO, Amazon is one of the biggest winners, and that doesn’t even count what AWS stands to earn from Anthropic’s commitment to spend more than $100 billion on its cloud services over the next decade.

====================================================================================================

iFixit’s Apple HomeView teardown reveals a device that will be difficult to repair
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
22:25:41 UTC
07:25:41 KST

After finding recent versions of Apple’s iPads and HomePods much easier to disassemble for battery replacements or other repairs, iFixit assumed it would be the same story for Apple’s new HomeView hub that looks like a seven-inch tablet stuck to a smart speaker. The reality was much different and by the time they got the HomeView apart iFixit’s skilled techs had “ripped a connector, snapped a speaker in two, and put more force on the poor logic board than any of us were comfortable with.”
iFixit’s Apple HomeView teardown reveals a device that will be difficult to repair
Apple’s inroads on designing repairable devices seems to have been abandoned with the HomeView.
The tablet section of the HomeView proved to be the bigger challenge to disassemble as a result of some questionable decisions with Apple’s design. The glue used to secure the display in place “held on harder than on any Apple product we’ve opened recently,” iFixit says. And while Apple’s decision to turn the HomeView’s entire aluminum chassis into a heat sink was clever, the thermal bond between a heat shield and the device’s logic board made separating the two a “remarkably risky operation.” While these design decisions possibly streamlined assembly at a factory, the extensive use of glue makes servicing the HomeView incredibly difficult.
In an accompanying teardown video iFixit also reveals how much smaller the speaker drivers are in the wall-mounted version of the HomeView versus the tabletop option. Although both versions are priced at $299, the wall version costs a little more when you factor in the required mount that’s not included, leaving iFixit feeling that you’re getting less performance while spending more money.

Latent Lattice Language Models
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
22:17:18 UTC
07:17:18 KST

1MIT2Adobe
Abstract
Language models typically increase per-token computation through deeper or looped layers and latent reasoning steps, while richer residual connections improve information flow across layers. We instead increase computational capacity: the number of active computational states a model maintains. We introduce the Lattice of Latents, a set of per-layer horizontal latent streams that run recurrently along time, alongside the vertical residual stream that flows through depth. Like hyper-connections, mHC, and attention or value residuals, the Lattice enhances the residual stream, but rather than adding or rerouting residual pathways through depth, it provides companion computational states that persist across tokens. Unlike hybrid Transformer–SSM architectures, whose recurrent states primarily serve as internal memory for sequence mixing, these latents directly condition the vertical stream’s attention, KV-cache writes, MLP computation, and decoding. On Transformers trained on 20B FineWeb-Edu tokens, a 406M-parameter Lattice model lowers validation perplexity from 15.41 to 14.40 over a parameter-matched Transformer. These gains extend across architectural variants. The Lattice composes with prior residual-stream enhancements: with hyper-connections and input residuals, it lowers validation perplexity from 15.41 (base Transformer) and 14.22 (Transformer with same enhancements) to 13.61. On Gated DeltaNet, Mamba-2, Triadic GDN, and GDN–Mamba-2 hybrids, it improves perplexity at every tested native state size, with Lattice-GDN & Lattice-Triadic-GDN variants outperforming pure baselines with 4× larger native states. These results identify persistent per-layer state as a complementary axis for scaling language-model computation.
Vertical residual streams flow through depth; horizontal latents persist across tokens. Switch between training and inference, and compare models.
Interactive versions of the main results. Hover over any bar or point for details.
LLLM (dmodel, dr) has a vertical residual-stream width dmodel and horizontal-latent width dr.
GFLOP/token: floating-point operations per token, excluding attention scores (which add only ≈ 0.1 GFLOP/token) and the embedding lookup.
J3 runs three sweeps; its sequential rollout uses a single pass at inference (click Show full table for results).
FineWeb-Edu validation perplexity as training continues. Circle area is proportional to parameter count.
Both LLLMs use only 22 layers vs. 40 for the Transformer 40L, so with the same attention width (1024) they keep a 45% smaller KV cache.
400M models, 20B FineWeb-Edu tokens. Lattice models narrow the vertical width from 1024 to 932 to stay iso-parameter. The Transformer and its single enhancements appear first, then the Lattice and its single enhancements, then two-enhancement combinations.
HC: hyper-connections; mHC: manifold-constrained HC; value: value residual (ResFormer; learned weights for the single +value runs, fixed 0.5 in x0 + value); x0: nanochat-style input residual.
A hard regime: the vertical residual stream is squeezed to 256 dimensions. Press play to add the horizontal-update enhancements one at a time and watch them recover most of the gap to a full-width Transformer. 10B-token pretraining.
+ adds a component; ↻ replaces one. Parameter counts are shown under each bar.
Pure vs. Lattice recurrent models (∼405M parameters, 10B FineWeb-Edu tokens) across native-state sizes per mixer. Each Lattice model is iso-parameter with its pure counterpart. Bars appear from highest to lowest perplexity, each at its state-size position.
Native-state size counts recurrent-memory entries per mixer (1K = 1024 scalars), excluding the horizontal latents and convolution states. Lattice models use input injection + vstream.

Quad9's withdrawal from France: A canary in the coal mine
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
22:13:34 UTC
07:13:34 KST

Quad9’s withdrawal from France: A canary in the coal mine
Last month, the Swiss non-profit foundation Quad9 announced that it will withdraw its free public DNS resolver from France. This difficult decision followed months of litigation and a French court ruling that imposed daily financial penalties on Quad9 for failing to block domains associated with alleged copyright infringement.
Quad9’s departure from France is both disheartening and understandable. The court’s ruling put the organization in the impossible bind of having to choose between crippling litigation and penalties, or fundamentally altering its service in a way that would undermine the privacy and security of its users. Left with no good option, Quad9 is choosing the exit door instead.
The withdrawal of a basic infrastructure service from France fundamentally harms the free and open Internet, and it should serve as a warning of the damage that will result if rightsholders and policymakers continue demanding maximal blocking across every layer of the Internet. It not only deprives French Internet users of a trustworthy and important tool; it creates a real risk of disruption to their daily lives. Quad9’s resolver is used by a wide variety of individuals and organizations in France — including government bodies, educational institutions, hospitals, and utilities — and Quad9 has no way to directly notify those individuals and institutions that their resolver will soon stop working. If the affected parties do not change their resolver before Quad9 removes its service, their Internet will fail with no explanation.
Quad9 is not the first public DNS resolver to withdraw from a country following blocking orders, and it almost certainly won’t be the last. Cisco previously withdrew its OpenDNS resolver from France, Portugal, and Belgium (temporarily) under similar circumstances. And a Belgian court recently issued an order that would impose substantial penalties if Cloudflare, Google, and Cisco do not begin blocking websites through their public resolvers by early January 2027. That’s something Cloudflare has never done.
Cloudflare has spent years advocating against orders of this kind. We are concerned that rightsholders’ unrelenting demands for blocking by public DNS resolvers will end up causing real harm to the Internet.
A refresher on DNS, the Domain Name System
To understand why this is such a problem, it helps to understand how the Internet works under the hood. DNS resolvers are a foundational piece of the Internet’s infrastructure. DNS is the Internet’s address book: when you type a website name such as cloudflare.com, the DNS system translates that domain name into the IP address your device needs to connect to it. A DNS resolver is the service that looks up that address and sends it back to your device, much like an operator finding a phone number in a directory.
DNS resolvers can be operated by anyone, but they are commonly run by Internet service providers (ISPs), mobile networks, businesses, and universities. If you’re reading this blog post at home and have never thought about DNS before, chances are that you’re using your ISP’s default resolver. Because you use your DNS resolver every time that you visit a website, it’s important for it to be reliable and trustworthy.
And because the DNS resolver has access to all of your browsing activity, which resolver you use has real privacy implications.
In addition to DNS resolvers that are provided by your local ISP, there are a variety of public DNS resolvers available. Cloudflare launched our public DNS resolver 1.1.1.1 in April 2018 to give back to the Internet. Our goal was to make a fast, reliable, privacy-first consumer DNS service available for free to everyone. We succeeded. 1.1.1.1 is among the fastest DNS resolvers in the world, including in Europe. Cloudflare's resolver doesn’t collect any user data, let alone sell it to advertisers. 1.1.1.1 has always been one of our favorite examples of Cloudflare fulfilling its mission to help build a better Internet.
1.1.1.1 is one of many public resolvers that anyone can use. In addition to 1.1.1.1 and Quad9’s 9.9.9.9, some of the other best-known public resolvers include Google’s 8.8.8.8 and Cisco’s OpenDNS. Just because these services are offered for free doesn’t mean they’re easy to provide. A well-run global public DNS resolver is an extraordinary engineering feat, built to securely and privately resolve hundreds of billions or even trillions of queries a day across a globally distributed network of hundreds of server locations.
As Quad9 notes, there are many other DNS resolvers available to users, many of them offered by essentially anonymous services whose practices are unknown or undocumented. It’s easy to change your DNS resolver, and people do so for a variety of reasons including performance, reliability, or to access specific security features.
Why public DNS resolvers should not be required to block
Rightsholders have a legitimate interest in preventing online piracy. But trying to address online infringement through public DNS resolvers is fundamentally the wrong approach.
Among other things, such blocking is ineffective. Think back to the analogy of an operator finding a phone number in a directory. If a court wants to shut down an illegal business, forcing the directory operator to cross the business's name out of the phone book doesn't actually close the business. The storefront is still open, the products are still there, and anyone who already knows the address, or uses a different phone book, can walk in.
The same is true for the Internet. DNS resolvers are just one link in the transmission chain. The only way a DNS resolver can “block” content is by declining to return the IP address for a particular website. But a public DNS resolver cannot remove the actual infringing content from the Internet, nor can it take down the servers hosting it.
Because of this, blocking by public DNS resolvers is remarkably easy to bypass. Any Internet user who wants to reach a blocked site can circumvent the block in less than a minute simply by switching to one of the thousands of other DNS resolvers available online. In fact, users don’t even need a resolver at all to reach infringing content if they already know the underlying IP address. Unsurprisingly, while blocking via public resolvers may inject some friction for some users when accessing content, there is no evidence that it actually reduces the total amount of online infringement that occurs.
In addition to being ineffective, as we’ve written previously, DNS blocking is a blunt instrument that causes collateral damage. Because DNS operates at the domain level, a resolver cannot block a specific infringing video or file; it must block the entire domain. This affects all content on that domain, whether legal or illegal. It is also entirely opaque to the user: when a resolver is forced to block a domain, the website simply fails to load. The user receives no explanation or legal notice, making it impossible to know if the site is down for maintenance, if their network is broken, or if the site was blocked by a court order.
The problem is substantially worse with respect to public DNS resolvers. Because public resolvers operate globally, any blocking will naturally cross borders, meaning that a single country could dictate what content is accessible around the world. For example, while the EU-dedicated DNS resolver DNS4EU operated by Whalebone blocks in response to country-specific orders, it does so across the entire EU. Efforts to reengineer public DNS resolvers to allow for more localized blocking would fundamentally alter the service, harming performance and resilience, undermining user privacy, and fragmenting the open Internet.
For these reasons, Cloudflare, Google, Cisco, and Quad9 have all opposed efforts to mandate blocking by public DNS resolvers, with some success. Several years ago, courts in Germany ruled in favor of Cloudflare and Quad9, rejecting requests to require DNS blocking. More recently, a French court rejected penalties against Cloudflare for failing to block through 1.1.1.1, finding that other actions we had taken through our CDN and reverse-proxy service were sufficient to comply with the underlying order.
Undeterred, rightsholders have continued to press for blocking by public resolvers with a level of determination that is hard to explain. A recent study found that the majority of online piracy in Europe is hosted in Europe. If rightsholders want to genuinely address online infringement, there are far better places to focus their efforts — most notably by requiring the hosting providers who actually store the pirated files to take them down.
Forcing public DNS resolvers out of markets hurts the Internet
The French court’s penalty order against Quad9 represents the culmination of these rightsholder efforts. As a small non-profit that only offers a public DNS resolver, Quad9 had no alternative methods for demonstrating compliance with the order. The French court therefore granted rightsholders’ request to issue penalties for non-compliance, forcing Quad9 to undermine its own service or leave the country.
Quad9’s withdrawal from France represents a genuine loss for the Internet. In the short term, removal of Quad9’s resolver is likely to cause immediate and potentially severe disruptions to Internet access. Because of the way their service is set up to preserve privacy, Quad9 has no way to know who exactly is using its resolver, let alone to directly communicate to them about the upcoming withdrawal of services. That means that individuals, hospitals, universities, and government offices all may be caught unaware when the resolver stops working. Again, because of how DNS resolvers work, those users will not receive a detailed error message explaining what happened — their Internet will simply stop working. The problem will be even worse when devices like security systems or thermostats are hardwired to use Quad9’s resolver. Fixing these issues may be slow or even impossible.
More generally, driving Quad9 out of France does absolutely nothing to prevent online infringement, but it does leave everyday Internet users with fewer safe, reliable options, particularly ones like Quad9 that also protect users from phishing and malware. Taking established public DNS resolvers away forces Internet users to fall back on the default resolvers offered by their ISPs (which may monetize their browsing data), try lesser-known resolvers operated by anonymous or less trustworthy organizations, or run their own resolver. The irony is that Quad9 and Cloudflare are being pulled into court precisely because we are transparent, reputable organizations. Penalizing transparent services only pushes users toward less accountable corners of the web.
The way forward
If nothing else, we hope that Quad9’s announcement may serve as a wake-up call to rightsholders and policymakers that seeking to block infringement at every layer of the Internet has real costs.
We believe there are far better ways to work together to fight piracy without compromising the integrity of the Internet, and Cloudflare continues to pursue innovative collaborations to that end. Our collective efforts in addressing online infringement would be put to better use in seeking to have such content removed at the hosting provider, which would both achieve a more durable solution and avoid collateral damage to the Internet.
In the meantime, we will keep resisting misguided efforts to require blocking by public DNS resolvers. Along with Google and Cisco, we have appealed the Belgian order that would impose penalties on us if we do not start DNS blocking by January 2027. We hope to get a resolution before the end of the year that would avoid another situation like the one Quad9 faces now in France.
We have grave concerns about the disruption that would be caused if additional resolvers are forced to withdraw from markets, and we will continue to advocate against that possible result.

Nvidia says it does not intend to reinstate Jensen Huang as CEO
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
22:05:05 UTC
07:05:05 KST

Starting Nvidia’s quarterly earnings call on Tuesday, new board chair Harvey Jones said that when former CEO Jensen Huang resigned, “the Nvidia board unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO.”
Nvidia says it does not intend to reinstate Jensen Huang as CEO
The board said it would not take additional questions on the call about Huang’s departure.
Jones added, “We will not be addressing or taking questions on today’s call regarding the circumstances of Jensen’s resignation and the ongoing investigation.”
Huang had publicly floated the idea of returning earlier on Tuesday. In a statement posted to X, he wrote that he “did not live up to the standards expected of everyone” at the company, that he regretted his mistakes, and that he would cooperate with the Nvidia board’s investigation into his actions. He added that he’d be willing to return to the chief executive role if offered it, though a day earlier, the Nvidia board reportedly said it was not considering bringing him back. “NVIDIA is a once-in-a-generation company,” Huang wrote. “If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.”
But since his November 12th resignation, Huang remains entangled in scandal. He’s reportedly had three affairs, two with subordinates at Nvidia, one with an alleged Chinese intelligence asset suspected of facilitating illicit chip sales. (Huang has pushed against restrictions on Nvidia chip sales in China.) He’s scheduled to testify before Congress on December 1st, and an internal company investigation is ongoing.
Tech industry heavyweights have still voiced their support for Huang, dubbing him “irreplaceable” and petitioning for his return. Some have cast doubt on the allegations that Alice Huang (no relation) was spying on behalf of China and painted the leaks about their relationship as a conspiracy against the former CEO. President Donald Trump, a close ally of Huang for much of the past year, has also backed him. On Monday, Trump said he “would not mind” Huang returning, adding that “I think he learned a big lesson — I guess maybe one lesson is more important than another.”
Trump’s closeness with Huang could also complicate things for Nvidia, however. The company has ramped up its lobbying in Washington, D.C., spending $5 million in 2025 and $4.7 million so far in 2026. But as Democrats prepare to take Congress, they’re planning to investigate Trump’s dealings with Big Tech and other industries, alleging potential corruption. “The election wasn’t very positive for AI, and [the news around Huang’s resignation] is only going to complicate things, especially for the company in particular but for AI as a whole going forward,” Brendan Glavin, director of insights at OpenSecrets, told The Verge in an interview last week.
Lior Prosor, managing partner at Deep33, a tech VC firm, said last week that he didn’t expect Huang’s resignation to pop the AI bubble, although Huang has had a significant hand in mobilizing funds for the AI infrastructure buildout in the U.S. Under Huang’s leadership in August, Nvidia entered into partnerships with six financial institutions for about $500 billion to fund such endeavors. “NVIDIA supplies roughly 75 percent of the AI compute hardware the industry consumes, and demand continues to dramatically outpace supply,” Deep33’s Prosor said in an interview with The Verge. “Neither its customers nor its competitors can easily replace the capabilities it provides, and the economic incentives driving the AI trade remain unchanged.”
Even so, a potential market cooling has some worried, as Anthropic’s IPO, slated to potentially be the largest of all time, is set for Thursday. The chipmaker had agreed to buy $10 billion in Anthropic shares via private placement, on top of a $10 billion funding promise Nvidia had already made last year.

Optimizely Hack Turns World's Biggest Websites Into Malware Dispensers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
22:00:00 UTC
07:00:00 KST

Optimizely Hack Turns World's Biggest Websites Into Malware Dispensers (report-uri.com) 8
			
		 	
				PattonPending writes: As reported by The Register, Zoom, the BBC, CNN, HSBC, and numerous other websites briefly served malware on November 16 after an attacker exploited Optimizely's "Experiments" feature to inject JavaScript into their pages through Optimizely's CDN. The malware replaced cryptocurrency addresses, attempted to steal credit card information and cookies, and sought to compromise iOS devices. Websites belonging to at least 178 organizations were affected via the "OPTIMJACK" vulnerability, which has since been patched. "Earlier today, Optimizely identified a security incident in which an unauthorized party exploited a vulnerability in Experimentation and used it to add malicious JavaScript to some customer projects," the company said. "We've fixed the vulnerability and removed the injected code from affected projects. We've also taken steps to secure affected user accounts and put additional protections in place to prevent unauthorized code from being delivered."
The researchers said they found malicious code in 188 of the 1,611 Optimizely scripts examined, including those on the company's own website and login portal, suggesting the number of affected businesses could be significantly higher.

SEC reportedly investigating Nvidia amid Huang exit

Unknown Publisher

06:48:07 KST

The Securities and Exchange Commission is opening a civil probe into Nvidia over whether the company’s disclosures misled investors, according to The Wall Street Journal.
The investigation adds to Nvidia’s stack of drama after CEO Jensen Huang resigned following news of his extramarital affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy, Alice Huang, an executive at Singapore-based data company Megaspeed.
The Commerce Department has been investigating Megaspeed since late 2024 under suspicion that the company diverted some of its Nvidia chips to China, which would violate U.S. export laws.
Congress has also demanded that Huang answer questions about his affair and how it might have affected his stance that export restrictions should be loosened. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent put it more bluntly over the weekend, accusing Nvidia of knowingly selling powerful chips to China to “goose their revenues and their earnings.”
The news of the SEC probe also comes as Nvidia reports earnings to a market that has seen its stock fall more than 10% since Huang departed. The chipmaker reported revenue of $115.8 billion, up 103% from a year ago.

Nike’s new air sole technology can mix soft and firm cushioning
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
21:38:08 UTC
06:38:08 KST

In 2024, Nike announced its Air Max 1000 that were manufactured using a 3D-printing process resulting in a shoe that had a uniform silhouette but varying areas of support and flexibility throughout. The company is now doing something similar with its iconic air-filled soles using its new Tuned Air technology. While previously the level of cushioning in Nike Air shoes was uniform across the entire sole, a new manufacturing process can produce multiple air pockets with varying levels of cushioning and stability.
Nike’s new air sole technology can mix soft and firm cushioning
The first shoes with Nike’s new Tuned Air technology will be available for runners starting in late 2027.
Nike has been putting air pockets into its shoes for nearly 50 years as a way to increase comfort and cushioning while making them lighter. Its air pockets have even found their way into apparel as a way to improve insulation by trapping body heat, but Nike plans to use its new Tuned Air technology as a way to customize the cushioning experience in its shoes and tailor them to specific athletes and sports. The first shoes featuring the technology are expected to arrive in late 2027 and will be targeted at runners.
While one of Nike’s earliest successes was track shoes with a grip design inspired by a waffle iron, the company credits the “vibrant colors on magazine covers” as the inspiration for its latest shoe technology. A Tuned Air sole is manufactured using a “proprietary digital print and selective lamination process” where an ink is applied in specific patterns that define where the layers bond to each other. This process can be used to create internal pockets in an unlimited combination of shapes and sizes, and by fine-tuning how much air is in each of those pockets, some parts of the sole can feel firmer while others feel softer.
The Tuned Air technology will not only allow Nike to highly customize the fit and feel of air sole shoes for different sports, it will also help keep the benefits of the custom tuning consistent across different sizes. The new manufacturing process will also help reduce the amount of time it takes Nike to go from a concept for a new shoe to a final design ready for production. “Prototypes that once took three weeks can now be made in three days,” the company claims in a release today. That gives Nike’s designers more time to refine new shoes, and more opportunity to experiment.

====================================================================================================

Google joins the tech outage trend by announcing it’s not having one
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
21:34:50 UTC
06:34:50 KST

Was Google feeling a little left out of the recent wave of tech outages? The company took to X on Tuesday to announce that its Gmail service is actually running just fine. In a post, the company said it was not currently seeing evidence of a broad Gmail service disruption and reminded users they could always check Gmail’s real-time status on its online dashboard.
As it turns out, the company was trying to head off reports of a Gmail outage that began circulating on X on Tuesday around the same time that Apple was seeing a number of verified outages of its own. While some posts on the social network suggested Gmail was experiencing a disruption, others said they were not having any problems.
The concerns seemed to stem from an X post by Downdetector, a crowdsourced website where users can report service disruptions themselves and see if others are having problems. Downdetector is not an official source for verifying or understanding the true scope of internet outages, and as people become more concerned about possible cyberattacks, the site’s data may become less valuable over time if every minor glitch and hiccup is reported as an “outage” by non-technical users.
The fact that Google had to announce it was not having an outage speaks to the growing anxiety around the reliability of major tech services. Over the past week, Apple and Microsoft experienced outages, an FAA network failure led to a nationwide ground stop, and some banks and airlines also reported disruptions.
However, Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton said on Fox Business Network’s “The Claman Countdown” on Tuesday that the U.S. is not currently facing coordinated cyberattacks. He told the outlet, “We do not see the indicia of a coordinated cyberattack — let me just be clear about that — and we do not see the indicia of state-sponsored activity here.”
Instead, Clayton attributed the rise in issues to businesses patching their systems. The patching may be a downstream effect of AI, as newer models have become increasingly adept at uncovering previously unknown vulnerabilities. “They’re upgrading them, they’re putting patches in place,” he said, adding that this is happening at “a much larger scale” than before.
As a result, some of the systems are “not coming back as expected in all cases,” he added.
