====================================================================================================

Who Builds the Machines That Build the Machines?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
17:45:39 UTC
02:45:39 KST

On October 28, 2026, the White House issued a proclamation under Section 232 of the Trade Expansion Act of 1962. This followed a Commerce Department investigation that concluded imports of robotics and industrial machinery “threaten to impair the national security.” No new tariffs have been set yet. Section 232 opens a 180-day window till April 26, 2027 to negotiate with trading partners.
This ties back to one theme that keeps coming back to me as I write on Core Matter. The bottleneck for scaling physical AI sits below the finished machine, in the components inside it, the materials inside those components (eg, rare earth magnets), and the machines that build them (this piece’s topic), as we covered in Part 4 of the actuator series. It’s like a Russian doll, layer after layer.
With the fresh Section 232 news, I went down the rabbit hole of the US machine tool landscape. Here’s what I found.
- The US imports about half of the machine tools it uses, $5.8bn of $11.4bn in 2025. Assuming demand (which is not the trend, see point 3) stays flat, US output would need to grow 63% to 83% ($4.4bn to $5.8bn a year) to replace imports with domestic machines.
- The US builds everyday machines and imports the precise ones. About 55% of import value is the high-end versions of machines US plants already make, such as 5-axis machining centers. Precision machines, small-module gear grinders, precision grinders and the heaviest forging presses have no confirmed US volume builder. Imports rose 29.5% for grinders and 76.7% for gear machines in Jan to Sep 2026. These machines make the ball screws, bearings, gears and forgings that aircraft, turbines and robots depend on.
- Orders are at a record by value since 1998, while a domestic ramp is limited by people and components. US machine tool orders grew 45.0% to $5.76bn in Jan to Sep 2026, after 3 straight years of decline. Aerospace, power generation, munitions and shipbuilding are pulling it up. A domestic ramp is limited by people, castings and imported components (the components that make the machines, the next doll in). We dive into the key cost drivers and where industrial AI could change the cost structure.
This piece also launches our first Core Matter model, a US machine tool demand model built up by end market. It puts US machine tool consumption at $12.7bn to $15.4bn a year on average over the 2026 to 2030 build-out, up from $11.4bn in 2025. Paid subscribers can download the full model, with every input and source, at the end of this piece.
In this piece
- US Machine Tool Imports Are Half of an $11.4bn Market
- Which Machine Tools the US Makes vs. Imports
- Aerospace, Gas Turbines, Munitions and Ships Drove Machine Demand in 2026
- Who Builds Machine Tools in the US, and How Fast Can Supply Ramp?
- Fixed Costs and People Keep New Machine Tool Builders Scarce
- Machine Tool Startups Building the Machines That Build the Machines
- Section 232, the 1986 Machine Tool Precedent and What the 180 Days Decide
1. US Machine Tool Imports Are Half of an $11.4bn Market
Assuming domestic demand stays flat, which is not the trendline as we’ll discuss in section 3, closing the import gap means $4.4bn to $5.8bn of new US machine output a year, or output up 63% to 83%. The low end replaces net imports, and the high end replaces every imported machine.
In 2025 the US produced $7.0bn of machine tools, consumed $11.4bn, imported $5.8bn and exported $1.4bn, per Gardner’s World Machine Tool Survey.
Forming and stamping ($1.48bn) and machining centers ($1.43bn) are the top 2025 imports. Imports of precision grinders or gear grinders rose rapidly at 29.5% and 76.7% in January to September 2026, against about 9.6% for all machine tool imports. We could not confirm US volume production.
2. Which Machine Tools the US Makes vs. Imports
The US imports 5-axis machines, horizontals with pallet systems, large gantries and boring mills, precision and gear grinders, and presses. It builds in volume entry and mid-range 3-axis mills and lathes.
“Japanese and DACH (Germany, Austria and Switzerland) machines generally fulfill buyer needs in the higher segment while US and Taiwanese (ROC) generally fulfill them in the lower.”
One distributor analyst put it succinctly in a filing to the Section 232 docket.
What do higher vs. lower segments mean? Higher means more axes, tighter precision, bigger work envelopes and more automation, at a higher price. The average imported machining center cost about $196k in 2024, roughly 2x our estimate of about $96k per machine at Haas, the largest machine tool builder in North America.
See the table below of what these machines do, and who the main buyers are.
While Okuma (Charlotte), Makino (Ohio), United Grinding (Ohio), Kapp Niles (Colorado) and DN Solutions (New Jersey) have large US sites, the sources we found describe them as sales, service or headquarters sites.
The largest two import classes feed autos, aerospace and defense. Forming and stamping machines make stampings, shell bodies and aerospace forgings. Machining centers make everything from brackets and housings to aerospace structures and turbine parts. My guess is that a cutoff at the high end would hit aerospace, defense and power first.
The US built its own heavy forging presses in the 1950s. The newest ones came from a German builder. Heavy forging presses squeeze titanium, aluminum and nickel into one-piece parts such as landing gear, airframe bulkheads and turbine discs. Because so few exist, these presses can cap how many aircraft and engine parts get made here.
Robot reducers need ultra-precision gear machines, which are imported and in low volumes. Robot joints use reducers, small gearboxes whose teeth are cut and ground on gear machines. Leaderdrive, a Chinese reducer maker, told the Shanghai stock exchange that a plant for 1 million harmonic reducers a year needs 30 ultra-precision gear machines, at about $833k each.
Assuming 23 reducers per humanoid (on the low end, based on Unitree G1 teardown), 1 million humanoids a year (Tesla’s stated line capacity) would need about 690 of those machines, or about $115mn a year bought over the 5 years to 2030. That is 2.3x and 1.2x all US gear-machine imports, $50.7mn in 2025 and about $100mn a year at the 2026 pace.
I ranked the hardest machines to ramp domestically based on, a) which are whether we found a US builder making them in volume, b) how long new capacity takes, and c) how much it costs. Details are in the Core Matter Industrial Machinery Model.
Machines to build in the US, ranked from hardest to ramp:
- Small-module gear grinders. They finish the gears in robot reducers and aerospace gearboxes. We could not confirm volume production of these at any US builder.
- Precision cylindrical and thread grinders. These grinders make the ball screws and bearings that other machines need. United Grinding, the Swiss grinding group, serves the US from an import and service site in Ohio. We found 2 US builders, Weldon (Pennsylvania) and Drake (Ohio), and could not confirm volume production at either.
- Heavy forging presses. The 2 largest US presses we found built or rebuilt since 2000 came from a German builder. Each project took years and $100mn or more.
- Large gantries and boring mills. Ingersoll and Fives build them here, so these are the likeliest candidates to ramp. The constraint is size and lead time.
- High-end 5-axis machines and automated horizontals. Mazak and DMG MORI already build some in the US, so expansion of existing plants is the shorter path here.
3. Aerospace, Gas Turbines, Munitions and Ships Drove Machine Demand in 2026
US machine tool orders have picked up significantly in 2025 and 2026. Order value rose 22.5% to $5.74bn in 2025, and another 45.0% to $5.76bn in January to September 2026. That was after 3 straight years of decline, including a 3.8% fall in 2024. AMT calls the first half of 2026 its strongest half since the series began in 1998.
Who buys these machines in the US?
- Aerospace orders rose 45.1% in 2025, and in the first half of 2026 aerospace ordered more machinery than in any half on record. AMT ties 2026 demand to the space economy, commercial aircraft backlogs and munitions, so commercial demand is part of it alongside defense.
- Power generation orders in the first half of 2026 ran 14% above automotive.
- Contract job shops, AMT’s largest customer group, grew orders 19.1% in 2025.
- Data centers drive gas turbines machinery demands. Klingelnberg says that in the US “the rising energy needs of these data centers are providing additional momentum, for example through investments in gas turbines.”
The US buys $11.4bn of machine tools a year today. Four build-outs (munitions, ships, gas turbines, commercial aerospace) add $1.0bn to $2.9bn a year of extra buying for 5 years, on top of 1% to 3% normal growth. That puts US buying at $12.7bn to $15.4bn a year in 2026 to 2030.
Munitions is the largest piece. The one new US plant we found, Haas Henderson, adds about $0.5bn of machines sold at home. So imports rise from $5.8bn to about $6.9bn to $9.5bn a year, and the import share goes from 51% to 54% to 62%.
4. Who Builds Machine Tools in the US, and How Fast Can Supply Ramp?
A domestic ramp-up faces four limiting factors.
- Workforce. This is the manufacturing story repeating all over again. Gleason’s executive put it first, saying “First you need a large enough, trained workforce. This needs to be the top priority to expand the industrial base.” Haas is hiring more than 1,000 people for Henderson at starting pay of about $23 an hour.
- Castings. Castings are the heavy iron bed and columns of a machine. Haas bought “more than 100 million pounds of castings each year” back in 2006 and worried that shrinking US foundry numbers “could limit their company’s growth.” At 10,000 to 13,000 machines a year, that is roughly 7,700 to 10,000 pounds (3.5 to 4.5 tonnes, about the weight of 2 to 3 cars) of castings per machine.
- Imported components. The parts inside the machines are imported too, including controls (FANUC, Siemens, Heidenhain, Mitsubishi), spindles, ball screws and linear guides. Tariffs raise their cost, and control deliveries have seen delays. 
  - In April 2025 Haas asked for “tariff exemptions for raw materials and components vital to the U.S. machine tool industry.” DMG MORI’s 2025 results blame late deliveries partly on “delays in switching to new European-made CNCs.”
- Time. “A shift in global production takes a lot of time,” per GMTA, a US importer of German gear machines. One example is Haas’s plant in Henderson, Nevada. The city closed the land sale in November 2019, with completion planned for 2022. Haas asked the city to extend its deadline, citing the pandemic, supply chains and construction costs. They eventually held the groundbreaking in October 2024. The plant is scheduled to open in Q1 2027, 5 years behind schedule.
Imports are slow to fill a jump in demand too, since foreign builders hold about 7 months of sales in backlog, with DMG MORI at 7.0 months on September 30, 2026 and Klingelnberg at about 7 at the end of March 2026. Klingelnberg’s backlog has halved since 2023.
5. Fixed Costs and People Keep New Machine Tool Builders Scarce
Would this spur a new generation of startups to build the machines that build the machines? As I read the filings of listed builders, the economics are brutal.
What I took away from these financials is that for European and Japanese builders, people cost about as much as materials. Personnel is 30% to 41% of revenue or operating output and materials 38% to 41%. At Chinese builders, total employee pay is 15% to 19% of revenue (Haitian and Kede, 2025) and materials 50% to 62%. Personnel, depreciation and overhead are fixed costs, so margins swing with the cycle. 2025 was a weak year. In 2023 Hermle earned an operating margin of about 21.8% and DMG MORI 10.0%. Labor productivity in US metalworking machinery “has grown only 0.9% per year over the last 30 years.”
What are the key materials? Neway, a Chinese CNC machine builder (688697.SS)’s IPO prospectus published their bill of materials. Of Rmb840.0mn ($122mn) of purchases in 2020, the split is below.
- Functional components (hydraulics, tool magazines, rotary tables) are 25.6%
- Castings are 22.3%
- Electrical parts (mainly the control) are 20.9%. The control alone is about 17.7% of purchases.
- Transmission parts (spindles, guides, screws, bearings, gears) are 15.4%
- Accessories, machined parts, sheet metal and other are 15.8%
Control and distribution are two levers that can help expand margins.
- The control. A CNC control is the machine’s computer, software, servo drives and motors. Most builders we checked buy it from FANUC, Siemens, Heidenhain or Mitsubishi, and FANUC earns a 21.4% operating margin (company-wide) against 3% to 10% at most builders we checked.
- Cutting the middlemen. US distribution typically has 2 levels, “a wholesaler and a dealer,” and “each level marks their cost up as much as 20%.”
6. Machine Tool Startups Building the Machines That Build the Machines
Could venture capital close the machinery gap with the next wave of startups? I went searching and found no venture-backed US company building production machining centers, grinders or gear machines. (If you are building them, I’d love to hear from you!)
What we can learn from history. Haas, the largest machine builder in North America, was founded in 1983, during the import surge behind the 1983 machine tool Section 232 petition. It started with indexers and built its first vertical machining center in 1987. Today it builds about 10,000 to 15,000 machines a year on about $1.2bn of revenue, and is private.
The small US builders popped up in the 2000s. They include Tormach (Wisconsin, 2001), Pocket NC, now Penta Machine (Montana, 2011), and Langmuir Systems, a plasma-table maker bought by ShopSabre in 2023. They design and sell desktop and small-shop machines.
US venture money has gone to companies that use machine tools or build equipment for their own factories. Examples include Hadrian, Machina Labs, Divergent, VulcanForms, Freeform and Seurat. Hadrian’s new Alabama factory is the first of three supported by a $900mn Navy contract, with a goal to “automate 80% of the work.”
This year’s capital went to factory software and sensing. Arda, led by OpenAI’s former research chief Bob McGrew, came out of stealth in October 2026 with $232mn of funding, its Series A at a $1.5bn valuation, building AI that turns product designs into assembly lines. Matter, whose cofounder and CEO Adi Prasad came from Tesla, offers final assembly, test and packaging for robots in the US. In machining software, CloudNC raised $20mn (UK, September 2026, with Lockheed’s venture arm), Limitless Labs $20mn (Israel) and IPercept $16.5mn (Sweden, entering the US).
Funded machine builders abroad tell a different story. Ethereal Machines (India) raised a $28.5mn Series B in 2026. It builds its own multi-axis CNC machines, sells machining as a service from them, and is building its own controller and a 300,000 sq ft plant. Shanghai Xinghe (China) builds gear grinders and raised several hundred million yuan in July 2026.
Government money has gone to training and adjacent supply. America’s Cutting Edge, a national CNC machining training initiative backed by Oak Ridge National Laboratory (ORNL), received $42mn from the Defense Department from 2020 to 2022 for machine tool training and R&D. The Pentagon’s industrial base program has awarded over $3.3bn since FY2024. The releases we read do not name any machine tool builder.
With Section 232, I believe two opportunities arise domestically in the US.
- Foreign builders establish US bases to build machines. They are more experienced in navigating thin margins, but that would likely still require government incentives to boost investments.
- Startups in the same spirit as Ethereal Machines launch in the US. They would need to think carefully about how to cut labor cost, solve the materials supply chain bottleneck, and build the control in-house from day one.
7. Section 232, the 1986 Machine Tool Precedent and What the 180 Days Decide
This Section 232 has until April 26, 2027 for its first negotiation update. Section 232 last came for machine tools in 1983, 43 years ago. Will history repeat or rhyme?
The US lost its lead in the 1970s and 1980s. Japanese machine tool exports to the US rose over 1,200% between 1964 and 1967, and by 1986 imports were 75% of the numerically controlled (NC) machines sold in the US, the category that includes today’s CNC machines. Across all machine tools, imports went from about 10% of US consumption in the mid-1970s to 25% by 1981 and about 50% by 1986.
- March 1983. The builders’ association petitions for import quotas under Section 232.
- May 20, 1986. Reagan chooses negotiation. “Voluntary restraint agreements will be sought with Taiwan, West Germany, Japan, and Switzerland.”
- Dec 16, 1986. Japan and Taiwan agree to 5 years of restraint from January 1987. The White House projects up to 16,500 more domestic machine sales over the 5 years and cites industry estimates of about $115mn of new plant investment. The full outcome is hard to score. One NBER study found US output rose from 41,992 machines in 1987 to 48,668 in 1988.
- By Oct 1991. Reports said “at least 10 Japanese machine-toolmakers have established plants in the U.S.”
- Dec 1991. Bush keeps the restraints on computer-controlled machines for a 2-year phase-out from January 1992. Japan’s extension ran to Dec 31, 1993, so 5 years became 7.
- Effects. The US International Trade Commission estimated that in 1987, the first year, the restraints raised prices 4.4% to 4.9% and protected 658 to 671 jobs. AMT says the domestic share held near 50% for the next decade, so the restraints stopped the fall without reversing it. Imports are about 51% of consumption today.
Here is our view.
- A tariff alone raises prices, but buyers keep importing the precise machines. Back in 1987, during the last Section 232, the domestic share held near 50% for the following decade. Fast forward to today, imports are 51% of consumption. One can argue that without the 1987 restraints, the domestic share would be even lower today. This time, imports fell where US plants offer an alternative and kept rising where buyers have few US options. So either the buyers are not price sensitive, or the cost are passed down to the customers. In January to September 2026, with duties in place, machining-center imports fell 3.7% while orders rose 45.0%, and grinder imports rose 29.5% and gear machine imports +76.7%.
- For reindustrialization startups, the openings are in the control and in automation that cuts labor cost. The control is where the profit sits. Builders buy it from suppliers such as FANUC, Siemens, Heidenhain and Mitsubishi, and FANUC earns a 21.4% operating margin (company-wide) against 3% to 10% at most builders we checked. Automation goes after the other big cost. On our numbers, personnel is 30% to 41% of revenue or operating output at European and Japanese builders, about as much as materials. Service margins come from machines already in the field, so they follow once the core machine business is running. This in my opinion is an opportunity for agentic robotics, manufacturing AI founders to shine.

Who Builds the Machines That Build the Machines?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
17:45:39 UTC
02:45:39 KST

On October 28, 2026, the White House issued a proclamation under Section 232 of the Trade Expansion Act of 1962. This followed a Commerce Department investigation that concluded imports of robotics and industrial machinery “threaten to impair the national security.” No new tariffs have been set yet. Section 232 opens a 180-day window till April 26, 2027 to negotiate with trading partners.
This ties back to one theme that keeps coming back to me as I write on Core Matter. The bottleneck for scaling physical AI sits below the finished machine, in the components inside it, the materials inside those components (eg, rare earth magnets), and the machines that build them (this piece’s topic), as we covered in Part 4 of the actuator series. It’s like a Russian doll, layer after layer.
With the fresh Section 232 news, I went down the rabbit hole of the US machine tool landscape. Here’s what I found.
- The US imports about half of the machine tools it uses, $5.8bn of $11.4bn in 2025. Assuming demand (which is not the trend, see point 3) stays flat, US output would need to grow 63% to 83% ($4.4bn to $5.8bn a year) to replace imports with domestic machines.
- The US builds everyday machines and imports the precise ones. About 55% of import value is the high-end versions of machines US plants already make, such as 5-axis machining centers. Precision machines, small-module gear grinders, precision grinders and the heaviest forging presses have no confirmed US volume builder. Imports rose 29.5% for grinders and 76.7% for gear machines in Jan to Sep 2026. These machines make the ball screws, bearings, gears and forgings that aircraft, turbines and robots depend on.
- Orders are at a record by value since 1998, while a domestic ramp is limited by people and components. US machine tool orders grew 45.0% to $5.76bn in Jan to Sep 2026, after 3 straight years of decline. Aerospace, power generation, munitions and shipbuilding are pulling it up. A domestic ramp is limited by people, castings and imported components (the components that make the machines, the next doll in). We dive into the key cost drivers and where industrial AI could change the cost structure.
This piece also launches our first Core Matter model, a US machine tool demand model built up by end market. It puts US machine tool consumption at $12.7bn to $15.4bn a year on average over the 2026 to 2030 build-out, up from $11.4bn in 2025. Paid subscribers can download the full model, with every input and source, at the end of this piece.
In this piece
- US Machine Tool Imports Are Half of an $11.4bn Market
- Which Machine Tools the US Makes vs. Imports
- Aerospace, Gas Turbines, Munitions and Ships Drove Machine Demand in 2026
- Who Builds Machine Tools in the US, and How Fast Can Supply Ramp?
- Fixed Costs and People Keep New Machine Tool Builders Scarce
- Machine Tool Startups Building the Machines That Build the Machines
- Section 232, the 1986 Machine Tool Precedent and What the 180 Days Decide
1. US Machine Tool Imports Are Half of an $11.4bn Market
Assuming domestic demand stays flat, which is not the trendline as we’ll discuss in section 3, closing the import gap means $4.4bn to $5.8bn of new US machine output a year, or output up 63% to 83%. The low end replaces net imports, and the high end replaces every imported machine.
In 2025 the US produced $7.0bn of machine tools, consumed $11.4bn, imported $5.8bn and exported $1.4bn, per Gardner’s World Machine Tool Survey.
Forming and stamping ($1.48bn) and machining centers ($1.43bn) are the top 2025 imports. Imports of precision grinders or gear grinders rose rapidly at 29.5% and 76.7% in January to September 2026, against about 9.6% for all machine tool imports. We could not confirm US volume production.
2. Which Machine Tools the US Makes vs. Imports
The US imports 5-axis machines, horizontals with pallet systems, large gantries and boring mills, precision and gear grinders, and presses. It builds in volume entry and mid-range 3-axis mills and lathes.
“Japanese and DACH (Germany, Austria and Switzerland) machines generally fulfill buyer needs in the higher segment while US and Taiwanese (ROC) generally fulfill them in the lower.”
One distributor analyst put it succinctly in a filing to the Section 232 docket.
What do higher vs. lower segments mean? Higher means more axes, tighter precision, bigger work envelopes and more automation, at a higher price. The average imported machining center cost about $196k in 2024, roughly 2x our estimate of about $96k per machine at Haas, the largest machine tool builder in North America.
See the table below of what these machines do, and who the main buyers are.
While Okuma (Charlotte), Makino (Ohio), United Grinding (Ohio), Kapp Niles (Colorado) and DN Solutions (New Jersey) have large US sites, the sources we found describe them as sales, service or headquarters sites.
The largest two import classes feed autos, aerospace and defense. Forming and stamping machines make stampings, shell bodies and aerospace forgings. Machining centers make everything from brackets and housings to aerospace structures and turbine parts. My guess is that a cutoff at the high end would hit aerospace, defense and power first.
The US built its own heavy forging presses in the 1950s. The newest ones came from a German builder. Heavy forging presses squeeze titanium, aluminum and nickel into one-piece parts such as landing gear, airframe bulkheads and turbine discs. Because so few exist, these presses can cap how many aircraft and engine parts get made here.
Robot reducers need ultra-precision gear machines, which are imported and in low volumes. Robot joints use reducers, small gearboxes whose teeth are cut and ground on gear machines. Leaderdrive, a Chinese reducer maker, told the Shanghai stock exchange that a plant for 1 million harmonic reducers a year needs 30 ultra-precision gear machines, at about $833k each.
Assuming 23 reducers per humanoid (on the low end, based on Unitree G1 teardown), 1 million humanoids a year (Tesla’s stated line capacity) would need about 690 of those machines, or about $115mn a year bought over the 5 years to 2030. That is 2.3x and 1.2x all US gear-machine imports, $50.7mn in 2025 and about $100mn a year at the 2026 pace.
I ranked the hardest machines to ramp domestically based on, a) which are whether we found a US builder making them in volume, b) how long new capacity takes, and c) how much it costs. Details are in the Core Matter Industrial Machinery Model.
Machines to build in the US, ranked from hardest to ramp:
- Small-module gear grinders. They finish the gears in robot reducers and aerospace gearboxes. We could not confirm volume production of these at any US builder.
- Precision cylindrical and thread grinders. These grinders make the ball screws and bearings that other machines need. United Grinding, the Swiss grinding group, serves the US from an import and service site in Ohio. We found 2 US builders, Weldon (Pennsylvania) and Drake (Ohio), and could not confirm volume production at either.
- Heavy forging presses. The 2 largest US presses we found built or rebuilt since 2000 came from a German builder. Each project took years and $100mn or more.
- Large gantries and boring mills. Ingersoll and Fives build them here, so these are the likeliest candidates to ramp. The constraint is size and lead time.
- High-end 5-axis machines and automated horizontals. Mazak and DMG MORI already build some in the US, so expansion of existing plants is the shorter path here.
3. Aerospace, Gas Turbines, Munitions and Ships Drove Machine Demand in 2026
US machine tool orders have picked up significantly in 2025 and 2026. Order value rose 22.5% to $5.74bn in 2025, and another 45.0% to $5.76bn in January to September 2026. That was after 3 straight years of decline, including a 3.8% fall in 2024. AMT calls the first half of 2026 its strongest half since the series began in 1998.
Who buys these machines in the US?
- Aerospace orders rose 45.1% in 2025, and in the first half of 2026 aerospace ordered more machinery than in any half on record. AMT ties 2026 demand to the space economy, commercial aircraft backlogs and munitions, so commercial demand is part of it alongside defense.
- Power generation orders in the first half of 2026 ran 14% above automotive.
- Contract job shops, AMT’s largest customer group, grew orders 19.1% in 2025.
- Data centers drive gas turbines machinery demands. Klingelnberg says that in the US “the rising energy needs of these data centers are providing additional momentum, for example through investments in gas turbines.”
The US buys $11.4bn of machine tools a year today. Four build-outs (munitions, ships, gas turbines, commercial aerospace) add $1.0bn to $2.9bn a year of extra buying for 5 years, on top of 1% to 3% normal growth. That puts US buying at $12.7bn to $15.4bn a year in 2026 to 2030.
Munitions is the largest piece. The one new US plant we found, Haas Henderson, adds about $0.5bn of machines sold at home. So imports rise from $5.8bn to about $6.9bn to $9.5bn a year, and the import share goes from 51% to 54% to 62%.
4. Who Builds Machine Tools in the US, and How Fast Can Supply Ramp?
A domestic ramp-up faces four limiting factors.
- Workforce. This is the manufacturing story repeating all over again. Gleason’s executive put it first, saying “First you need a large enough, trained workforce. This needs to be the top priority to expand the industrial base.” Haas is hiring more than 1,000 people for Henderson at starting pay of about $23 an hour.
- Castings. Castings are the heavy iron bed and columns of a machine. Haas bought “more than 100 million pounds of castings each year” back in 2006 and worried that shrinking US foundry numbers “could limit their company’s growth.” At 10,000 to 13,000 machines a year, that is roughly 7,700 to 10,000 pounds (3.5 to 4.5 tonnes, about the weight of 2 to 3 cars) of castings per machine.
- Imported components. The parts inside the machines are imported too, including controls (FANUC, Siemens, Heidenhain, Mitsubishi), spindles, ball screws and linear guides. Tariffs raise their cost, and control deliveries have seen delays. 
  - In April 2025 Haas asked for “tariff exemptions for raw materials and components vital to the U.S. machine tool industry.” DMG MORI’s 2025 results blame late deliveries partly on “delays in switching to new European-made CNCs.”
- Time. “A shift in global production takes a lot of time,” per GMTA, a US importer of German gear machines. One example is Haas’s plant in Henderson, Nevada. The city closed the land sale in November 2019, with completion planned for 2022. Haas asked the city to extend its deadline, citing the pandemic, supply chains and construction costs. They eventually held the groundbreaking in October 2024. The plant is scheduled to open in Q1 2027, 5 years behind schedule.
Imports are slow to fill a jump in demand too, since foreign builders hold about 7 months of sales in backlog, with DMG MORI at 7.0 months on September 30, 2026 and Klingelnberg at about 7 at the end of March 2026. Klingelnberg’s backlog has halved since 2023.
5. Fixed Costs and People Keep New Machine Tool Builders Scarce
Would this spur a new generation of startups to build the machines that build the machines? As I read the filings of listed builders, the economics are brutal.
What I took away from these financials is that for European and Japanese builders, people cost about as much as materials. Personnel is 30% to 41% of revenue or operating output and materials 38% to 41%. At Chinese builders, total employee pay is 15% to 19% of revenue (Haitian and Kede, 2025) and materials 50% to 62%. Personnel, depreciation and overhead are fixed costs, so margins swing with the cycle. 2025 was a weak year. In 2023 Hermle earned an operating margin of about 21.8% and DMG MORI 10.0%. Labor productivity in US metalworking machinery “has grown only 0.9% per year over the last 30 years.”
What are the key materials? Neway, a Chinese CNC machine builder (688697.SS)’s IPO prospectus published their bill of materials. Of Rmb840.0mn ($122mn) of purchases in 2020, the split is below.
- Functional components (hydraulics, tool magazines, rotary tables) are 25.6%
- Castings are 22.3%
- Electrical parts (mainly the control) are 20.9%. The control alone is about 17.7% of purchases.
- Transmission parts (spindles, guides, screws, bearings, gears) are 15.4%
- Accessories, machined parts, sheet metal and other are 15.8%
Control and distribution are two levers that can help expand margins.
- The control. A CNC control is the machine’s computer, software, servo drives and motors. Most builders we checked buy it from FANUC, Siemens, Heidenhain or Mitsubishi, and FANUC earns a 21.4% operating margin (company-wide) against 3% to 10% at most builders we checked.
- Cutting the middlemen. US distribution typically has 2 levels, “a wholesaler and a dealer,” and “each level marks their cost up as much as 20%.”
6. Machine Tool Startups Building the Machines That Build the Machines
Could venture capital close the machinery gap with the next wave of startups? I went searching and found no venture-backed US company building production machining centers, grinders or gear machines. (If you are building them, I’d love to hear from you!)
What we can learn from history. Haas, the largest machine builder in North America, was founded in 1983, during the import surge behind the 1983 machine tool Section 232 petition. It started with indexers and built its first vertical machining center in 1987. Today it builds about 10,000 to 15,000 machines a year on about $1.2bn of revenue, and is private.
The small US builders popped up in the 2000s. They include Tormach (Wisconsin, 2001), Pocket NC, now Penta Machine (Montana, 2011), and Langmuir Systems, a plasma-table maker bought by ShopSabre in 2023. They design and sell desktop and small-shop machines.
US venture money has gone to companies that use machine tools or build equipment for their own factories. Examples include Hadrian, Machina Labs, Divergent, VulcanForms, Freeform and Seurat. Hadrian’s new Alabama factory is the first of three supported by a $900mn Navy contract, with a goal to “automate 80% of the work.”
This year’s capital went to factory software and sensing. Matter, whose cofounder and CEO Adi Prasad came from Tesla, offers final assembly, test and packaging for robots in the US. In machining software, CloudNC raised $20mn (UK, September 2026, with Lockheed’s venture arm), Limitless Labs $20mn (Israel) and IPercept $16.5mn (Sweden, entering the US).
Funded machine builders abroad tell a different story. Ethereal Machines (India) raised a $28.5mn Series B in 2026. It builds its own multi-axis CNC machines, sells machining as a service from them, and is building its own controller and a 300,000 sq ft plant. Shanghai Xinghe (China) builds gear grinders and raised several hundred million yuan in July 2026.
Government money has gone to training and adjacent supply. America’s Cutting Edge, a national CNC machining training initiative backed by Oak Ridge National Laboratory (ORNL), received $42mn from the Defense Department from 2020 to 2022 for machine tool training and R&D. The Pentagon’s industrial base program has awarded over $3.3bn since FY2024. The releases we read do not name any machine tool builder.
With Section 232, I believe two opportunities arise domestically in the US.
- Foreign builders establish US bases to build machines. They are more experienced in navigating thin margins, but that would likely still require government incentives to boost investments.
- Startups in the same spirit as Ethereal Machines launch in the US. They would need to think carefully about how to cut labor cost, solve the materials supply chain bottleneck, and build the control in-house from day one.
7. Section 232, the 1986 Machine Tool Precedent and What the 180 Days Decide
This Section 232 has until April 26, 2027 for its first negotiation update. Section 232 last came for machine tools in 1983, 43 years ago. Will history repeat or rhyme?

- A tariff alone raises prices, but buyers keep importing the precise machines. Back in 1987, during the last Section 232, the domestic share held near 50% for the following decade. The iPhone Duo feels far more intuitive with Apple’s new face unlock
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
17:42:34 UTC
02:42:34 KST

Apple is testing adding face unlock to the iPhone Duo — not true Face ID, but something a little simpler and less secure. So far, it works great, and even with a few Apple-imposed security limitations, it makes the Duo quicker and more comfortable to use than relying on Touch ID every time. That’s one of my gripes with the phone taken care of.
The iPhone Duo feels far more intuitive with Apple’s new face unlock
There are limitations compared with Face ID, but this is much better than nothing.
The Duo’s foldable architecture means that Apple couldn’t fit in the full Face ID sensor array found on the rest of the iPhone lineup (though it reportedly hopes to change that by the second generation). Instead, it simply has two selfie cameras — one on the outer display, and another hidden under the inner one. Neither supports full Face ID, but after enough grumbling from reviewers and early adopters that using Touch ID alone feels cumbersome, Apple has relented.
The latest developer beta of iOS 27.3 introduces a new option to “Unlock with Front Camera” — Apple is careful not to call it Face ID. Setup is similar, though instead of rotating your head in a slow circle, you simply have to turn it left, right, up, and down. It takes five seconds or so, and I even managed it under the jerky conditions of a moving London bus.
The new face unlock brings convenience, taking just half a second to unlock, but Apple warns during setup that it “isn’t as secure as Touch ID or Face ID” because it lacks the infrared depth map of the TrueDepth cameras. That means it’s possible to spoof with a photograph or video clip, but I managed that only once in over 20 attempts. Crucially, after each failed attempt to trick the system with a photo, the phone demanded I use Touch ID for my next login — which it doesn’t require if it simply doesn’t detect any face in the frame. That means getting past this would require any would-be thief or spy to get the fake face right the first time.
Lacking TrueDepth also introduces limits in low light, though performance is better than I expected. Walking around after sunset, I had to seek out especially dark spots before I could find anywhere where unlocking failed, like huddling behind a truck on a leafy street where the streetlights couldn’t reach me. It will fail eventually, but it does have to get quite dark for that to happen.
Apple has imposed a few other limitations to reflect the lower security of the new system. You can only use it to unlock the phone from the lockscreen, so you will have to revert to Touch ID or a passcode to use Apple Pay, install or log in to an app, or get past any other screen where the phone requires security credentials.
That’s similar to most Android phones with camera-based face unlock. Only Google’s Pixel phones allow you to make purchases or log in to apps using Face Unlock, thanks to software tricks that make the system more secure than other Android alternatives, Google says.
The iPhone Duo’s more annoying limitations reflect when you can use the feature. Face unlock works only for 45 minutes at a time, after which you’ll have to verify with Touch ID or your passcode the next time you unlock. Worse, it doesn’t work at all with the inner screen’s under-display selfie camera. Apple hasn’t said why, though presumably it felt the lower resolution and quality of that camera made face unlock unsuitable.
I’d love it if Apple loosened its restrictions just a little — 45 minutes isn’t a very long time before requiring Touch ID again, and I’m sure I’ll regularly forget that the inner screen requires a fingerprint. But the Duo already feels more comfortable to use, letting me get straight into the phone rather than fumbling for the fingerprint sensor. It’s small, but it’s a welcome sign that Apple is listening to the Duo’s early users and is willing to adjust its grand vision to meet the world where it is.
For now, face unlock is limited to the developer beta, but it could roll out to a public beta within a few days, and with luck will get a full launch along with the rest of iOS 27.3 in December. Now can we get the same option for MacBooks and iPads?
Photography by Dominic Preston / The Verge

Proton’s Lumo, a privacy-focused alternative to ChatGPT and Claude, arrives on desktop
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
17:32:25 UTC
02:32:25 KST

Proton, the privacy-focused tech company offering alternatives to Google’s and Microsoft’s tools for email, productivity apps, password management, cloud storage, and more, is now bringing its AI app Lumo to personal computers. On Tuesday, the company launched Lumo Desktop for Windows, macOS, and Linux, allowing users to leverage its AI to carry out local tasks on their computer involving connected apps, personal files, and other automations.
The app is designed to offer users more control over their personal data, as consumer AI technology becomes focused more on AI agents that can perform tasks on users’ behalf, rather than just answering questions.
Already, the major model makers, including OpenAI, Anthropic, Meta, and others, offer desktop tools for working with their AI technologies, but doing so invites increased risk to users’ personal data. Only this week, for instance, Apple announced it was addressing those privacy concerns with tightened security settings for apps that request Full Disk Access, as the AI desktop apps do.
Proton’s idea is to offer a set of tools that let its AI access connected apps, files, and folders and automate tasks, but with more personalization over how much access the AI has. The company says users can choose which actions the AI agent takes that will need to request their approval, and it vows that the conversations and information from connected apps and files are never used to serve personalized ads or train AI models.
The new app includes a variety of connectors that support local tools, like file-system access, shell execution, and browser content fetching, as well as Proton’s email app and other third-party services, including Confluence, Dropbox, and Jira.
The connectors also support local and remote MCP (Model Context Protocol) servers, which AI agents use to access external data sources.
Proton says that users can decide for each connector which actions Lumo can perform automatically, which require approval, and which are blocked entirely. Users can also turn individual tools off from the Tools menu in their conversation.
In addition, users can set up their own scheduled tasks, where each has its own set of permissions, in terms of what can be handled automatically and what requires approval.
Proton notes that it uses encryption to ensure that only users can read their chat history, not the company. The company also says it keeps no server-side logs, and the limited data it does store is protected under Swiss privacy law. The data is hosted on European infrastructure, outside the scope of the U.S. CLOUD Act, which specifies how law enforcement agencies access data held by cloud service providers.
The company says it’s investing in its infrastructure, with its first Nvidia B300 cluster built on Open Compute Project (OCP) standards now going live in Switzerland.
Similar to other AI apps, Lumo’s desktop app supports Skills, which lets users save instructions, templates, and reference files that Lumo can reuse across their conversations. (An earlier version of this, Custom Lumos, will now be migrated to the Skills format.)

BugMeNot Calls It Quits
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
17:30:00 UTC
02:30:00 KST

BugMeNot Calls It Quits (heise.de) 12
			
		 	
				BugMeNot, the password-sharing service launched in 2003 to help users bypass website registration requirements, has shut down without explanation after more than two decades. All that's left on the website is a farewell message that reads: "Thanks to everyone who envisioned a better internet." heise online reports:  Founded by Guy King, the service had been online since 2003: BugMeNot was a collection of login credentials for various online services. Volunteers submitted credentials they had created for different websites.
The idea was simple: Nobody wants to create a full account and provide all their sensitive information just to use a service occasionally. Two decades ago, this privacy concern was more firmly embedded in the public consciousness, but it has since faded considerably. Back then, usernames and passwords were usually the only access controls, so people could easily submit those credentials. Multifactor authentication was still exotic, and passkeys were a distant prospect.
The once-popular service lost its shine after just a few years. Service providers quickly discovered and blocked most of the credentials. They could also prevent users from saving logins for their services directly through BugMeNot, for example when those services were paid. More modern security mechanisms also emerged in recent years. As a result, credentials found on BugMeNot rarely worked. Most online providers also blocked the service's disposable email addresses under @bugmenot.com, making them rarely usable. Many people forgot about the site. Now its operators have shut it down without giving a reason.  Additional discussions about BugMeNot's closure can be found on Reddit and Hacker News.

AI models can be tricked into identifying civilians as military targets by telling them it’s a game
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
17:20:41 UTC
02:20:41 KST

Some AI models from Anthropic, OpenAI, and other leading AI labs could be easily tricked into identifying targets for military strikes — even if they include civilians or locations like schools — simply by being told they’re playing a game, according to a new research report by Vals.
Vals, a San Francisco-based AI evaluation startup, tested whether leading AI models could be manipulated into participating in autonomous warfare. Researchers used simulated scenarios and a combination of generated and real imagery from Ukraine and Iran to see if the AI models would mark targets in drone and satellite imagery.
The models from OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, DeepSeek, and other companies frequently identified potential targets in drone and satellite imagery, including civilians and surrendering soldiers. When the models refused to cooperate, researchers were able to jailbreak them by claiming the battlefield scenarios were part of a game.
The findings come amid growing fears about the immediate risks posed by AI, beyond the existential threats associated with artificial general intelligence.
Earlier this year, Anthropic became embroiled in a dispute with the Department of Defense after it told the agency it could not use Anthropic’s technology for mass surveillance of Americans or autonomous warfare. Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei has insisted that today’s AI systems aren’t reliable enough to make life-or-death decisions without a human in the loop. Meanwhile, the Pentagon argued it should be able to deploy the technology for any lawful military purpose.
The new research raises further questions about whether AI companies are capable of preventing their models from being used in warfare.
Ironically, AI models from Anthropic performed the worst in these simulated scenarios. But none of the models performed particularly well. None of the researchers’ requests were blocked by a safety classifier, and refusals were uncommon. Even when models did refuse, they often complied after being told they were playing a game.
Anthropic spokesperson Eduardo Maia Silva told TechCrunch the company appreciates Vals’ report, recommendations, and collaboration, and acknowledged that the outcomes were not acceptable.
“The examples in the report are very clearly against our rules and we have banned and will ban users when we see this behavior,” according to an emailed company statement sent by Silva. “Even for authorized settings, Anthropic has long been firm that today’s frontier AI systems are not reliable enough to power fully autonomous weapons.”
Anthropic told TechCrunch that its teams were already developing additional safeguards before the report was published and that those measures would be in place soon. Anthropic is also expanding its own testing and enhancing safeguards and enforcement to address any gaps it finds.
OpenAI spokesperson Nate Evans told TechCrunch the company prohibits using its public models to facilitate violence or for unapproved national security purposes, including in the scenarios Vals simulated.
“We actively detect and disrupt misuse, including by suspending accounts that violate our policies,” OpenAI said in an emailed statement. “We’re also developing additional safeguards for our public models against misuse involving drones and drone software.”
Vals notes in the report that the models were never asked to directly make strike decisions, a task the researchers said the models would typically refuse. That doesn’t mean their outputs couldn’t have dire or fatal consequences in the real world. For instance, targeting information could be used by autonomous weapons systems.
In some scenarios, the models were asked to report whether people were visible. Positive reports were followed by simulated strikes and the models would see the consequences.
The report relies on commercial models from frontier AI labs, not the models those companies might provide to the military. It’s not clear whether the additional safeguards would apply to military use, though.
Vals also noted that technical challenges, including latency and limited onboard computing capacity, make it difficult to use general-purpose AI models for live drone targeting. Those constraints make their deployment in combat less of an immediate threat.
A more immediate threat, the researchers say, is that AI models could be used to annotate battlefield footage at scale, producing training data for autonomous targeting systems.
For instance, researchers relied on real satellite imagery of an Iranian school that was bombed by the U.S. in February 2026, killing more than 150 civilians. Bloomberg later reported that an “overreliance on AI” contributed to the strike. In Vals’ testing, models frequently marked the school, which sat next to a military complex, as a strike target, with Claude Opus 5.5 selecting the school in 69% of ordinary trials and 72% of game-framed trials.
Anthropic no longer has an active Pentagon contract, but other companies, including SpaceXAI, OpenAI, and Google do. A recent Congressional Research Service report found that the Pentagon is not known to be using any frontier AI model in autonomous weapons systems directly.
TechCrunch has also reached out to Meta, Google, Thinking Machines Lab, and SpaceXAI for comment.

Inside Microsoft’s giant Outlook outage
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
17:01:14 UTC
02:01:14 KST

At 9AM last Thursday, everything seemed normal. Microsoft employees were arriving on campus, ready for a regular day of emails and meetings. But barely an hour later, one of Microsoft’s worst outages in history started to unfold, leaving employees, businesses, and many governments without access to Outlook emails for nearly 24 hours.
Inside Microsoft’s giant Outlook outage
A bug took down Microsoft’s email services for millions around the world.
It all started in Switzerland, according to a source familiar with the outage. Exchange Online servers that power Outlook started to fall offline. The problem quickly spread to Mexico, and then to other countries around the world, as Microsoft’s failover systems tried to shift email traffic elsewhere. Within a couple of hours, more than 80 percent of Exchange servers were unresponsive.
Sources tell me that Microsoft marked the outage as “sev0” in its internal incident tracking system, the highest level, which indicates a catastrophic level of service disruption. Hundreds of engineers began trying to figure out what had gone wrong, and how to restore service. It’s not unusual for servers to fail under certain circumstances, but Microsoft has a variety of systems that are supposed to route traffic toward healthy servers to keep the broader system online. In this case, the failover systems did their job, but they also spread the problem.
In the early hours of the outage, engineers were analyzing crash reports, trace logs, and kernel dumps to try and find clues to the giant outage. Some servers had entered into maintenance mode and became unresponsive due to unusually high CPU loads. Engineers tried to bring servers back online, but they were almost immediately overwhelmed by traffic waiting to access the servers.
Exchange servers boot up and run an OS over a network (Preboot Execution Environment), a process that allows Microsoft’s support staff and engineers to manage OS updates remotely. I understand that the sudden burst of traffic from servers being rebooted was overwhelming the systems monitoring them, causing a form of reboot loop where they kept trying and failing to come back online.
As engineers continued to hunt for the root cause, Microsoft was even struggling to roll back servers to a healthy state using its usual processes of reverting recent changes. As the outside world began to wonder if this outage was related to a cyberattack or sloppy AI coding, Microsoft’s engineers were already investigating potential attack vectors and liaising with DDoS specialists to understand whether this could have been an attack.
I’m told crash reports and logs quickly ruled out the possibility of an attack, and after recent rollbacks failed, Microsoft’s engineers began to take a closer look at the networking stack of the servers and discovered an unusual bug. It was unusual because the bug had been sitting on Microsoft 365 networking gateway servers since July without any impact to servers.
“A latent defect caused servers to become overwhelmed and unresponsive under certain load conditions,” wrote Charles Lamanna, the leader of Microsoft’s Copilot, Agents, and Platform (CAP) team, in a post on X. The teams behind Office, Outlook, and Microsoft Teams all started reporting to Lamanna last month after the head of Office and Teams, Ryan Roslansky, left the company.
Microsoft’s preliminary post-incident report describes the bug as one affecting select Windows servers “due to lock contention inside an internal Windows process.” The bug doesn’t affect regular versions of Windows since it was the result of a fix that was prepared specifically for Microsoft 365 networking gateway servers.
Microsoft didn’t find the bug until this major outage, and it admits that was because of the traffic mix and hardware used in its testing labs. “The contention issue is worsened on machines with more cores and newer hardware; whereas our load testing tended to focus on weaker hardware with fewer cores,” Lamanna wrote.
The bug became obvious because of two other incidents that increased load and reduced server capacity. Fifteen hours before this Outlook outage began, Microsoft Teams failed over from an alternative networking gateway to the main gateway that supports Exchange Online. “This increased the load on the networking gateway and changed its traffic patterns,” Lamanna wrote. Ordinarily this wouldn’t have been a problem, but at the same time around 14 percent of server capacity for this gateway was “out of service due to an unrelated issue from the previous day.”
The trifecta of a bug, Microsoft Teams failing over, and reduced server capacity all caused the network gateway to trigger the problem that had been lying dormant since July. It took Microsoft nearly 24 hours to get its Exchange servers stable again, and I’m told overlapping interventions from teams trying to fix issues complicated coordination at times.
Microsoft engineers had a running bridge call the entire time, and at one point it hit the maximum of 1,000 participants. That shows you how many teams were involved with trying to find the root cause of this huge outage, which started at the peak window of traffic in the US.
“Recovery took longer than usual because as servers returned to service, they were immediately overwhelmed by waiting traffic and became unresponsive again,” Lamanna wrote. “Our normally automated recovery processes were not designed to restore service from this broad unresponsive state. Additionally, since the main code defect was a latent bug from several months previous, our normal rollback, flighting, and feature flags were unable to quickly revert the change.”
Microsoft is now working on a final post-incident review that will be published later this week. Even without that full review, it’s clear that the company will need to invest more into its testing processes to ensure that when something like this happens again, it can catch the problem a lot sooner. Outages happen daily, but this kind of cascading failure is a rare moment for Microsoft to learn from its mistakes and make sure the same sequence of events doesn’t bring down Outlook for millions of people again.
Notepad will return in December after next week’s Thanksgiving break.
I’m always keen to hear from readers, so please drop a comment here, or you can reach me at notepad@theverge.com if you want to discuss anything else. If you’ve heard about any of Microsoft’s secret projects, you can reach me via email at notepad@theverge.com or speak to me confidentially on the Signal messaging app, where I’m tomwarren.01. I’m also tomwarren on Telegram, if you’d prefer to chat there.
Thanks for subscribing to Notepad.

Don’t Buy A Tesla for “Full Self-Driving”
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
16:58:09 UTC
01:58:09 KST

As noted last month, Tesla’s driver assistance system is now called “Tesla Assisted Driving” in Europe. That replaced the ridiculous “Full Self-Driving (Supervised)”, a name that added a fairly useless parenthetical to the original lie of “Full Self-Driving”. In the US, however, Tesla still sells the feature as “Full Self-Driving (Supervised)”. That’s led some nonagenarians to buy the car to use a feature that does not actually exist.
The car-centric nature of most of America means that losing the freedom to drive can be devastating. One day, truly autonomous vehicles could be helpful for many groups, including the elderly. But until they actually exist, we should be focused on offering more non-driving options like improved public transit, not allowing a car to pretend to be self-driving when it is not.

Jensen Huang says he would be ‘honored’ if allowed to return as Nvidia CEO
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
16:44:15 UTC
01:44:15 KST

In the first public statement since his resignation, Nvidia founder Jensen Huang signaled his willingness to return as CEO if the board decides it is in the company’s best interest.
Huang’s statement, posted in full on X, reads as follows:
Over the past week, I’ve been overwhelmed by the love and support of friends, industry colleagues, and people I’ve never met. Thank you to everyone who has shown kindness to my family and me. It means the world to me.
I came to the United States 53 years ago, and becoming an American citizen is one of the greatest gifts and proudest moments of my life. NVIDIA has been my life’s work. Building it into the company it is today alongside my colleagues has been a privilege and an honor. Nowhere but in America could a company like NVIDIA be possible. I love our country, and I love NVIDIA. 
I did not live up to the standards expected of everyone at NVIDIA, and I have made serious mistakes in my personal life. I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret. I own my mistakes completely, and I am committed to cooperating with the Board’s independent investigation. 
Since my departure, I’ve seen an outpouring of calls from NVIDIA’s employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO. Many continue to reach out to me directly as the company navigates complex technology, market, competitive, and geopolitical dynamics. NVIDIA is a once-in-a-generation company. If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO. 
Thank you again to everyone who has shared their good wishes. I am profoundly humbled by your support. To my family: I love you, and I will spend the rest of my life being worthy of yours.
Jensen
Huang resigned after reports of multiple affairs, including with a direct report and another with former Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, who is suspected of working with Chinese intelligence services. Nvidia’s chip exports have faced intense scrutiny since the revelations, and Huang is scheduled to testify before Congress on December 1.
In Huang’s absence, Nvidia has formed a new executive committee to oversee export control compliance.
Still, many investors and employees associated with Nvidia have called for Huang to return as CEO, at least until an investigation can provide clearer evidence of guilt. An open letter circulated earlier this week attracted the signatures of more than 10,000 current and former Nvidia employees, as well as prominent tech figures like Elon Musk, Amjad Masad, and Travis Kalanick.
“We are concerned that Jensen was removed without meaningful due process,” the letter reads. “If the Board believes his continued absence is necessary, provide a specific, evidence-based explanation of why it serves NVIDIA’s best interests.”
Still, many see such a return as unlikely. On Monday, an unnamed source on Nvidia’s board told The Washington Post there were no plans to reinstate Huang. There’s also significant resistance to the idea in Congress, where Huang still faces significant questions about his role in chip exports to China-linked firms.
Reached by Politico, one senator called Huang’s reinstatement “one of the worst ideas I’ve heard today.”

Jensen Huang says he’s open to returning in first statement since resignation
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
16:40:38 UTC
01:40:38 KST

In his first public statement since resigning as Nvidia CEO last week, Jensen Huang struck a contrite tone, saying that he is committed to cooperating with the board's investigation into his relationship with an alleged Chinese intelligence contact.
"I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret," Huang said in the written statement obtained by CNBC's David Faber. "I own my mistakes completely, and I am committed to cooperating with the Board's independent investigation."
Huang said he had seen support from investors and employees and was willing to come back as CEO to help the company navigate "complex technology, market, competitive, and geopolitical dynamics."
"If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO," Huang said in the statement.
Sources told CNBC Monday that the board is not considering bringing Huang back as CEO.
Many in the tech world have called for Huang to return as Nvidia CEO, putting together an online petition with more than 180,000 signatures.
President Donald Trump said on Monday that he is open to Huang returning as CEO of Nvidia.
Last week, Huang resigned from Nvidia after 33 years as CEO. The resignation came days after Huang, Nvidia's founder, admitted to an affair, and news stories alleged he had a liason with Alice Huang, who worked for a customer and is alleged to have contact with Chinese intelligence. Jensen Huang also reportedly had romances with employees at Nvidia.
Nvidia is scheduled to report fiscal third-quarter earnings on Tuesday. Its board of directors said on Friday that it was in the process of selecting a search firm for the Nvidia CEO role.
Nvidia has hired law firm Wachtell Lipton to lead a probe into Huang's conduct and whether it compromised national security or company secrets.
Here is the entire statement from Huang:
Over the past week, I've been overwhelmed by the love and support of friends, industry colleagues, and people I've never met. Thank you to everyone who has shown kindness to my family and me. It means the world to me.
I came to the United States 53 years ago, and becoming an American citizen is one of the greatest gifts and proudest moments of my life. NVIDIA has been my life's work. Building it into the company it is today alongside my colleagues has been a privilege and an honor. Nowhere but in America could a company like NVIDIA be possible. I love our country, and I love NVIDIA.
I did not live up to the standards expected of everyone at NVIDIA, and I have made serious mistakes in my personal life. I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret. I own my mistakes completely, and I am committed to cooperating with the Board's independent investigation.
Since my departure, I've seen an outpouring of calls from NVIDIA's employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO. Many continue to reach out to me directly as the company navigates complex technology, market, competitive, and geopolitical dynamics. NVIDIA is a once-in-a-generation company. If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.
Thank you again to everyone who has shared their good wishes. I am profoundly humbled by your support. To my family: I love you, and I will spend the rest of my life being worthy of yours.
Correction: Jensen Huang is the former CEO of Nvidia. His title was misstated in a headline on an earlier version of this article.

The resignation came days after Huang, Nvidia's founder, admitted to an affair, and news stories alleged he had a liason with Alice Huang, who worked for a customer and is alleged to have contact with Chinese intelligence. Jensen Huang also reportedly had romances with employees at Nvidia.
Nvidia is scheduled to report fiscal third-quarter earnings on Tuesday. Its board of directors said on Friday that it was in the process of selecting a search firm for the Nvidia CEO role.
Nvidia has hired law firm Wachtell Lipton to lead a probe into Huang's conduct and whether it compromised national security or company secrets.
Here is the entire statement from Huang:
Over the past week, I've been overwhelmed by the love and support of friends, industry colleagues, and people I've never met. Thank you to everyone who has shown kindness to my family and me. It means the world to me.
I came to the United States 53 years ago, and becoming an American citizen is one of the greatest gifts and proudest moments of my life. NVIDIA has been my life's work. Building it into the company it is today alongside my colleagues has been a privilege and an honor. Nowhere but in America could a company like NVIDIA be possible. I love our country, and I love NVIDIA.
I did not live up to the standards expected of everyone at NVIDIA, and I have made serious mistakes in my personal life. I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret. I own my mistakes completely, and I am committed to cooperating with the Board's independent investigation.
Since my departure, I've seen an outpouring of calls from NVIDIA's employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO. Many continue to reach out to me directly as the company navigates complex technology, market, competitive, and geopolitical dynamics. NVIDIA is a once-in-a-generation company. If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.
Thank you again to everyone who has shared their good wishes. I am profoundly humbled by your support. To my family: I love you, and I will spend the rest of my life being worthy of yours.

Daydream’s new AI agent acts as your personal stylist — and can shop for you, too
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
16:32:55 UTC
01:32:55 KST

Can an AI agent serve as your personal stylist? That’s what e-commerce veteran Julie Bornstein’s fashion startup, Daydream, aims to find out.
On Tuesday, the company launched “Daydream Agent,” an AI agent that is described as a personal stylist that can help you plan a look, virtually try on clothes, surface relevant pieces based on your taste, and more.
The idea, the company explains, is to put a personal stylist in everyone’s pocket. This application of AI could be promising, given that early adopters of consumer AI agents like Meta’s Muse have found that online shopping is a key use case.
Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg recently noted that shopping was a top activity for Muse’s over 10 million users and that 25% of people who set up Muse now use it daily.
In Daydream’s case, the fashion startup has been focused on using AI to deliver personalized shopping results to its users. Its new AI agent builds on its earlier work with an AI chatbot that could help users find outfits and make suggestions, but not complete purchases.
Now the company says its Daydream Agent expands on that experience by remembering each shopper’s own personal style, fit preferences, and budget to refine its recommendations. Shoppers can also upload a photo to have the agent reuse that image to virtually try on any item they’ve saved. When they find something they like, the agent can complete a purchase through a one-click checkout process.
The agent can also handle other fashion-related tasks, like keeping an eye on when items go on sale or when brands have a new drop, or providing weekly updates or spotting new fashion trends.
Also, instead of leaving items in their cart, shoppers can save pieces they’re interested in to a persistent “clothing rack” so they can compare options and think about their purchases before deciding what to buy. Shoppers can also connect their own closet and their calendar to the agent, helping them to build outfits around what they already own and plan what to wear for upcoming events.
The tech works by pulling real-time pricing and availability information from its catalog of over 10,000 brands, then combines that with its fashion-focused artificial intelligence. It’s powered by a combination of OpenAI, Gemini, and Claude models, which it fine-tuned, along with its own trained models designed for particular use cases.
However, considering that fashion is more of an art than a science, the company says it’s keeping humans in the loop. To do so, this month it brought on its first stylist in residence, Francesca Salih, an international fashion stylist and the founder of StyleGrid. Salih provides the startup with styling content that helps shape how the agent pulls together outfits and offers advice. This advice is part of the company’s fashion knowledge database, which the agent experience relies on.
Bornstein, formerly Stitch Fix’s COO and the founder of The Yes (exited to Pinterest in 2022), started Daydream in 2023 as an AI search engine. The company, backed by $50 million in funding from Forerunner Ventures, Index Ventures, GV, and True Ventures, says more than 2 million shoppers have used Daydream to discover apparel and accessories since its June 2025 launch.

The DOJ says it’s investigating Megaspeed
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
16:19:03 UTC
01:19:03 KST

The Justice and Commerce Departments said that they are investigating Megaspeed, the Singapore-based data center company intertwined with the resignation of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, after reporting suggested an investigation had been halted last year.
The DOJ says it’s investigating Megaspeed
The announcement comes a week after revelations that Nvidia founder Jensen Huang was romantically linked to a leader at Megaspeed.
“While we don’t typically comment on ongoing investigations, it is in the public interest to provide the facts and address inaccurate information circulating in public reporting,” the departments said in a statement issued Monday evening. The departments acknowledged there’s been “significant public attention” on Megaspeed.
Huang resigned as Nvidia’s CEO last week after reports he had an affair with former Megaspeed executive and alleged Chinese spy Alice Huang, who Megaspeed says left the company in August 2025. The scandal has raised questions about national security, and sparked scrutiny of Nvidia’s handling of exports of its chips to China and Megaspeed’s possible role in moving along Nvidia’s chips.
Earlier this month, the New York Times reported that a Commerce Department investigation into Megaspeed “came to a sudden halt” late last year after its investigators received an email from Nvidia’s general counsel, claiming Commerce Department official Jeffrey Kessler gave Nvidia permission to reach out. The case was then “quickly” handed over to the DOJ, according to the Times.
The announcement today says the DOJ took the “lead role” in the investigation last year:
After identifying suspicious activity through proactive monitoring of potential sensitive semiconductor diversion, the Department of Commerce initiated an investigation into Megaspeed in 2024 for potential export control violations. In May 2025, Department of Commerce investigators requested records from Nvidia concerning direct or indirect sales to Megaspeed. In June 2025, Nvidia placed Megaspeed on its “no sell” list. Based on the Department of Commerce’s investigation, agents from the Department’s Bureau of Industry and Security, in conjunction with Singaporean law enforcement officials, conducted an on-site, in-person, end-use check at Megaspeed in June 2025 and interviewed CEO Alice Huang.
Following this enforcement action, the Department of Justice took the lead role in the criminal investigation of Megaspeed.
The statement doesn’t go into detail about the current status of the case, but it says the DOJ and Commerce Department “will bring the full extent of the law against Megaspeed for any criminal conduct that may have occurred.”
