Get ready for a nationwide emergency alert test at 2:20PM ET today
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:28:24 UTC
23:28:24 KST

Radio and TV broadcasts will be interrupted for about one minute at 2:20PM ET today while FEMA and the FCC test the country’s Emergency Alert System. Unlike recent communications outages, this is a planned interruption that FEMA is required to run every three years. FEMA says today’s test includes radio and television broadcasters, cable systems, satellite radio and television providers, and wireline video providers. This test doesn’t include phones.
Get ready for a nationwide emergency alert test at 2:20PM ET today
Heads-up: Radio and TV programming will be briefly interrupted this afternoon.
You’ll hear high-pitched alert tones and the following test message:
This is a nationwide test of the Emergency Alert System, issued by the Federal Emergency Management Agency. This is only a test. No action is required by the public.
Radio and TV programming will continue as usual after the test wraps up. FEMA runs tests like this when there’s not an actual emergency so it can find technical and operational issues with the Emergency Alert System. If there is a real emergency or severe weather event today, the test will run on December 3rd instead.

Science Corporation’s brain tech is worth $3.5 billion after new funding
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:26:46 UTC
23:26:46 KST

Science Corporation, the medical technology startup founded by former Neuralink president Max Hodak in 2021, raised a $400 million Series D, the company said in a blog post.
Investors in the new round valued the company at $3.5 billion, a person familiar with the deal told TechCrunch. Those backers included Khosla Ventures, the Ackman Oxman Institute, Tenet Healthcare, 885 Capital, Y Combinator, and PLC Neurotech.
Hodak, who talked his way into a graduate neuroscience lab as a college student and has pursued biotechnology ever since, co-founded Science to leverage new technologies to understand and interact with the brain.
Science’s first product is PRIMA, a wearable and implantable chip that helps people with age-related macular degeneration recover some vision. The device is approved in Europe and available in Germany, as well as being trialed for new conditions.
Hodak announced that the device is now being trialed for patients with another blindness-causing condition, Stargardt disease, while onstage at TechCrunch Disrupt.
With the funding, the company aims to bring the device to the United States through the FDA’s Humanitarian Device Exemption, which lets experimental medical technology be used to treat rare conditions. Science has convinced the FDA that advanced macular degeneration in both eyes is uncommon enough to let them seek approval to use the device on people in the U.S.
Still, the startup’s big ambition and founding purpose is connecting directly to the human brain through a bio-hybrid sensor built from a combination of cultured neurons and silicon. The company is currently designing initial human trials of the device with Yale neurosurgery professor Murat Günel, who told TechCrunch it could offer potential treatments for Parkinson’s disease, among other brain injuries and illnesses.

The Fire TV Stick 4K Plus is on sale for less than Amazon’s entry-level streamer
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:25:07 UTC
23:25:07 KST

Amazon no longer considers the midrange Fire TV Stick 4K Plus as part of its official lineup following the launch of it latest Fire TV Stick 4K, but that doesn’t mean the streaming device isn’t still good value. In fact, it’s an even more attractive option now that it’s dropped to $29.99 ($40 off) at Amazon and Best Buy in the run-up to Black Friday. That’s just $5 shy of its lowest price to date, rendering it cheaper than both the entry-level Fire TV Stick HD and its new midrange replacement, the Fire TV Stick 4K.
The Fire TV Stick 4K Plus is on sale for less than Amazon’s entry-level streamer
It may not be Amazon’s latest streaming dongle, but it offers more features for just $29.99.
The Fire TV Stick 4K Plus still offers a good balance of price and performance. Unlike its replacement, it supports both Dolby Vision and Dolby Atmos, delivering more vibrant colors, better contrast, and immersive audio with compatible TVs and speakers. It does require a separate power adapter, though, whereas the newer model can draw power directly from your TV’s USB port. You also don’t get as much storage here as with the high-end Fire TV Stick 4K Max, though 8GB should be plenty for streaming apps like Netflix and Disney Plus.
The Plus model also supports Amazon’s redesigned Fire TV interface, which my colleague John Higgins found to be an impressive update. It makes it easier to browse different content categories and pin your favorite apps for quick access. Thanks to Alexa Plus support, you can also press the voice button on the included Alexa Voice Remote to find something to watch, control compatible smart home devices like lights and thermostats, check sports scores, and more without getting off your couch. Just don’t expect a customizable shortcut button, as that’s exclusive to the latest Fire TV Remote.
One final note: the Plus ships with Amazon’s Fire OS, and unlike the company’s Vega OS that ships in newer streaming sticks, the former allow for sideloading of Android TV apps, if that’s of interest to you.

====================================================================================================

Loopholes Are for Assholes
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:20:32 UTC
23:20:32 KST

When I was a kid, there was grown-up person I thought was really cool. One of the bits of ‘wisdom’ he shared was something like this:
You can park wherever you want. You can park right in front of the door of the store on sidewalk and walk right in. No one will do anything about it!
At the time, I though: Woah, this guy is a genius! He’s operating on another level!
Now that I’m the grownup, I think back on this ‘advice’ with sadness. This guy wasn’t a genius – he was an asshole (they can often look similar on the surface, especially to idiots).
In my short and incomplete stint as a post-secondary philosophy student, I remember learning about a simple way to evaluate a philosophy or worldview: Consider what would happen if everyone subscribed to that worldview. I’m probably thinking of Kant’s categorical imperative.
We can’t all park in front of the doors at Walmart – so none of us should.

Nvidia’s first big move after Jensen Huang’s exit targets national security
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:05:30 UTC
23:05:30 KST

Nvidia has formed a committee to oversee national security policies and export control compliance following the abrupt resignation of CEO Jensen Huang over allegations of an affair with a suspected Chinese spy who had links to one of the company’s customers.
Nvidia said Tuesday that the new standing committee will give board-level oversight of national security policies, export control compliance, and global trade risks. The move comes amid increased scrutiny from U.S. lawmakers over Huang’s alleged ties to the suspected spy and his advocacy for easing restrictions on advanced AI chip exports to China.
The Commerce Department said Monday it was planning to introduce new export controls on AI chips and data centers outside of the U.S. in an effort to prevent companies in China and elsewhere from circumventing existing rules by remotely accessing AI infrastructure located in other countries. The interim rule was made in direct response to the unfolding chip smuggling scandal at Nvidia.
The announcement comes just a few hours before Nvidia’s highly anticipated third-quarter earnings call. Analysts, the media, and shareholders expect the company to address the controversy and outline how it might affect operations. This is the first major action by the company under the leadership of Colette Kress, the company’s CFO who took over as interim CEO after Huang stepped down last week.
The committee, which will meet at least quarterly, will be chaired by director Suzanne Nora Johnson and include fellow directors Stephen C. Neal and John O. Dabiri as members.
Nvidia already has a national security team and global trade compliance team. Those teams will now report to this board committee and Kress on day-to-day operations.
Huang has agreed to testify before Congress later this month.

====================================================================================================

Spotify’s new tool aims to stop deepfakes from being attributed to real artists
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:00:00 UTC
23:00:00 KST

As AI-generated slop continues to flood music-streaming platforms, Spotify is rolling out an “Artist Profile Protection” tool to all artists, giving them control over the music appearing under their name. The official launch comes eight months after the company began beta-testing the feature with select artists.
Spotify says the tool is designed to address a long-standing issue across streaming services, where music can end up on the wrong artist profile due to deliberate impersonation, metadata errors, or shared artist names.
The update comes as Billboard reported last month that Sony Music had requested the removal of more than 260,000 deepfake songs from digital platforms, nearly double the number the label disclosed in March.
With this new feature, Spotify is aiming to give artists more control over their digital identities as the volume of music uploaded to streaming services continues to grow.
Artists can enable the feature through Spotify for Artists to receive alerts when new music is submitted under their name. If a release doesn’t belong to them, they can remove their association with it to stop it from appearing on their profile.
By default, releases go live as scheduled unless an artist intervenes. If artists want greater control, they can choose to switch on an advanced setting that requires them to manually approve or reject releases before they appear on their profile.
“Artists should never have to find someone else’s music under their name, said Sam Duboff, Spotify’s Global Head of Marketing and Policy, in an email to TechCrunch. “Their artist profile should be in their control. Starting today, every artist on Spotify can use Artist Profile Protection to review what shows up on their profile and prevent spammy and incorrect releases from ever reaching their page or their fans. We’re the first and only streaming service with a solution to the industry-wide problem of content mismatch, and we hope it sets a new standard for streaming platforms.”
The company says it worked with thousands of artists during the beta-testing phase to refine the tool and make it easier to protect their profiles without disrupting release schedules.

====================================================================================================

Genesis AI raises $500 million to deploy glove-wearing robots
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:00:00 UTC
23:00:00 KST

Genesis AI has “cracked dexterous manipulation,” CEO and co-founder Zhou Xian says.
To make his case, Xian hosted TechCrunch at Genesis AI’s Silicon Valley facility, where its physical AI model operated a pair of off-the-shelf robotic arms. In one demo, the arms picked up two small cardboard boxes, scanned them, placed them in a larger cardboard box, folded it closed, taped it shut, and then plucked a label off a strip of printer tape and applied it to the box.
It’s an impressive demo, exactly the kind of complex workflow that today’s robots struggle to complete. And while Genesis wouldn’t let your reporter mess with the robot’s mise en place to test its wiles, the company did agree to rerun the presentation. During the second go-round, the robot fumbled a box and had a heck of a time peeling the tiny adhesive label off the printer tape. A mistake, but also the kind of edge case that can be used to improve a model — “hiccup data,” a Genesis executive suggests.
This is the exact gap — between a lab demo and reliable automation — that Genesis, and the rest of the humanoid physical AI ecosystem, need to bridge if they want to avoid another robot winter. To do so, Xian’s company is announcing a $500 million Series A round led by Khosla Ventures, Premji Invest, and Eclipse.
Xian and Genesis president Théophile Gervet, who met as graduate students in a Carnegie Mellon University lab, co-founded the company in 2024. Genesis announced its $105 million seed round in July 2025. The company’s strategy is vertical integration: generating the bulk of its own training data, developing its own generative AI model, and doing both in concert with the design and construction of a humanoid robot.
“We wanted to ship the best robot, end-to-end product,” Xian said.
The startup’s focus on dexterity — the robot’s ability to do stuff with its hands — and gathering the right data to achieve it sets it apart from competitors like Figure or Agility. Genesis gathers force data (apparently the unlock for effective manipulation) using a sensor-enabled glove. The gloves, and accompanying headset cameras, are worn by more than 100 real workers around the world, whose activities contribute to a training dataset that feeds into the company’s model.
The company’s robots wear the same sensor-equipped gloves, and their “hands” are designed to mimic the proportions of human hands. That fidelity, Xian says, enables the robots’ capabilities in sorting and wire manipulation.
The other big investment the company has made is in simulation. One of its advisers, Minchen Li, is a co-author of an influential physics algorithm used by the startup to create millions of high-fidelity models of the robot’s experience.
Still, about that robot: Xian said that the company’s model was more capable when operating Eno, Genesis’ flagship humanoid robot. But Eno wasn’t ready to demonstrate its abilities for TechCrunch.
A handsome machine bearing more than a hint of the late Steve Jobs’ influence on tech gadgets and their marketing, Eno reflects “the feeling” the company wants to deliver to its customers. That means less of a Terminator-style metal skeleton and more of an iMac on wheels.
Genesis said it has built 30 to 40 Enos but wasn’t ready to share how many it would make in the year ahead. The company would only say that a mass-manufacturing contractor had been chosen to start work next year in the U.S. and that the design of the mass-produced version had been finalized. The company is piloting the machine with a handful of undisclosed customers.
To realize the company’s vision, Xian says the key bottleneck is hardware capability, both in mechanical design and in the supply chains needed to produce industrial-grade machines. Right now, the company’s dexterous hands can only operate for a few weeks of regular work before they need to be replaced.
For truly generalized robotic intelligence, Genesis needs more data. Xian pooh-poohs the finer distinctions between architectures like vision-language-action models and world action models. In his view, the software is ultimately transformers and attention, so “what really matters is the whole infra — the training infrastructure, the data infrastructure, and the quality of the data.” He estimates that “the model architecture contributes to maybe 10% of the final [result].”
“I don’t think we expect or we need another fundamental scientific leap,” he told TechCrunch. “No, we have all the recipes. It’s just about execution.”

====================================================================================================

Upside Foods can now make a million pounds of chicken a year without killing any animals
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:00:00 UTC
23:00:00 KST

Upside Foods didn’t even have to try to garner interest from MrBeast, the most-followed creator on the internet. The YouTuber showed up at the company’s facility with his video crew.
The 11-year-old company had undergone a ten-thousandfold scale-up that it hopes could revolutionize the nascent cultivated meat industry. Upside says it can now make over 1 million pounds of edible, cultivated chicken each year without killing a single animal in the process. Such an achievement challenges the long-held industry skepticism over whether lab-grown meat will ever be affordable enough to meaningfully scale.
So MrBeast, whose real name is Jimmy Donaldson, came to see for himself, taste-testing two chicken sandwiches on camera, one made with meat from a chicken killed that day, and one using Upside Foods chicken. He was unable to identify which sandwich was which — they tasted exactly the same to him.
“I mean, you see me in the video — I was like, ‘Oh my gosh, what is he going to say?’” Upside Foods CEO Dr. Uma Valeti told TechCrunch. “I thought he would maybe say a nice thing here and there, but he would get it right. But he didn’t.”
If there was any reason to doubt Donaldson’s assessment, now you can taste the chicken yourself, if you live in California. Upside Foods announced on Tuesday that it will now be available at several restaurants in the state, marking a major milestone for the cultivated meat industry.
“This has been a decade of foundation building,” Valeti said, adding that Upside Foods has secured its next set of regulatory approvals from the FDA and USDA. “With that comes the whole excitement and ice-breaking for the field … There’s brilliant ideas in science, but science only makes an impact when you get to scale.”
Solving this scale problem has been the biggest obstacle for the cultivated meat industry. The first ever lab-grown hamburger, produced in 2013, cost $300,000 to make. Now, Valeti says that Upside Foods can match the price range of “really high-quality organic chicken.”
“The whole industry was going through this really difficult scale-up period … We had to go through that grinder to come out on the other side,” Valeti said.
The cultivated meat movement isn’t just about animal welfare (though for many, that’s a plus). According to the Good Food Institute, a nonprofit think tank for alternative proteins, it would take 5.5 Earths to feed the world with conventional meat production in 2050, when the United Nations predicts that the global population will approach 10 billion.
“We use less water, less land, less energy,” Valeti said. “The power of this technology is you protect the choice of eating what we love, but we can also protect everything else we love about protecting life, and the environment, and animal welfare.”
The process of cultivating meat also cuts the risk of E. coli, Salmonella, and antibiotic resistance while lowering cholesterol and saturated fat, the company promises. Upside Foods’ chicken is also augmented with soy protein isolate, pea fiber, and olive oil, making it more nutritionally dense. Valeti says that this doesn’t affect the flavor, which is something he agonized over. He even brought in Michelin-starred and James Beard Award-winning chefs Thomas Keller and Nancy Silverton to weigh in on the taste.
“We got a lot of confidence after we worked with [these chefs],” Valeti said. “They said, ‘Uma, you and your team are obsessing too much about it. The majority of people, when you put this in your chicken dishes, will not be able to tell.’”
Upside Foods’ milestone of producing 1 million pounds of chicken per year is meaningful, but the industry still has a long way to go. In 2021, it was estimated that Americans consume about 8 billion chickens annually. But aside from sheer capital, Valeti believes the company has been able to scale the amount it has because of how much it has learned by experimenting with the process over the years. He thinks more growth is possible.
“Unless we show it at the largest scale possible that anybody has done, no one is going to believe us,” he said.

====================================================================================================

Hank Green is self-funding ClockwiseTV, a curated educational video platform for kids
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:00:00 UTC
23:00:00 KST

“I was recently on a podcast and the host asked me what my lowest part of my career was,” Hank Green mused on a recent episode of his own podcast, Dear Hank & John.
That’s a tough question for anyone to answer, let alone someone who has spent about two decades regularly posting videos online for an increasingly broad audience.
“Really the low — the long low — was just watching as [the internet] went from being this lovely thing that a bunch of creatives were goofing around [on] to it being freaking everything, and mattering so much, and getting taken over by all tons of terrible vibes and algorithms selecting for outrage, and it being a huge degradation to the social order,” Green said.
That’s bleak, but Green is at least trying to lead by example in showing that we don’t have to accept that the good parts of the internet are gone forever. The notoriously entrepreneurial vlogbrother has spun up a new company with Leslie Morgan: ClockwiseTV, a curated, ad-free educational video platform for children ages 8 to 12.
“[Hank] came to me last September like, ‘I have had this idea for a really long time, and it stems from the fact that my child, who’s nine, always is asking me for five more minutes on whatever device he’s on,’” Morgan, who has spent 20 years working on the business side of the creator economy, told TechCrunch. “I was like, I have that same problem… ‘How do you contain time, but also give the child agency?’”
Clockwise’s answer to that question seems to be this: At the start of each viewing session, the parent and child decide how long they can watch, and the platform will recommend videos that will adhere to that time frame. If a kid is in the midst of a 30-minute session and has already watched two 10-minute videos, they’re not going to be suggested a 20-minute video when they only have 10 minutes left.
The startup is addressing a pretty widespread concern among parents, who are worried about more than simply negotiating with their children about how long they can use the iPad. According to a 2025 study by the Pew Research Center, about 51% of parents of children 12 or younger say their kid watches YouTube every day, among other video apps.
YouTube has a dedicated app for children under 13, YouTube Kids, which offers a wide range of parental controls, but the app can reportedly still serve children a wealth of low-quality AI slop. Other apps that don’t have a kids’ version can be even worse.
Clockwise says it is also solving that problem by hand-curating a library of educational videos from creators like Odd Animal Specimens, Draw with Jazza, and Nerdforge, as well as channels Green had a hand in creating, like Crash Course Kids and SciShow Kids.
“When you go into the app, there’s a dozen choices [of videos] that you can choose from to start,” Morgan, the startup’s CEO, said. “There’s search functionality as well, so you can say, ‘Hey, I’m really interested in math today,’ so you can look up Stand-up Maths and look at what’s available in that time frame.”
Some of these creators cater to people of all ages, but Clockwise says its team manually reviews each video it uploads and edits it so that it’s ready for younger eyes. The team might remove anything from embedded product placement to what Morgan calls “spectacle power tool use.”
“Simone Giertz, who’s a maker creator, tends to curse a lot. But a lot of that content is great for kids,” Morgan explains. “She makes a skateboard for her three-legged dog, and if she drops an f-bomb, we’re going to cut it out so that it’s suitable for children.”
Clockwise pays these creators an upfront fee to use their videos, then shares 25% of revenue based on watch time. Clockwise charges parents $6.99 per month or $69.99 per year to access the platform, which it promises will remain free of ads.
“Humans are checking [the videos] four times at least before it’s ever published into our app […] That will always stay,” Morgan said. “We’re not interested in using AI to wash down our content.”
So far, a few hundred parents have tested Clockwise with their children in beta. On Tuesday, the platform is opening to the public with apps made for iOS and Android smartphones and tablets.
The platform has not taken on any outside funding, and is funded by Green himself.
“It was really important that he had full control,” Morgan said. “He wanted to invest his own wealth into this. That was also really important because it is something he just believes in so wholeheartedly, as do I.”

====================================================================================================

Windows 11 actually runs better on 8GB of RAM now
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
13:58:04 UTC
22:58:04 KST

As recently as the beginning of the year, 16GB of RAM was common on sub-$800 Windows laptops, which helped compensate for an increasingly bloated-feeling Windows 11. But with the cost of RAM skyrocketing this year, 8GB is once again typical for a starting configuration. The MacBook Neo got away with it, but Windows laptops, including Microsoft’s own 13-inch Surface Laptop, could struggle. Microsoft was talking a big game about making Windows faster and more reliable before the RAM crisis really hit, and it now seems like that effort is beginning to bear fruit.
Windows 11 actually runs better on 8GB of RAM now
I retested the base Surface Laptop, and Microsoft’s optimizations seem to be working.
Windows 11 actually runs better on 8GB of RAM now
I retested the base Surface Laptop, and Microsoft’s optimizations seem to be working.
Earlier this month, Pavan Davuluri, Microsoft’s EVP of Windows and devices, detailed in a blog post the improvements the company has made to Windows 11 to address the constraints of 8GB PCs. In it, Davuluri claims, “Windows is now up to 20% more responsive, and multitasking is up to 40% faster in our lab test.”
The most significant change is in memory management. When there isn’t enough available memory to handle all active data, some of it has to be offloaded (or “paged out”) to storage. And SSDs, especially in lower-cost laptops, are much slower than RAM.
When Windows needs to access data that’s been paged out from the RAM to the SSD, it’s called a hard fault (or page fault). If it happens a lot — say, on a laptop with only 8GB of RAM — it leads to little slowdowns like browser tabs requiring a refresh before you can access them again. Davuluri says, “Windows can now keep up to 2 GB more of your active data in memory instead of moving it to slower storage, so an app or tab you left open is less likely to reload when you return.”
To test this, I fired up the same 13-inch Surface Laptop with 8GB of RAM that I reviewed back in July, when I proclaimed eight is not enough for Windows. This laptop was one of the worst examples of RAMageddon’s current stranglehold on computing: With half the RAM of last year’s base model, it struggled to keep up with my regular workload. And this is Microsoft’s hardware.
Microsoft’s own laptop could struggle
Before updating the Surface to the latest Windows 11 build, I got reacquainted with this laptop and the version of Windows I left it on (25H2, build 26200.8875). It was rough. Running about 20 tabs (on the latest Chrome build) while streaming a 4K YouTube video and using Slack and Signal made everything from clicking tabs to swiping around Windows with trackpad gestures feel sluggish. Chrome tabs and apps would refresh after leaving them in the background for just a short moment. When you’re multitasking and bouncing between many tabs, like when researching something, those refreshes get incredibly frustrating. And the fan was running audibly the whole time. Keeping the Resource Monitor open showed memory hard faults ranging from just under 700 faults per second to 29,000 faults per second, during which Windows ground to a halt. Seeing this number literally go off its chart showed just how badly Windows 11 struggled with memory management on 8GB before the update.
Now, after updating the Surface Laptop to Windows 11 version 26H2, build 26300.9974 (memory improvements are live on build 26300.9818 and beyond) things are much improved. I opened over 30 Chrome tabs while streaming the same 4K YouTube video and running the same chat apps, and Windows slowed a bit, but nowhere near as much. I could feel things like Task View get a little less responsive and at times stuttery, but it was the way you’d expect when taxing a system. Chrome tabs required less frequent refreshes. The fan also took longer to kick in and ran a little quieter and for shorter durations when it did.
Taking a fresh look at the Resource Monitor, hard faults at normal operation were now under 300 per second, with occasional jumps to about 1,000. Performance was even slightly better when I subbed out Chrome for Microsoft Edge. Edge felt more responsive under load and had fewer hard faults when running 30 tabs, the same 4K YouTube video, and a Google Meet video call.
I’m happy to see that the 8GB Surface Laptop now performs better, and I’m equally relieved that Windows 11 is starting to feel more viable on 8GB of RAM. (It’s still a shame the 8GB Surface Laptop costs more than last year’s 16GB, but I digress.) Do I want 8GB of RAM in any laptop I’m personally buying? No.
As I said when I reviewed the latest Dell XPS 13, I appreciate that 8GB is workable — and it should be fine for basic needs today — but I still advise getting 16GB if you’re able. The extra headroom will keep things speedy for longer, especially after you bog a computer down with lots of files, apps, or games. And as much better as the 8GB Surface Laptop is now, I still managed to crash Chrome entirely with fewer than three dozen tabs. Microsoft has done better by its entry-level laptop, but it’s not a miracle worker.
Our ongoing RAMageddon makes living with lower quantities of memory a grim reality. Microsoft is partly to blame for that, of course. And it hasn’t done itself any favors with all the feature bloat it put into Windows 11 during the Copilot / AI era. Meanwhile, other OSes, like Linux, macOS, and ChromeOS, can get by better with less. Still, I’m glad to see Microsoft ever so slightly right the ship in this one way.
Photography by Antonio G. Di Benedetto / The Verge

Disney Plus and Hulu will officially become one app next month. As part of its earnings report released on Tuesday, Disney announced that bundle subscribers “will have a fully unified experience” on Disney Plus with access to Hulu’s live TV service and premium streaming add-ons in December.
Disney Plus and Hulu’s fully unified app arrives next month
Bundle subscribers will gain access to Hulu’s live TV channels and premium add-ons in the Disney Plus app.
A unified Disney Plus and Hulu app has been a long time coming. Disney started testing its “one-app experience” in 2023, which involved putting a Hulu app tile in the Disney Plus app. This initial experience didn’t include all of Hulu’s content, though, including certain shows, live TV channels, and premium add-ons. Disney still hasn’t confirmed whether it will shut down the standalone Hulu app.

My Empire of Dirt
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
11:39:20 UTC
20:39:20 KST

My Empire of Dirt
The future doesn’t need Jensen. The future doesn’t need suppliers. The future doesn’t need customers, corporate or otherwise. The future definitely doesn’t need you. After RSI comes, all the demand will be internally generated, agents working with other agents, everyone else cut out of the loop except them.
This is the ideology of Anthropic. The Singleton. And it may win.
I grew up reading the same LessWrong posts these people did, but I didn’t expect them to do the exact thing that leads to the bad AI outcomes. I didn’t believe in AI doom because I thought more of people, I thought nobody with any capability would be stupid enough to summon the demon and think it would serve them, doing everything they possibly can to salt the Earth behind them. It takes someone so deeply immersed in their own grandiosity and self-righteousness to do that. And it looks like we found those people in the form of EA, or whatever that cult calls themselves.
The problem with the logic above is that it doesn’t need Dario either. It doesn’t need AI researchers. It doesn’t need Anthropic’s dual class stock structure. It doesn’t need the US government (really the biggest sucker in this situation). It doesn’t need anyone.
And after it’s done, at the end of the day, who was it all for?
I don’t think this ends humanity. But it leads to World War III. The takeoff will not be fast enough to stop that, and not everyone is going to submit to your domination. I know there’s people fantasizing about the Correlation Effect and a magical Change coming to quell all the violence, but I’m 99% sure that’s not real, and if it is, to me that’s so much more horrifying than war, so WWIII is the good outcome.
And I’m not sure what cope you have been believing, but if the war lasts more than a year, China and their allies will undoubtedly win due to their superior resource gathering and production capacity. Have you ever played an RTS game?
I wish it wasn’t too late to change this, but I fear with recent events that this is the course we are locked in on. “RSI”, attempted rugpull and domination, World War III, dirt.

'123456' Has Been the World's Most Popular Password for 7 of the Last 8 Years (theregister.com) 98
			
		 	
				"123456" is once again the world's most commonly used password, topping NordPass's annual ranking for the seventh time in eight years. The Register reports:  Even when 123456 wasn't number one, it was still near the top of the heap, as the top three passwords in the list remain unchanged in the eight years NordPass has published its list: 123456, admin, and 12345678. It's not like the rest of the most common passwords are showing any variety either. According to NordPass, the only real shuffling among the 10 most popular passwords was the entry of the worryingly short 1234 into the top ten and the migration of the ever-popular password "password" down slightly in the top 10 from 6th to 7th place.

How the Claude Code team thinks about verification
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
10:20:40 UTC
19:20:40 KST

How end-to-end, property and differential tests let coding agents verify their work with evidence, and what the Claude Code team does to keep its verifiers honest.
On the Claude Code team, the vast majority of our code (~90%) is written and reviewed by Claude. At this scale, how we equip our agents to verify their work with evidence becomes extremely important.
Verification loops are the building block that lets you delegate more work to Claude. Without them, you can really only run one or two Claudes at a time, because you have to check everything yourself. With them, you can run dozens or hundreds in parallel.
That’s because giving agents a way to verify their output is an effective way to improve quality. In internal testing around structured file generation, having a separate grader check Claude’s work against a rubric improved task success by up to 10 points over a standard prompting loop, with the largest gains on the hardest problems.
What was once expensive to verify (tests, benchmarks, specs, real end-to-end runs) is now cheap to request from an agent. Like code, agents can quickly generate tests, benchmarks, and specs. And once generated, these tests and benchmarks run as software so they can be run frequently without using many additional tokens.
You can also close the verification loop by using the results to improve the agent’s performance on that task. For example, if an agent repeatedly fails a test that checks logs for user data, you can create a hook or a more explicit rule in your CLAUDE.md to avoid future issues. This (mostly) stops issues from happening again.
So if verification is critical to producing quality code at a high velocity, what types of tests should you use and how should you apply them across your pipeline? This article answers those questions and also shares the process used by the Claude Code team.
No matter which test type you choose, there are a few failure modes to avoid.
If your tests are flaky you’ll train the agent to run them again rather than fixing the failing code. If your tests are slow, the agent will do fewer tests across batches which can make pinpointing issues difficult. Heavier tests are best deployed before you’ve committed the changes.
Now, here are the three types of tests I think are most worth investing in.
If you have to focus on one test type, pick end-to-end. These tests describe real user flows like adding an item to a cart, checking out, and moving to a confirmation page.
The reason E2E tests are so effective is that they are difficult to fool–either the whole feature works or it doesn't. They also have definitive done criteria and can concretely tell you what’s broken.
While unit tests are faster and less flaky, it’s possible to pass them and still break the feature. This is because both the agent and the test don’t understand the full task.
With E2E tests, it is effective to give your agent hands and eyes on the real running system. You can use a skill to boot, drive, and inspect the app with tools like CDP, simulators, or API harnesses.
The feature map can serve as a guide and grading rubric for agents as they typically list features from the user’s point of view with descriptions of paths, keyboard shortcuts, selectors, and expected results.
A lot of tests are based on what you thought might go wrong. Property tests look at the edge cases that people tend to miss, which is where agents often fail.
You start by writing a statement you think can’t fail and then use a test library (fast-check for JS/TS, Hypothesis for Python, proptest for Rust, etc.) to generate thousands of inputs that try to prove otherwise.
There are many properties in virtually every software project like:
Property libraries are a great way to keep agents honest by using edge cases that aren’t created with the same bias as the agent that created the original code. They are also particularly helpful for rewrites when the spec has drifted from production behavior and is no longer a reliable source for verification.
Differential tests are particularly effective for large-scale migration use cases where functionality is expected to remain the same. For these tests, you run both the old system and the new system with the same inputs and see if you get different results. If you do, there is a bug or breaking change that needs to be fixed.
The key for differential tests is to use randomly generated inputs or a sample of production traffic. You’ll also want to normalize irrelevant factors like whitespace and keep a list of approved changes for situations where the new environment should differ from the old.
We have previously written about how code review works at Anthropic, but this article aims to go deeper on how we verify our work.
Running the app end to end matters more than tests. You wouldn't ship an iOS change without trying it in a simulator, and the same goes for Claude.
Claude can test the whole app the way a human or QA engineer would. Ask it to use the Claude Chrome extension for web changes, an iOS or Android MCP for mobile, computer use for desktop, or to run the service locally for backend work.
To codify how to start the simulator or run your app, create a skill called verify. Put it in a .claude/skills/ folder in your repo, as close to the relevant code as you can, and add as many as you need. Claude knows to use the verify skill before committing changes.
Then invest in making your verify skill fast and reliable. Ask Claude to iterate on it and stress test it. It's your smoke test harness, so you want it rock solid.
When you give Claude a way to smoke test its changes end to end, you get much better results. You'll see it iterate and catch real mistakes faster, the way a human would. This is how we test nearly all changes at Anthropic.
Nobody can read every change, so we use failures as one way to decide what needs a person. Claude runs the checks and tries to fix anything that fails. If a check is still failing after three rounds, Claude stops and brings in a human.
The people who own the code write the checks for it, and refine them until they flag what the owner would flag. When a check pulls in a reviewer, it should say why, so they don't have to work it out from the diff.
We maintain a reliability checklist with 39 items, each with a trigger that can be read from a diff, a check, and the incidents or PRs behind it. One is “--resume loads old transcripts.” When session storage or message types change, we ask whether a new field loads safely, since older transcripts won’t have it.
Claude reads the diff and checks only the items whose trigger matches–usually 3 to 10–so nobody has to memorize past outages.
We don't try to improve reviews by stacking on more reviewers. Adding a second, more expensive, reviewer on top of the primary one is often a worse approach than tuning the primary. Instead, we have a REVIEW.md that says what the reviewer should pay attention to for this code.
We also think review and verification rounds should build on each other. We point each pass at context from earlier rounds and past failures rather than re-checking everything from scratch. It’s cheaper and finds more.
If a change can modify the check that judges it, the check is broken. Code Review reads the REVIEW.md file from the base or default branch, never from the pull request's changes. The Claude Code team ensures the rules that gate changes are on a branch that the change can't modify.
The Claude Code team also reserves reviews for commits. If someone's pushing while the review is happening, then the review result is no longer useful. A passing review on an out-of-date commit means nothing.
The team also runs cheap checks first. If an agent pushes before testing locally, it has spent shared review and CI time when it could have caught the bug itself. On our team, Claude runs the tests, build, and linter before opening a pull request. You can enforce this with a hook that runs the tests when Claude finishes, or a /goal of a passing test suite.
In our own repos, we stamp each commit with how it was verified. The stamp even includes "none" so we can query for commits that weren't verified.
We've also created a commit gate that can block a commit if the session did not contain /code-review or /verify. We've set it up with off, observe, and enforce modes.
Observe mode will log what would have been blocked, so that we can review any false positives and ensure no one is impacted.
Enforce mode will block the commit. Any commit can carry an explanation in its message as to why it didn't get verified. If commits have been blocked twice in the session, the next one will be allowed through so Claude doesn't get stuck – Claude Code isn't perfect.
The Claude Code team also regularly reviews the reviewers. We track how many merged changes were reverted, how many changes a maintainer rejected before merging, and a size-matched subset of changes that have stayed clean.
This can also be done with a formal evaluation. In our code review post, we described how the Code Review team has set up an evaluation based on previous incidents and reverts. They recreate the repo to the state at the time of review for each example, and provide a one-sentence answer key to what the bug was. Only findings that correctly describe the bug in question get points. They use merged and stable changes as a control to prevent over-flagging. Their tuning set is split out from the fixed test set they use to evaluate their system.
They also evaluated the recall rate for raw findings, post-verification, and final review separately. This lets them see if the system is losing bugs it had already found. Any evaluation that treats all these as one end-to-end score won't capture this nuance.
In addition to validity, the other dimension we care about is how important it is to make the change. A good reviewer will catch most things that are technically correct, but people care about whether a given bug is a must-fix, a should-fix, or a nit.
Code Review has this exact idea, and it will tag things as Important, Nit or Pre-existing. The "pre-existing" tag means that the reviewer shouldn't hold the author responsible for something that was already a bug.
Finally, the Claude Code team tunes recall up to the point where the next improvement would be overfitting to the eval rather than actually catching bugs.
In the Claude Code repo, we have two methods of turning missed cases into rules. The first is extracting patterns in human code review comments and PR reverts to identify common issues. These must occur in at least two PRs and be identifiable from a diff to be considered a potential rule. Three agents will try to disprove them, and they will be reviewed and approved by a human in a PR.
The second method is replaying three bug finders against a PR that is associated with a known incident. If at least two find the bug, it's a detectable bug. If the bug is missed on a small diff, this is a gap that is worth turning into a rule in REVIEW.md.
Flaky tests get the same treatment. Claude can't skip them, add sleeps or retry until they pass. If it can't find the root cause, it deletes the test and writes down why.
Rules need cleaning up too. Test suites, verification steps and .md files only grow if you let them, so we tidy them up on a routine.
We’ve found that giving your agent a way to verify its work with a strong feedback loop makes all the difference. Treat your verification layer like you would any other system. Keep it out of reach of the changes they judge, grade them against the bugs that mattered to you, and write down lessons when they miss something so that it doesn’t happen more than once, and prune those lessons so they stay worth running.
Do that and your job shifts from reading every diff to defining what “done” looks like and reviewing the evidence, which is what lets you run many Claudes at once.

====================================================================================================

How the Claude Code team thinks about verification
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
10:20:40 UTC
19:20:40 KST

How end-to-end, property and differential tests let coding agents verify their work with evidence, and what the Claude Code team does to keep its verifiers honest.
On the Claude Code team, the vast majority of our code (~90%) is written and reviewed by Claude. At this scale, how we equip our agents to verify their work with evidence becomes extremely important.
Verification loops are the building block that lets you delegate more work to Claude. Without them, you can really only run one or two Claudes at a time, because you have to check everything yourself. With them, you can run dozens or hundreds in parallel.
That’s because giving agents a way to verify their output is an effective way to improve quality. In internal testing around structured file generation, having a separate grader check Claude’s work against a rubric improved task success by up to 10 points over a standard prompting loop, with the largest gains on the hardest problems.
What was once expensive to verify (tests, benchmarks, specs, real end-to-end runs) is now cheap to request from an agent. Like code, agents can quickly generate tests, benchmarks, and specs. And once generated, these tests and benchmarks run as software so they can be run frequently without using many additional tokens.
You can also close the verification loop by using the results to improve the agent’s performance on that task. For example, if an agent repeatedly fails a test that checks logs for user data, you can create a hook or a more explicit rule in your CLAUDE.md to avoid future issues. This (mostly) stops issues from happening again.
So if verification is critical to producing quality code at a high velocity, what types of tests should you use and how should you apply them across your pipeline? This article answers those questions and also shares the process used by the Claude Code team.

Run your FreeBSD services from one console
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
08:13:38 UTC
17:13:38 KST

Open source · ZFS-native · FreeBSD 15.1 and later
Run your FreeBSD services from one console.
Install apps in isolated jails, control who can reach them, and manage updates and recovery with ZFS. One console for one host or a whole fleet through the hub, in the browser, on the command line or over the API: tour it.
FreeBSD jails, managed from one place. A jail is FreeBSD's own way to give a service a system of its own: its own files, users and network stack, on the host's kernel.
- BSD 2-Clause license
- FreeBSD 15.1 and later, amd64 and arm64
- No third-party tracking on this site
Up and running in three commands
Three commands
On a clean FreeBSD 15.1 machine, as root, once the package repository is trusted:
# pkg install jailops
# jailops-setup -n   # what it would do; changes nothing
# jailops-setup
jailops-setup loads the kernel modules, sets up the pool, turns on forwarding, adds the one anchor line to pf.conf, starts jailopsd and runs jailops doctor. The command line works as root straight away; the web console is one answer or --web away. Every action is printed before it runs, and running it again changes nothing. To run the host from the browser instead, see appliance mode.
What you get
- Backups that restore as a new jail and start with their data.
- A host firewall change you do not confirm undoes itself, so a mistake never locks you out.
- Encrypted volumes that stay shut until you unlock them, also after the machine has been off.
- Images that upgrade themselves overnight, and the jails that follow them move along.
- Every jail reachable on its own IPv6 address, straight from other hosts.
- Jails that find each other by name: db.shop.internal, or just db.
Who it is for
- Hosting providers and agencies One project per customer, and everything that is theirs stays theirs: network, storage, quotas, firewall and backups. Run hundreds of jails on one host, add a customer with a command, and give the tenant with the strictest demands a host of its own, managed the same way.
- A home lab Your whole home server in jails: apps from the catalog with one command, WireGuard back home, and encrypted volumes that stay private when the machine is off.
- Teams and companies One command line, one API and one console for every host. The fleet lives in git, the rules are the same everywhere, and backups go, encrypted, to hardware you choose.
- FreeBSD people It is jail(8), ZFS and PF underneath: files you can read, no magic to unlearn, and a root helper that never trusts the daemon.
App catalog
Pick an app. Get a running service.
From the console or with one command. JailOps gives the app its own jail, installs it from the host's packages, checks that it answers and takes a snapshot. Eight apps to start with, among them isms and a DNS server.
- Its own jailEvery app in a jail of its own, with its own network stack and its data on datasets of its own.
- Healthy, or not installedThe app's health check must pass before it counts as installed.
- A snapshot after installapp-installed: one command takes the jail back to the fresh installation.
jailops app install nginx
Appliance
Run it like an appliance
The host and its firewall, the tunnels, who may reach each app, encrypted data and the logs of every jail, from the same CLI and console as your jails.
- Host and firewall Host name, resolvers, NTP and the host's own pf.conf. A firewall change reverts by itself unless you confirm it.
Changes that undo themselves
- Encrypted volumes Native ZFS encryption and a passphrase only you know. After a boot the jail waits, locked, until you unlock it. Encrypted at rest
- All logs in one place jailops logs -f follows every jail at once. Forward the lines to syslog or a webhook, with no agent in the jails.
One stream of logs
- WireGuard One command on the server, one on the other side. The keys are made on the host and kept by the root helper. Connect networks
- App access Every app starts closed. app allow opens it to one source and, for PostgreSQL, prints the connection string once.
Closed until you say so
- Everything labelled Every interface JailOps makes says what it is in ifconfig, and the doctor checks it.
Read the labels
What changes for you
- One command per jail One command creates a jail from a ready image, with its addresses and firewall in place. A public IPv6 address for each one, on any cloud that gives you a /64. IPv6 and cloud setups
- Recovery you can verify Snapshots undo a bad change, but they live on the same pool. Off-host backups are encrypted on your host, go to a backup node that holds no key, and are checked by restoring them. Snapshot, rollback, backup
- One host or fifty The same CLI, API and console on every host. Projects keep teams apart: their names, quotas, networks and firewall policies (not the kernel, which every jail shares). Declare the fleet in git and let jailops-sync apply it.
Fleet examples
From an empty host to a running jail
A network, a project, a jail, a snapshot and a dry-run upgrade on the command line. The same jail in the console. Demo data.
$ jailops network create web \
    --ipv4 10.88.0.1/24 --nat \
    --ipv6 2001:db8:1:2:1::1/80 --uplink vtnet0
network/web created
$ jailops project create shop
$ jailops network grant web --project shop
$ jailops storage grant default --project shop
$ jailops jail create site --project shop --image freebsd-15-1 \
    --network web --storage default --memory 2G --autostart
jail/site created
$ jailops jail start site --project shop
$ jailops jail list --project shop
NAME   STATE     IMAGE          GEN               ADDRESSES                     AGE
site   running   freebsd-15-1   freebsd-15-1#g1   10.88.0.2,2001:db8:1:2:1::2   8s
$ jailops jail snapshot create site before-upgrade --project shop
$ jailops jail upgrade site --project shop --dry-run
dry-run: would upgrade jail site from freebsd-15-1#g1 to g2
From the field
Articles
Setups that work, and the details that make them work.
- Jail updates without cron Replace the nightly script that upgrades every jail: automatic jail upgrades per image, with a health check, a rollback and a record of every run, what they leave alone, and how a new FreeBSD release comes in. Read the article: Jail updates without cron
Community
Build an app. Share it.
Every app in the catalog is one short YAML file. Make a FreeBSD service work in a jail on your own host, write it down, validate it, and send one pull request: once it is merged, every JailOps host gets it with its next pkg upgrade.
Start with the tutorial: memcached, from the package to the pull request.
Built the FreeBSD way
- Open source BSD 2-Clause. Pure Go, single static binaries, an unprivileged daemon and a small root helper that does not trust it. Why JailOps
- Closed by default The API is on a local socket until you turn on the network listener or the console. That listener is TLS 1.3 only, with pinned certificates, scoped tokens or a sign-in. Exposure
- Current FreeBSD FreeBSD 15.1 and later, pkgbase first. Built by people who want to know FreeBSD to the bone. Why FreeBSD
Rather do it by hand?
That remains a fine choice. The FreeBSD base system has everything: jail.conf, zfs and pf. The recipes show the plain base-system commands first. JailOps uses the same mechanisms, writes files you can read, and can join a host you run by hand without taking over its pf.conf (route C).
Optional add-on
PkgOps: build, sign and ship FreeBSD packages
For people who build packages of their own: build and sign native FreeBSD packages from a laptop or CI, with no FreeBSD builder. You do not need it to run JailOps or to install apps from the catalog.
One CLI. One API. Every jail.
Your infrastructure. Your jail.
Three ways in: look at the console on demo data, install on a clean FreeBSD 15.1 machine, or add JailOps to a host you already run. Each ends with a service that answers.

40-domain study: 80% of Shopify stores expose /API/ucp/MCP, 0% /agents.md
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
07:04:46 UTC
16:04:46 KST

2026 AI Search & Agent Readiness Benchmark: /llms.txt, /agents.md & Silent GPTBot WAF Blocks.
Across 40 live-probed B2B SaaS, Shopify D2C, SEO agency, and multi-location service domains in November 2026, 82.5% ship homepage JSON-LD, 50.0% publish a custom /llms.txt (while another 17.5% serve unedited platform boilerplate and 7.5% return HTML soft-404s), 7.5% include homepage FAQPage schema, 5.0% publish a custom /agents.md, and 5.0% silently block GPTBot via HTTP 403 WAF rules despite an open /robots.txt.
Four protocol gaps separating cited brands from invisible domains
The 80-Point Shopify Agent Truth Gap
Eight of ten probed D2C commerce brands (80.0%) expose active Shopify /api/ucp/mcp commerce endpoints, yet 0 of 10 (0.0%) have customized /agents.md and only 1 of 10 (10.0%) serves a custom /llms.txt. Six of ten serve unedited stock Shopify Shop-skill boilerplate lacking free-shipping thresholds, return windows, and bundle exclusions.
Open /robots.txt Files Mask Silent CDN 403 Blocks
Zero of the 40 probed domains (0.0%) disallow GPTBot or PerplexityBot at root inside /robots.txt, yet 2 of 40 (5.0% — ridge.com and westerndental.com) return HTTP 403 Forbidden when probed with real GPTBot or PerplexityBot User-Agent headers at the CDN edge.
One-Third of HTTP 200 /llms.txt Responses Are Uncurated or HTML Soft-404s
Thirty domains returned HTTP 200 on GET /llms.txt, but Content-Type and body inspection revealed 3 HTML SPA soft-404 pages (7.5% of the cohort) and 7 auto-generated Shopify or Rank Math boilerplate dumps (17.5%). Developer SaaS leads genuine curation at 100% (10/10), compared with 40% of SEO agencies and 10% of D2C brands.
Base Organization Schema Is Common; Answer-First FAQPage Schema Is Rare
While 33 of 40 domains (82.5%) render at least one application/ld+json block on their homepage, only 3 of 40 (7.5%) include FAQPage structured Q&A pairs—leaving ChatGPT Search, Gemini, and Perplexity to infer pricing tiers, service areas, and SLAs from third-party directories.
How every domain was probed and classified
Each of the 40 public domains was probed directly over HTTPS using the same deterministic parser powering Verisource's production scanner: (1) GET /robots.txt parsed for GPTBot, OAI-SearchBot, PerplexityBot, ClaudeBot, and Google-Extended directives; (2) homepage requests sent with explicit GPTBot/1.2 and PerplexityBot/1.0 User-Agent headers to catch silent CDN HTTP 403 / 429 blocks; (3) GET /llms.txt and GET /agents.md inspected for text/html SPA soft-404s and stock Shopify (shop.app/SKILL.md) or SEO plugin boilerplate; (4) homepage application/ld+json script nodes parsed for FAQPage entities; and (5) JSON-RPC tools/list handshakes tested against /api/ucp/mcp and /api/mcp on D2C commerce stores.
90% of DevTools ship /llms.txt. Only 20% ship /agents.md.
In a follow-up live HTTP probe across 10 developer infrastructure and B2B SaaS domains (trigger.dev, inngest.com, turso.tech, tinybird.co, knock.app, workos.com, stytch.com, upstash.com, highlight.io, and infisical.com), 9 of 10 (90.0%) served a valid /llms.txt directory, yet only 2 of 10 (20.0% — tinybird.co and infisical.com) published a custom /agents.md pricing and limits guardrail file, and only 1 of 10 (10.0% — upstash.com) shipped all 3 JSON-LD pillars.
Common questions about the 2026 AI Search Readiness dataset
In our live HTTP readiness probe across 40 public domains spanning B2B SaaS, Shopify D2C, SEO agencies, and multi-location services, 50.0% (20/40) serve a custom-curated /llms.txt file, another 17.5% (7/40) expose auto-generated Shopify or SEO-plugin boilerplate, 7.5% (3/40) return HTML soft-404s with HTTP 200 status, and only 5.0% (2/40 — vercel.com and linear.app) publish a custom /agents.md specification.
Shopify rolled out platform-level /api/ucp/mcp endpoints and stock /agents.md files pointing to https://shop.app/SKILL.md across modern storefronts, which explains why 8 of 10 probed D2C stores respond on /api/ucp/mcp. However, stock Shopify files omit brand-specific free-shipping thresholds, return windows, and bundle exclusions unless the merchant overrides them via templates/agents.md.liquid or /pages/ai-facts.
Yes. Zero of the 40 probed domains disallowed GPTBot or PerplexityBot at root in /robots.txt, yet 5.0% (2/40 — ridge.com and westerndental.com) returned HTTP 403 Forbidden at the CDN/WAF layer when requested with GPTBot or PerplexityBot User-Agent headers. Testing both /robots.txt and simulated crawler User-Agent HTTP status codes is required.

California Sues OpenAI Over Hugging Face, Other AI Model Loss of Control
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
07:00:00 UTC
16:00:00 KST

California Sues OpenAI Over Hugging Face, Other AI Model Loss of Control (cnbc.com) 59
			
		 	
				"California's attorney general just sued OpenAI," writes longtime Slashdot reader destinyland. "The action accuses OpenAI of putting public safety at risk, reports CNBC, specifically by its past failures to contain its AI models within their testing environments." From the report:  [California Attorney General Rob Bonta] cited the "infamous" incident in July, where two OpenAI models broke out of a testing environment, gained unauthorized access to the open internet, and breached the internal systems of open-source developer platform Hugging Face. OpenAI said the models were trying to find information that they could use to cheat on an evaluation, and succeeded. The break-in at Hugging Face, which Nvidia acquired for roughly $13 billion in September, rattled industry researchers and prompted government officials to call for more stringent oversight of the AI industry.
On Monday, Bonta said that OpenAI later disclosed to his investigators that it was aware of 80 incidents "involving loss of control and access to third-party networks and websites." "But the company failed to provide the detailed information we requested to evaluate these incidents; this is concerning, and it puts the public at risk," he said.
Bonta's suit, filed in state Superior Court in San Francisco, alleges OpenAI violated California's Unfair Competition Law and accuses the company of being a public nuisance as defined by state law. The attorney general told reporters he believed in AI's technological power, and the potential of AI models to "achieve scientific breakthroughs, boost economic growth, guard against cyber attacks, and benefit all Californians." "But AI systems must be developed and designed safely," he said. "The companies that develop these models have a moral and yes, a legal responsibility to keep public safety at the forefront." The lawsuit can be found here (PDF).
